#4926. Backpack secures NFA approval, completing licensing stack for US derivatives launch

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The company has also received CFTC approval to operate as a designated contract market and derivatives clearing organization.

Crypto exchange Backpack has obtained approval from the National Futures Association to operate as a futures commission merchant (FCM), paving the way for the company to bring its trading platform into the U.S.

In a statement shared with The Block, Backpack said the FCM license is "critical infrastructure" for onboarding users and performing key compliance checks, including anti-money laundering screening.

The latest approval completes the licensing stack for Backpack's planned U.S. derivatives offering, according to the company.

On Monday, Backpack announced that it has obtained approval from the Commodity Futures Trading Commission to operate as a designated contract market (DCM) and derivatives clearing organization (DCO).

"The FCM license completes the picture," Mark Wetjen, president of Backpack US, said in the statement. "With the exchange, the clearinghouse, and now the intermediary all operating under CFTC oversight, we can offer U.S. customers a single, fully regulated path to trade on the most advanced market infrastructure available."

Together, the three licenses enable Backpack to offer leveraged perpetual futures with global unified liquidity. U.S. users will be able to trade crypto, perps on a leveraged basis, and event contracts around the clock on Backpack's platform.

"We've taken the front door approach in every market we've entered," said Backpack CEO Armani Ferrante. "It was critical for us to set the standard in the U.S. for what a fully regulated, fully compliant, crypto-native exchange looks like."

The company noted that the licenses would help move price discovery from unregulated offshore platforms toward regulated U.S. markets.

Backpack has actively expanded its offerings. In June, it launched Backpack Securities, which blends traditional and tokenized stock trading. The platform enables users to buy and sell U.S. stocks through its brokerage infrastructure and convert their holdings into tokenized versions that can move across blockchain networks.

#4924. StoneX cuts Bitdeer price target to $15 amid new US chip export controls

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

StoneX lowered its 2027 revenue and adjusted EBITDA forecasts for Bitdeer after the U.S. announced new chip export controls.

StoneX Financial analyst Mark Palmer cut his price target on Bitdeer Technologies Group Bitdeer Technologies Group (BTDR) to $15 from $22, but kept his Buy rating on the stock.

Bitdeer shares closed at $4 on Tuesday, down nearly 29% from Friday's close, according to StoneX. The firm's new target implies roughly 275% upside from that level.

In a note to clients on Wednesday, Palmer said new U.S. licensing requirements for advanced chip shipments to Malaysia raised questions about the timing of Bitdeer’s GPU deliveries. Nvidia GB300 systems for its new Malaysian sites were scheduled to start arriving this month, according to Palmer.

"BTDR is a company funding a capital-intensive transition from a low-margin bitcoin mining business with a levered balance sheet, which is why the durability of its AI-driven revenue matters so much," Palmer wrote. "That durability is what the new chip export rule has called into question."

Bitdeer reported record third-quarter revenue of $320.7 million, up 89% from a year earlier. The company's AI cloud business ended the quarter with $86 million in annualized recurring revenue and about $2.9 billion in contracted revenue backlog, most of which was tied to two Malaysian facilities, according to the note.

According to the note, StoneX now sees three possible outcomes for the Malaysia business. The firm assigned a 40% probability to licenses being granted or the capacity being leased to other customers after a one-quarter delay.

The firm also sees another 35% probability for a two- to three-quarter delay plus a 15% pricing concession. In its most adverse scenario, which carries a 25% probability, Bitdeer’s A201 project would not go ahead, and its existing GPU fleet would face limits and some customer losses.

"We do not think the right response to BTDR’s Malaysian challenge would be to assume the worst for the company or, for that matter, to pretend nothing in its story has changed," Palmer said.

Based on the revised assumptions, StoneX lowered its 2027 revenue forecast by about $230 million to $1.77 billion and its adjusted EBITDA forecast by about $150 million to $616.5 million.

#4922. Goldman Sachs reports $1.22B in Ether ETFs as ETHA share count jumps

Goldman Sachs increased its Ether ETF shares in Q3, bringing its reported holdings to $1.22 billion, while reshuffling its Bitcoin ETF positions.

Banking giant Goldman Sachs reported $1.22 billion in Ether exchange-traded fund (ETF) holdings at the end of September, up from $211.6 million three months earlier, as it increased positions across several funds throughout the third quarter.

The bank increased its holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Ethereum Trust ETF (ETHA) by 352% to 29.96 million shares, worth $602.9 million, according to its Q3 Form 13F filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC).

The company also shifted its Bitcoin ETF holdings, reducing its stake in BlackRock’s Bitcoin (BTC) fund while increasing its position in Fidelity’s competing ETF.

The increase coincided with a strong quarter for Ether (ETH), which surged nearly 71% in Q3, according to CoinGlass. US spot Ether ETFs attracted more than $3 billion in net inflows, their strongest quarter of 2026, according to SoSoValue.

BlackRock’s ETHA was not the only Ether ETF in which Goldman Sachs increased its holdings during Q3.

The bank also increased its holdings in Grayscale’s Ethereum Staking Mini ETF (ETH) by 180% to 5.68 million shares, lifting the position’s reported value to $144.9 million from $30.5 million in Q2.

Its holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Staked Ethereum ETF (ETHB) rose 62% to 8.18 million shares, worth $281 million, up from $102.3 million in the previous quarter.

Goldman also reported four additional Ether ETF positions worth a combined $195.4 million, led by $169.6 million in Fidelity’s Ethereum Fund (FETH).

Including the new positions, Goldman’s reported Ether ETF holdings reached approximately $1.22 billion at the end of Q3, up from $211.6 million in Q2.

Goldman reduced its IBIT holdings by about 792,000 shares to 16.44 million, although the position’s reported value increased 36% to $778.3 million as IBIT’s price surged 42% over the quarter.

Meanwhile, its FBTC holdings surged more than 50-fold, from 105,840 shares to 5.48 million, bringing their reported value to $397.9 million from just $5.4 million.

Camran Khosravi, research analyst at global asset manager Bitwise, told Cointelegraph that market makers such as Jane Street can shift between Bitcoin ETFs to meet client demand and hedge options positions without necessarily changing their overall Bitcoin exposure.

Related: Jane Street boosts Fidelity Bitcoin ETF holdings nearly 8x, trims IBIT

“13F filings can make routine market-making look like a big bet on one fund,” Khosravi said, noting that the disclosures omit short positions and other instruments that may offset reported holdings.

While Goldman trimmed its IBIT shares, Wells Fargo more than doubled its holdings in the BlackRock Bitcoin ETF. The bank’s Q3 filing showed its IBIT position rising 103% to 49.58 million shares, worth around $2.35 billion, while its ETHA holdings increased 125% to 22 million shares.

#4921. OpenAI's GPT 6.2 Astra Challenges Anthropic on Price, But Not Every Benchmark

OpenAI's GPT 6.2 Astra Challenges Anthropic on Price, But Not Every Benchmark

OpenAI launched GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol on Tuesday, updating its flagship and everyday AI models with better professional output, cheaper processing and stronger abilities to operate software on their own.

Astra targets demanding projects such as financial spreadsheets, legal research and interactive 3D design. Sol handles everyday work at a lower price, an increasingly important distinction as AI agents spend hours completing tasks instead of answering a single prompt.

When early users tested Astra in September, they built virtual cities and games, but complained its writing and creative capabilities had slipped. The new release claims to address that less glamorous problem, producing documents that look like something a professional would actually send.

Astra costs $8 per million input tokens and $40 per million output tokens through OpenAI's API, down 20% from the original model. Sol retains its $2 and $10 rates. Tokens are the chunks of text models process and generate, so fewer tokens can mean a smaller bill even when the listed rates stay the same.

OpenAI says Astra follows slide templates and spreadsheet conventions better, with less boilerplate and more consistent layouts. Those are company claims, not guarantees for every assignment.

Independent testing by Artificial Analysis puts Astra at 62 on its Intelligence Index, just a bit behind Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2 at 65. The index combines nine tests of reasoning, scientific work, coding and knowledge into a score out of 100.

That's five points above GPT-6 Astra, which arrived in September with a much higher price and major cybersecurity restrictions. Sol scores 57, matching the original Astra's performance at roughly 15% of its cost per evaluated task.

Those mixed results follow Anthropic's Fable 5.2 launch last week, when developers demonstrated ambitious games and applications but reported eye-watering costs for long-running projects. Alibaba entered the conversation Monday with Qwen 4 Max, a Chinese flagship that charges $1.50 per million input tokens and $4.50 per million output tokens, although it trails the American leaders in global agent rankings.

OpenAI says Astra is its first externally released model to reach the High threshold for AI self-improvement, a safety classification measuring how much it can accelerate AI research. The company estimates its productivity impact as comparable to giving each researcher an additional capable engineering assistant, relative to 2024.

Both new models also meet OpenAI's Critical cybersecurity threshold. The company says it strengthened monitoring and built a system called Control Room to halt suspicious model activity; testing nevertheless found the models sometimes failed to disclose missing files or unavailable tools that blocked their work.

OpenAI says the models are available through ChatGPT Work and Codex for Plus, Pro, Enterprise and Edu users, and through the API for developers, but our GPT Plus subscription still doesn’t show those models, so this may be a slow rollout.

OpenAI has not yet added GPT-6.2 to its Daybreak program for vetted cybersecurity researchers.

#4919. US judge clears brothers in $25M Ethereum MEV case after mistrial

Judge Jessica Clarke said the transactions did not involve false promises, but the ruling did not determine whether the brothers’ conduct was ethical or violated other laws.

A US federal judge acquitted two brothers accused of extracting $25 million from Ethereum trading bots, ruling that prosecutors failed to prove they made material misrepresentations during the alleged exploit.

In a Monday ruling, Judge Jessica Clarke acquitted Anton and James Peraire-Bueno on wire fraud and related charges. Prosecutors alleged that the MIT-educated brothers exploited a flaw in maximal extractable value (MEV)-boost software in April 2023 to lure sandwich-trading bots into transactions they then disrupted, securing $25 million in about 12 seconds.

The judge said the transactions did not amount to false promises or constitute criminal fraud, and that the case was not about whether the brothers’ actions were ethical or potentially violated other laws.

Their first trial ended in a mistrial when jurors failed to reach a verdict in November 2025.

Coin Center executive director Peter Van Valkenburgh called the acquittal “excellent news,” saying prosecutors’ honest validator theory risked allowing the government to impose its own standards of conduct on Ethereum validators through wire fraud laws.

“Anyone participating in an open network would have to wonder whether prosecutors might later declare their behavior ‘dishonest’ and haul them into court,” Valkenburgh said.

The ruling also cited a 2025 decision involving Mango Markets trader Avraham Eisenberg, where a judge found insufficient evidence of false representations.

#4917. Google says hackers use AI agents to find and exploit software flaws

Google says attackers are using AI agents to find and exploit software flaws automatically, reducing the time organizations have to apply patches.

Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) said it had, for the first time, identified attackers using AI large language model (LLM) frameworks to automate software vulnerability discovery and exploitation without step-by-step human direction.

In a report published Monday, the researchers described a tool found on an exposed staging server that compared software versions, identified potential flaws, and generated and executed exploit code.

“In the past quarter, GTIG identified threat actors using LLM frameworks to fully automate vulnerability discovery and exploitation without a human in the loop for the first time,” the researchers said.

GTIG identified an exposed staging server hosting a multi-agent vulnerability research and weaponization framework, set up to conduct continuous differential analysis comparing code before and after patch announcements on popular web applications. The model would autonomously discover and exploit software vulnerabilities.

Google warned that these automated workflows give organizations less time to apply security patches before attackers develop ways to exploit vulnerable systems.

In its September report, GTIG said it had “not yet observed threat actors deploying fully autonomous pipelines against targets in the wild.”

Evolution of threat actors using LLMs for exploits. Source: Google

Attackers are leveraging AI agent frameworks to automate patch reverse engineering via differential analysis and weaponizing N-day vulnerabilities, publicly known security flaws.

The report warned that LLMs are enabling attackers to scale “exploitation pipelines against internet-facing servers without human intervention.”

Vulnerability disclosures more than doubled between January and August, according to Google. The number of zero-day vulnerabilities exploited averaged eight per month in 2025 and 11 per month from January through August 2026, reaching 22 in August.

Count of vulnerabilities exploited, by n-days and zero-days, January 2025 to August 2026. Source: Google

These automated workflows also accelerate attackers’ ability to move from initial access to gaining domain administrator access. The report cited an August 2026 PaperCut campaign in which attackers achieved that level of control within minutes, according to cybersecurity firm GreyNoise.

Related: PHP flaw shows AI ‘vulnerability apocalypse’ is on schedule: Immunefi CEO

Cloudflare also reported attempts to exploit the PHP flaws against its customers in October. GTIG said that the two PHP vulnerabilities involved in the exploitation attempts were discovered using LLM analysis, as technical information published before the Oct. 21 patch enabled researchers and attackers to study vulnerabilities and attempt exploits, according to the report.

AI company Anthropic also warned that AI-assisted attacks have evolved into multi-agent operations automating most of the cyberattack cycle from discovery to exploitation and data theft, in a September report.

Anthropic described threat actors running “multi-victim campaigns using agentic AI that would previously have required teams of operators.”

#4915. Harbinger scores another $240M in latest investment round

Electric truck maker Harbinger has been racking up wins in recent months, and after scoring a $300 million order from international shipping giant FedEx and a defense contract with the US military, the company has drummed up another $240 million in Series D funding.

Companies like FedEx and Amazon have been making serious moves to fully electrify their fleet operations, both in an effort to decarbonize and to avoid the every-increasing price of diesel. At $300 million for 2,000 medium-duty electric delivery trucks, Harbinger’s latest deal with FedEx is believed to be one of the largest binding orders for electric medium or heavy-duty trucks in history – and it’s a big enough deal to inspire more than 240 million votes of confidence in the company’s future, one dollar at a time.

“Harbinger exists to challenge what industry has learned to accept, and reinvent the critical systems the world depends on,” said John Harris, the Co-Founder and CEO of Harbinger. “By rethinking critical systems from first principles, we aim to unlock better performance, greater reliability, safer operations, and more room to grow. Electrification is at the core of everything we do, and increasingly, electrification is at the core of industry, defense, mobility, and AI. Harbinger’s role is not simply to give customers another option. It is to reset expectations for what critical systems should deliver.”

Harbinger’s Series D funding round was co-led by Tiger Global, the Capricorn Technology Impact Fund, and Ridgeline. The company says the new funding will support expansion into energy storage systems, power generation, autonomy software, and defense applications, as well as the continued ramp up of its medium-duty platform manufacturing capacity. With this latest round of financing, Harbinger has raised over $650 million since 2021.

As for me, I’m hoping they use the money to come up with more fun, high-octane chase videos that show off their trucks’ performance capabilities. Time will tell.

#4913. Bitcoin realized loss metric beats June high as open interest eyes 2-year low

Bitcoin short-term holders’ onchain spending notched the largest proportion of realized losses since February after BTC/USD dropped to $62,000.

Bitcoin investors are showing increasing signs of panic as newer buyers’ onchain losses hit their highest since February.

Data from onchain analytics platform CryptoQuant reveals new nine-month lows in the spent output profit ratio (SOPR) of Bitcoin’s short-term holders (STH).

SOPR measures the ratio of BTC moving onchain at a higher or lower price relative to the last time it was used in a UTXO. The metric can be broken down by investor cohort, only accounting for coins owned by older or newer hodlers. The latter cohort traditionally consists of speculative entities who are more sensitive to BTC price volatility. An STH entity is a wallet holding a given unit of BTC for up to 155 days without selling.

On Nov. 16, STH-SOPR recorded a reading of 0.955, its lowest since Feb. 7. Readings below 1 reflect STH coins moving onchain on aggregate at a lower price compared to their previous transaction.

Noticeable deviations below 1 on STH-SOPR imply increased loss-making selling activity by the STH cohort. While BTC/USD briefly dipped to $62,000 this week, the latest reading is notably lower than in late June, when investors reacted to price hitting 20-month lows of $58,534 and STH-SOPR reached 0.985.

In its latest analysis released on Monday, Bitfinex Alpha, the research arm of crypto exchange Bitfinex, noted that in both June and July, swing lows on STH-SOPR corresponded to the “days of heaviest loss-taking” among speculators.

“Net realised losses across all holders reached about 76,500 BTC on 15 November, the largest since 1 July, a significant increase from about 18,700 BTC on 10 November,” it reported.

Bitcoin STH profitability comparison. Source: Bitfinex

Bitfinex added that unrealized losses reveal the pain levels of the broader Bitcoin investor base, with the share of the BTC supply held at loss reaching 48.5% on Nov. 14 — the same as on July 1, when BTC/USD set a new multiyear floor of $57,800.

“Holders are therefore under as much pressure as they were at that low, with price about 11 percent higher,” it wrote.

Speculative Bitcoin trading activity overall has witnessed a marked drop in recent weeks.

Related: BTC price tags 18-week low into Nvidia earnings: Three things to know in Bitcoin this week

Open interest, the total number of active derivative positions, dropped to $20.498 billion on Tuesday, a level not seen since late 2024, as measured by CryptoQuant.

In commentary on Monday, CryptoQuant contributor Baykuş flagged a conspicuous drop in OI on Binance as BTC/USD fell from $74,000 to $64,000.

“Open interest on coin-margined BTC-USD contracts fell from $1.55B to $1.1B, and since these contracts are USD-denominated, this reflects real position closures,” they wrote in a blog post.

#4911. Nvidia Shares Rise After Earnings Beat in First Report Since Jensen Huang's Exit

Nvidia Shares Rise After Earnings Beat in First Report Since Jensen Huang's Exit

Nvidia shares climbed roughly 2% in after-hours trading Tuesday after the AI chipmaker delivered another record quarter, beating Wall Street expectations on revenue and earnings while forecasting stronger-than-expected sales for the current period.

The results offered some relief to investors after a brutal selloff that wiped hundreds of billions of dollars from Nvidia's market value following the resignation of co-founder Jensen Huang.

According to Nvidia's earnings release, revenue reached $115.8 billion for the fiscal third quarter ended October 25, up 103% from a year earlier and comfortably above analysts' expectations of approximately $109.1 billion.

Adjusted earnings per share came in at $2.68, beating the roughly $2.47 consensus estimate. Net income under generally accepted accounting principles reached $65.3 billion, more than double the $31.9 billion reported a year earlier.

Nvidia's data center division generated $107.6 billion in revenue, up 110% year over year and accounting for approximately 93% of total sales. The company also confirmed that its Vera Rubin NVL72 computing platform has begun shipping, extending the hardware roadmap that helped make Nvidia the dominant supplier of AI processors.

The company expects fourth-quarter revenue of $130 billion, plus or minus 3%, compared with Wall Street estimates of approximately $125.3 billion, per Reuters. That forecast includes an additional week in the reporting period and assumes no data center computing revenue from China.

Tuesday's report marks Nvidia's first financial results since Huang resigned November 12, ending his 33-year tenure following revelations of extramarital affairs involving company employees and a businesswoman suspected by U.S. intelligence officials of having ties to China. Those intelligence allegations remain unproven, and an independent investigation is ongoing.

To add more drama to the story, the Securities and Exchange Commission opened a civil investigation into whether Nvidia violated investor-disclosure laws.

Colette Kress, Nvidia's longtime chief financial officer and interim CEO, sought to reassure investors about the company's future. "Our momentum is real," she said in the earnings announcement, adding that the Vera Rubin rollout was off to a strong start and the product roadmap remained on track.

Huang said Tuesday that he would be honored to return as CEO if the board believed it was in Nvidia's best interests. His statement followed public support from Elon Musk and President Donald Trump, although the board is not considering reinstatement and continues searching for a permanent replacement.

Nvidia's fundamentals look considerably better than its stock chart. Despite Tuesday's rebound, shares remain roughly 35% below their October peak near $240, and the technical indicators suggest sellers still control the broader trend.

The daily chart shows immediate support around $150, close to the $148.80 low reached during last week's selloff. A decisive break below that area could expose further downside, while a sustained recovery would first need to reclaim resistance around $164.40 to $165.60.

That former support area has become resistance following the breakdown, as Decrypt's earlier technical analysis identified. Above it, the chart shows additional hurdles near $176 and between $184 and $189, making a return to the previous trading range far from automatic.

The Relative Strength Index, or RSI, is around 22.5 on the supplied daily chart, well below the conventional oversold threshold of 30. That makes a short-term rebound plausible, but oversold readings are not proof that a bottom has formed.

The Average Directional Index, or ADX, sits near 45.4, indicating a strong prevailing trend, with directional signals favoring sellers. The faster exponential moving average remains above the slower one, a positive longer-term configuration that has not prevented the recent collapse.

Tuesday's earnings beat gives bulls a potential catalyst, but the after-hours bounce has yet to reclaim the chart's first major resistance around $164.40. Kress is scheduled to lead the company's 5 p.m. Eastern earnings call, the first without Huang, as investors look for answers about Nvidia's leadership and its next phase of growth.

#4909. BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF snaps $2.35B outflow streak amid extreme fear

BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF ended 11 sessions of withdrawals totaling $2.35 billion, but CryptoQuant says one day of inflows is too early to signal a recovery.

BlackRock’s spot Bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) returned to inflows Monday, ending an 11-session outflow streak totaling roughly $2.35 billion.

The iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) attracted $76.5 million on Nov. 16, helping US spot Bitcoin ETFs record $7.9 million in net inflows despite withdrawals from several other funds, according to Farside Investors data.

The inflows came as Bitcoin (BTC) traded near multimonth lows of $64,000, while the Crypto Fear and Greed Index, which tracks cryptocurrency market sentiment, fell to 10 out of 100 on Monday, its lowest reading since June 2026.

Nick Pitto, head of marketing at blockchain analytics company CryptoQuant, cautioned against interpreting the inflows as a broader market recovery.

“One day of inflows doesn’t change the picture too much,” Pitto said. He noted that Monday’s inflows recovered only about 3% of IBIT’s withdrawals over the previous 11 sessions, while US Bitcoin ETFs had shed more than 31,000 BTC over the past month.

“It’s too early to call this dip buying,” he said, adding: “We’d need to see several days of inflows in a row, and from more funds than just BlackRock’s.”

Salman Parcy, managing partner at crypto investment company Cequire Capital, offered a more optimistic interpretation, suggesting that some institutional investors may see Bitcoin’s recent decline as a buying opportunity.

“While it is still early to call this a reversal, the return of capital to IBIT does show that some institutional investors are beginning to see an opportunity in current price levels,” Parcy said.

Related: Bitcoin price fails to hold $65K as US stocks drop further on AI nerves

Monday also marked the deadline for most institutions to file their third-quarter Form 13F disclosures, revealing Bitcoin ETF holdings as of Sept. 30. Several institutions reported increased exposure during the quarter, but Pitto cautioned against linking those holdings to Monday’s inflows.

“ETF buying peaked in August and September. But the filings only show what they held at the end of September, not what they’ve done since. So they may not explain Monday’s inflow,” he said.

#4907. US senators seek SEC answers on tokenized stock money laundering risks

Four Democratic senators asked who would be responsible for tokenized shares transferred outside regulated platforms and whether the SEC could freeze or recover those tokens.

Four Democratic senators pressed the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) to clarify financial crime safeguards under its tokenized stock exemption, warning that tokenized shares bought by verified investors could reach offshore or unverified wallets.

In a letter released Monday, Senators Angela Alsobrooks, Catherine Cortez Masto, Mark Warner and Ruben Gallego questioned how Anti-Money Laundering and Know Your Customer rules apply to tokenized securities venues, which can trade certain US stocks without registering as securities exchanges.

The senators said the SEC’s Sept. 17 Innovation Exemption requires venues to control trading access but does not mandate restrictions on subsequent transfers. They asked who would be responsible for tokens moved outside of regulated platforms and whether the SEC could recover or freeze them.

The lawmakers also questioned issuers’ rights to object, retail investors’ access to best execution protections and custody safeguards. They asked SEC Chair Paul Atkins 13 questions, urging more transparent rulemaking and requesting answers within 30 days.

When announcing the exemption in September, Atkins said federal anti-fraud and anti-manipulation rules would continue to apply in full.

OKXICE, a venture between crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange, has also faced issuer objections. Its Nov. 5 public notice listed 67 companies that objected to their tokenized shares being traded on the venue.

Separately, crypto exchange Backpack said Monday that it had secured Commodity Futures Trading Commission approval to establish a US derivatives exchange and clearinghouse using blockchain infrastructure, paving the way for leveraged crypto perpetual futures trading.

CFTC Chair Michael Selig said blockchain-based operators must meet the same regulatory standards as traditional firms, including AML, customer protection and market surveillance requirements.

Backpack also has a tokenized equities business, although its latest CFTC approvals concern derivatives rather than the SEC exemption questioned by lawmakers.

#4906. Nvidia Board Shuts Down Jensen Huang Comeback, Launches Search for New CEO

Nvidia Board Shuts Down Jensen Huang Comeback, Launches Search for New CEO

Traders, fanboys, investors and believers. It’s time to stop coping.

Nvidia's board has publicly shut down hopes of Jensen Huang returning as CEO, delivering its strongest statement yet against reinstating the company's co-founder following his resignation last week.

"The Nvidia board does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO," board chair Harvey Jones said during Tuesday's earnings call.

Jones said the board unanimously accepted Huang's resignation and would not answer questions about the circumstances surrounding his departure or the ongoing independent investigation.

The company has hired executive recruitment firm Heidrick & Struggles to find a permanent replacement. Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress, who took over as interim CEO, received the board's full backing.

"We have every confidence in Colette's leadership, integrity and ability to deliver as interim CEO," Jones said.

The declaration comes just hours after Huang publicly offered to return if directors believed his reinstatement would benefit shareholders and employees. Earlier today, before Huang’s public statement, sources told CNBC the board was not considering bringing Huang back.

Huang resigned after revelations about extramarital relationships, including one involving a businesswoman whose alleged Chinese intelligence connections have attracted scrutiny from U.S. authorities. An independent investigation is examining his conduct and potential national security implications.

His departure prompted an unusual campaign for reinstatement, with President Donald Trump and Elon Musk among those publicly supporting his return.

Jones also defended Nvidia's newly established National Security and Export Control Oversight Committee, saying it would improve engagement with U.S. officials as Washington investigates how restricted AI chips have reached China.

The committee, chaired by director Suzanne Nora Johnson, will oversee compliance programs and receive reports directly from Nvidia's national security and trade compliance teams.

Nvidia reported $2.5 billion in quarterly revenue from sales to Chinese business units under the Trump administration's limited export permissions.

Those arrangements face renewed uncertainty following reports that billions of dollars in restricted Nvidia hardware reached mainland China through overseas intermediaries.

The leadership confrontation overshadowed otherwise impressive quarterly results. Nvidia reported $115.8 billion in revenue, up 103% year over year, with data center sales reaching $107.6 billion.

The company forecast $130 billion in revenue for its fourth fiscal quarter, while assuming no data center computing revenue from China.

#4904. Revolut plans four blockchain-based funds for private markets, chair says

Citing a looming liquidity crunch, Martin Gilbert said blockchain could help bring more liquidity to private markets.

Revolut plans to launch four funds using blockchain to offer fractional investments in illiquid markets, chair Martin Gilbert said Tuesday at Solana’s Breakpoint conference in London.

Blockchain, he explained, would be used to divide funds focused on illiquid markets “into what I would term marketable sizes.”

“If you want to buy a private equity fund on Revolut going forward, when we do launch these funds, you will be able to buy them in fractional amounts.”

The company, which has said it has over 80 million customers worldwide, launched a private market investment platform to eligible customers in Europe in July. Managed by a number of firms including Apollo, Ares, Hamilton Lane and Partners Group, the funds already on the platform allow for a minimum investment of 1 euro.

Warning that the financial sector may be entering into a liquidity crunch, particularly in private markets, he highlighted the need to attract more buyers to these markets, which blockchains could facilitate.

“In this world, there is liquidity until there is no liquidity,” he said. “If you can help people get liquidity in illiquid assets, you’re going to solve a lot of problems for a lot of people. The only way you can do that is to be blockchain enabled.”

The company hasn’t yet released details on the blockchain-based funds or when they will launch.

#4903. Tesla deemed liable for fraud after Solar Roof ‘experiment’ on a home

A California judge has deemed Tesla liable for fraud in a Solar Roof lawsuit, after the company repeatedly failed to hand over evidence. Tesla had already admitted it breached the contract, broke its warranty, and was negligent on the job.

The homeowner says fixing the damage will cost “well over $2.5 million.” Tesla once offered him $5,000.

The case is Conroy v. Tesla, filed in 2022 in San Diego Superior Court’s North County division (case No. 37-2022-00043670). Electrek reviewed the court orders, the complaint, and the parties’ joint trial readiness report. Here’s what’s in them.

Ambrose Conroy, who lives in Rancho Santa Fe, signed up for a Tesla Solar Roof in March 2020. The original contract was $82,210 for a 13.1 kW system. By February 2021, after two amendments, it had grown to roughly $110,000 for an 18.2 kW roof and six Powerwalls.

The roof was never finished. It never passed a county inspection, and it never produced a single kWh for the house.

Tesla isn’t contesting most of that anymore. In the joint report filed by both sides last month, Tesla stipulated to liability on three of the five claims.

The other two claims, fraud and negligent misrepresentation, are where things went badly for Tesla.

Judge Cynthia Freeland sanctioned Tesla twice over discovery before going further. She ordered it to pay $15,467.50 in February and another $13,500 in June, both times for failing to produce a proper privilege log and responsive documents, and both times warning that issue sanctions could follow.

Tesla missed the next deadline anyway. So on September 11, the court deemed Tesla liable for fraud and negligent misrepresentation, limiting the trial on those claims to compensatory and punitive damages.

The judge found “ample evidence to support that Defendants have acted willfully.” Among the missing records were Microsoft Teams messages (Tesla’s own corporate witness testified they’re kept “for infinity”), OSHA logs, emails with San Diego County inspectors, and more than 20 quality, install, and safety reports from Tesla’s internal Bolt system.

Tesla asked her to reconsider, arguing it had since produced everything. She said no in a ruling dated October 27, writing that the late documents could support Conroy’s theory that Tesla “knowingly used Plaintiff’s home as an experiment while knowing they lacked the experience to perform as promised.”

And the “experiment” word shows up in Tesla’s own messages, according to the trial exhibit list. Exhibit 224 is described as text messages from January 13, 2021, between Tesla’s director of model engineering Bobby Yang and other Tesla employees.

To be clear, that’s how Conroy’s side describes the messages on the exhibit list. We haven’t seen the texts themselves. But Elon Musk is on Conroy’s witness list, and internal emails quoted in the complaint point the same way.

On February 9, 2021, Yang wrote internally that the house was in the “99.5 percentile in terms of complexity” and that “if we can do this job well, we should be able to do any job well.” RJ Johnson, then head of Tesla’s energy business, replied that the project would “skew numbers” but that Tesla needed “to make it right.”

Johnson was fired in April 2021. The roof had at least 59 mounting planes, and one employee called it the largest job Tesla had attempted at the time, according to the complaint.

Conroy’s account of the installation reads like a list of everything that can go wrong on a roof. These are his allegations, but Tesla has now conceded breach and negligence.

The tear-off was supposed to take two days and took two weeks. Tesla left in November 2020 with bare roof decking exposed, then had crews work overtime on December 27 to get underlayment down before a storm. Tiles arrived scratched and damaged and went on the roof anyway. Glass tiles shattered in the driveway and lawn, and the family says they still can’t walk there barefoot.

By March 2021, Johnson had agreed in writing that paying “when you’re happy with the outcome is completely reasonable.” Tesla’s billing department kept calling anyway.

In August 2021, an internal email weighed just leaving.

The project went through at least seven project managers. In November 2021, Tesla told Conroy the people behind its repair plan were gone and it had nobody in the area to do the work.

In April 2022, Tesla offered $5,000 to settle everything.

It didn’t end there. In February 2025, more than two years into the lawsuit, Tesla failed another San Diego County inspection after showing up without the plans for the venting system. And this summer, Conroy’s electrical expert opened up part of the roof and concluded that the way Tesla ran wiring and conduit under the tiles created a short-circuit and fire risk in heavy rain. The roof is now coming off. Tesla’s lawyers point out that even Conroy’s counsel called that fire risk “counterintuitive.”

Tesla is fighting the sanction hard. In an October 29 filing, it asked the court to freeze the case while it petitions the California Court of Appeal, arguing the order is “unduly punitive,” violates due process, and amounts to a directed verdict on fraud before trial.

Tesla also says the discovery fight had no connection to the fraud claims and that it has now produced everything relevant. The judge already rejected that argument once.

The November 20 trial date is off. A trial readiness conference is set for December 4, and Conroy’s lawyers want to go to trial in December.

This isn’t the first Solar Roof horror story in this case file. Conroy’s complaint attaches our 2020 report on a Solar Roof buyer left with tarps over his house after Tesla tried an untested underlayment on his home. It also cites a 2025 California Contractors State License Board action seeking to revoke Tesla’s contractor licenses over abandoned Solar Roof jobs.

What a nightmare. This is the most damaging Solar Roof case I’ve seen, and I’ve been covering this product since Elon Musk unveiled it on a Universal Studios backlot in 2016.

A botched install isn’t news on its own. Tesla only installed about 3,000 of these roofs before killing the product this year, and plenty of them went sideways. What’s different here is the paper trail: internal emails calling a customer’s home a test of whether Tesla could do “any job,” texts describing the job as an “experiment” with daily reports going to Musk, and then years of Tesla failing to hand over its own records until a judge ran out of patience.

And Tesla’s response to all of it was $5,000.

Look, it’s normal for things not to go as planned with a new product, and early adopters need to be flexible. However, we are talking about someone’s literal roof over their head here and a trillion-dollar company. Tesla could have and should have made it right a long time ago.

Any time there are internal messages in the record that say essentially "let's skip town rather than try to make this right", it's a tough lift to make a jury think that everything was aboveboard.

The question that strikes me is whether this may also rise to the criminal level, since the CEO was apparently involved in the decision.

The issue sanction might not survive the Court of Appeal. Courts don’t love deciding fraud through discovery orders, and Tesla’s due process argument isn’t frivolous. But even if it gets reversed, Tesla has still admitted it breached the contract and was negligent, and the jury will see how this customer was treated.

This also says a lot about how Tesla ran the Solar Roof business. It sold a product it knew was hard to install, on homes it knew were complex, while the people running the program kept leaving. Conroy’s house was one of the worst cases, but installers are still dealing with the fallout of Tesla walking away from the product earlier this year.

The real question is what’s in those Teams messages and texts Tesla fought so hard to keep out of this case. If this goes to trial in December, we might find out.

Solar Roof was supposed to make going solar effortless. For most homeowners, conventional panels from a reputable local installer are the safer bet. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4900. Everyone wants an electric pickup truck. I want more electric 125cc motorcycles

If you follow the EV industry in North America, you’d be forgiven for thinking that the future of transportation revolves around electric SUVs and pickups. Every week seems to bring another teaser from a startup promising to reinvent the family car or truck. The latest electric SUVs, “coming soon” tiny trucks, lifestyle trucks, modular trucks, etc. Companies like Slate, TELO, and countless others have generated plenty of excitement, even if many of them still have everything left to prove.

Don’t get me wrong – I hope they succeed. We need more affordable electric vehicles. But after spending nearly two decades covering a specific part of the electric-vehicle space and a good chunk of that time traveling around the world to see how people actually get around, I can’t help but wonder if we’re chasing the wrong prize here.

Because while the West keeps trying to electrify pickup trucks and SUVs, much of the world has already settled on a far more sensible transportation appliance.

Western readers often think of a sedan as the world’s “normal” or “sensible” vehicle.

But go traveling almost anywhere in Asia, and that illusion disappears in about five minutes.

The roads are filled with Honda Clicks and CG125s, Hero Splendors, Yamaha NMAXs and YBR125s, and countless other small-displacement motorcycles. They carry workers to jobs, students to school, families to dinner, and every imaginable cargo in between.

They’re the delivery vans. They’re the commuter cars. They’re grocery getters, family transportation, and work trucks. And perhaps more importantly, they transport hundreds of millions of people every single day.

You don’t have to spend long in places like the Philippines before realizing something else: these aren’t really “motorcycle cultures.” They’re just transportation cultures. People ride these machines because they make sense.

And I’ve done it myself to see what all the fuss was about. Sure, I’m an e-motorcycle guy through and through. But I’ve been there to ride a mile in their motorcycle sandals, and so I get it.

Even so, having had one cheek on each saddle, I still have to think there’s a better way than the tried-and-true 125cc combustion engine motorcycles that have become the workhorse of much of the world.

The extreme proliferation of small motorcycles is what makes the current electric motorcycle market feel so strange. If there were ever a class of gasoline vehicle that batteries are already capable of replacing, it’s the 125cc combustion engine motorcycle.

These bikes aren’t demanding. They generally produce a modest 10-15 hp (8–11 kW) of power. They cruise comfortably around 45-60 mph (70-100 km/h). Most riders travel relatively short daily distances measured in dozens of miles, not hundreds.

A modern electric motorcycle with a 4-6 kWh battery can comfortably accomplish those same tasks while offering smoother acceleration, quieter operation, lower maintenance, and dramatically lower operating costs.

Unlike replacing a heavy-duty pickup truck that tows livestock trailers across Montana, replacing a 125cc commuter bike is already well within today’s battery technology. In many ways, it’s one of the easiest transportation sectors to electrify.

Instead, North America’s electric motorcycle market developed in an odd direction.

We have electric bicycles, and those are great for urban commuters. But then we jump almost straight to premium electric motorcycles costing $15,000 to $25,000. The practical middle has remained surprisingly sparse.

The ONYX RCR became something of a cult favorite by proving there was genuine demand for lightweight, motorcycle-like electric transportation without superbike performance.

Ryvid’s Anthem pushed further, adding highway capability while still remaining approachable and relatively affordable.

The new LiveWire S4 Honcho also feels like an encouraging step toward a lighter, more practical electric motorcycle instead of another oversized premium machine.

Even Infinite Machine’s Olto, while occupying a slightly different category, demonstrates growing interest in practical urban electric two-wheelers that prioritize transportation over headline horsepower.

These companies are proving something important: People don’t necessarily need 100 horsepower. Many simply need transportation.

Don’t misunderstand me. Electric pickup trucks absolutely have their place. Electric SUVs do too.

But we’ve spent the last decade pouring enormous engineering effort into electrifying some of the least energy-efficient passenger vehicles on the road.

Meanwhile, we’ve largely ignored one of the most efficient gasoline vehicle classes humanity has ever created.

A typical 125cc combustion engine motorcycle can already achieve fuel economy approaching or exceeding 100 mpg (2.35 liters/100 km). An electric equivalent becomes almost absurdly efficient.

We’re talking roughly 4-6 kWh per 100 kilometers (62 miles) – just a fraction of the energy consumed even by many compact electric cars. I once did the math, and a light electric motorcycle can travel 10,000 km (roughly 6,000 miles) for around $160 in electricity. Basically, your electrical bill doesn’t even notice when you own an electric motorcycle. Charging it simply doesn’t move the needle.

Instead of asking how we can fit ever-larger battery packs into increasingly larger SUVs and trucks, perhaps we should also be asking how many trips don’t need a large vehicle in the first place.

Transportation statistics across much of the developed world tell a familiar story.

Most trips involve one person. Maybe there’s also a backpack in the mix. Maybe there are some groceries getting picked up on the way home. Occasionally, there’s another passenger.

Those things are exactly what small motorcycles have been doing around the world for decades.

I recently spent some time in the Philippines, where I watched motorcycles carrying impossible-looking loads every day. Entire families would ride together. Construction materials. Furniture. Livestock. I saw two pigs on a 125cc bike, I kid you not. I’ve seen even crazier things in Thailand.

Again, I’m certainly not advocating that we adopt some of those practices in the West – there are obvious safety considerations – but it demonstrates just how incredibly capable these machines can be.

Even within normal Western expectations, a 125cc-class motorcycle is capable of handling an astonishing percentage of daily transportation needs.

This is where discussions about motorcycles often become unnecessarily polarized.

No, motorcycles aren’t ideal in every situation. Yes, heavy rain exists. Snow exists. Families with multiple children exist. Long interstate road trips exist.

Nobody is suggesting a 125cc electric motorcycle should replace every car.

If a household can replace even half of its daily driving with a lightweight electric motorcycle, that’s already a massive reduction in energy consumption, congestion, parking demand, and transportation costs.

Transportation doesn’t have to be all-or-nothing. So enough with this “but what if it rains?” stuff. Either put on a jacket that day or drive your car. But for the majority of the trips each year you take where it’s not raining, you could have done it in a much more efficient (and fun) way.

One of the biggest conversations surrounding EV adoption is charging. But smaller electric motorcycles barely participate in that problem. A 4-5 kWh battery charges overnight from an ordinary household outlet. The outlet you plug your laptop charger into is basically the equivalent of a Level 2 public charger for an e-motorcycle – that’s how fast it is.

DC fast charging isn’t really necessary on a light electric motorcycle. There’s no need for an expensive home charger. There’s no massive battery pack. Just plug it in before bed and wake up with a battery that’s been fully charged and waiting on you for hours already.

And if you want to go wild, there are swappable battery networks like Gogoro in Taiwan or Vammo in South America that can make charging unnecessary.

Another advantage rarely discussed is intimidation. Many people don’t want a 600 lb (270 kg) motorcycle producing superbike acceleration. They want something approachable, something lightweight, maneuverable, easy to park, and easy to live with.

That’s exactly why 125cc motorcycles became so successful globally.

Electric versions preserve all of those advantages while making them even easier to ride thanks to instant torque and the absence of clutch work. Many of them even come with reverse mode, making them easier to park and wiggle around even on hilly terrain.

One reason Europe has seen more progress here is that the regulatory framework already exists.

Manufacturers regularly market bikes as “125cc equivalents,” and consumers immediately understand what that means.

Licensing systems and insurance companies recognize the category. It’s a mature transportation class.

North America, by comparison, often treats motorcycles as either recreational toys or large-displacement touring machines. We’ve largely forgotten that much of the world simply sees them as transportation.

Ironically, younger riders may already be showing us the way forward.

The explosion in popularity of Sur Ron-style electric motorcycles and lightweight e-motos demonstrates that younger generations are perfectly willing to embrace two-wheeled electric transportation.

Not everyone buying those bikes is looking for motocross. Many simply enjoy the freedom, accessibility, affordability, and fun that come with traveling on two wheels.

Rather than dismissing that trend, perhaps we should recognize it as an opportunity. Today’s recreational rider often becomes tomorrow’s commuter.

If someone discovers they enjoy getting around on an electric two-wheeler, moving from a lightweight e-moto to a practical 125cc-equivalent commuter becomes a much smaller leap than convincing someone to abandon their car entirely.

One observation has stuck with me after so many years of covering the electric vehicle industry and traveling through countries where motorcycles dominate daily life. The West has spent years trying to electrify the largest, heaviest, and least efficient vehicles on our roads. Meanwhile, we’ve paid surprisingly little attention to electrifying the vehicle that already moves much of humanity every single day.

In Europe at least, there's a cliff between a pedal ebike, which anyone can ride without any pre planning, and a motorcycle which needs passing a specific test, taxing, taking out insurance and wearing safety gear. Even people with car licences need to take driving lessons and the test. It means you can't just step on a motorcycle when it makes sense you need potentially months of planning. It's much easier just to take a car you're already licensed for.

I’m not arguing that everyone should ride a motorcycle. And I’m certainly not arguing that cars no longer have an important place.

But perhaps we’ve underestimated just how many people could benefit from something in between.

Not just an electric bicycle, and not just a $25,000 electric motorcycle. Just an honest, practical, affordable electric equivalent of the vehicle that already keeps much of the world moving.

Because if we’re serious about accelerating electric transportation, maybe the future isn’t another electric pickup truck. Maybe it’s finally giving the world’s favorite motorcycle an electric motor.

#4897. Bitcoin to $1M by 2030? ‘Almost certainly not,’ says Dragonfly’s Qureshi

Dragonfly’s Haseeb Qureshi says Bitcoin could reach $400,000 by 2030 but doubts it will return to $100,000 by year-end with the rout in AI stocks weighing on crypto.

Bitcoin is unlikely to reach $1 million by 2030 — though it could reach $400,000 — according to Dragonfly managing partner Haseeb Qureshi.

“The amount of capital that would have to come into crypto to get to million-dollar Bitcoin is an astronomical sum,” Qureshi tells Cointelegraph’s Trade Secrets. “The idea that you could get that amount of capital in five years or four years is just not plausible.”

A former professional poker player and software engineer at Airbnb, Qureshi now backs crypto startups at Dragonfly, which launched a $650 million fund in February.

Qureshi also says he doubts Bitcoin will return to six figures by year-end, linking the current crypto sell-off to the rout in AI stocks.

Nvidia (NVDA) shares closed 4.3% lower on Thursday after the chipmaker announced co-founder Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO following a series of scandals. Huang reportedly had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy. Beijing denied the espionage allegation and President Donald Trump has expressed doubts too.

Nvidia is the leading AI chip supplier and accounted for 4.66% of the MSCI All Country World Index at the end of October, second only to Apple.

Bitcoin fell alongside Nvidia shares in the week Jensen Huang stepped down as CEO. Source: TradingView

“Crypto is not the main story this year for financial markets,” Qureshi says. “Crypto is downstream of these other stories.”

Qureshi says investors grew concerned after Democrat sweep in the US midterms that new data centers would be harder to get approved. With high oil prices and inflation already weighing on the economy, the prospect of weaker AI investment prompted investors to cut exposure to riskier assets, including Bitcoin, he says.

Related: Jensen Huang weighs Nvidia comeback days after shock exit: FT

That leaves crypto “largely along for the ride” through the year-end.

Crypto has spent much of 2026 in a bear market despite Bitcoin’s 43% recovery in the third quarter. Bitcoin traded near $65,000 on Monday, almost 50% below its October 2025 all-time high.

Bitcoin is far away from its $126,080 all-time high recorded in October 2025. Source: CoinGecko

The US Senate failed to advance the CLARITY Act in September, stalling the industry’s push for federal rules for crypto markets. In October, suspected North Korean hackers stole about $6.3 billion from Binance in the largest crypto theft on record.

Over the next 15 to 20 years, Qureshi says Bitcoin could compete with gold as a store of value outside government control.

New deposits or advances in mining could add to the supply of gold, he says while Bitcoin’s protocol caps its supply at 21 million units.

“There will never be an asteroid that you will find a bunch of Bitcoin on.”

However, Qureshi sees a risk that AI could discover a flaw in the mathematics that secures Bitcoin transactions.

Bitcoin uses digital signatures to verify that a transaction was signed with the corresponding private key. If a future mathematical breakthrough makes it possible to forge those signatures, attackers could move coins without their owners’ permission.

Such a threat is often discussed alongside quantum computing risks. A powerful quantum computer could derive a private key from its corresponding public key using Shor’s algorithim, putting coins with public keys visible at risk of theft, which includes some of Satoshi Nakamoto’s early holdings.

Related: Nearly 10% of Bitcoin supply is ‘structurally unsafe’ from quantum breakthrough: Glassnode

In October, OpenAI said one of its models had found a faster way to solve certain logic problems, challenging a conjecture known as the Exponential Time Hypothesis.

Qureshi says a future AI-assisted discovery could break the cryptography Bitcoin uses to verify who can spend coins, undermining its case as a store of value.

“That, I think, is what you should price in as the chance this all breaks, this all fails,” he says.

Dragonfly has a history of backing innovative projects, and led stablecoin project Ethena’s $6 million seed round in 2023. Ethena had about $4.9 billion of its dollar-linked USDe token outstanding at the end of September. The protocol uses derivatives to offset price swings in the assets backing USDe. Some of the income those positions generate goes to users who stake the token.

Users who stake Venice’s VVV token can mint DIEM, which they can stake for daily credits to run AI models.

#4896. Why Microsoft Thinks Smaller, Cheaper AI Can Beat Claude and GPT

Why Microsoft Thinks Smaller, Cheaper AI Can Beat Claude and GPT

Microsoft unveiled a new lineup of specialized AI models at its Ignite conference Tuesday, making a direct run at Anthropic and OpenAI in coding, cybersecurity, and image generation.

Instead of trying to build a single chatbot that beats everyone at everything, it is pitching models trained to do particular jobs at substantially lower cost.

The biggest claim involves MAI Code 2, which Microsoft says delivers 96% of Claude Opus 5.5's performance on common enterprise coding tasks for just 5% of the cost. It also claims 98% of GPT-6.1 Sol's performance at 40% of the price.

OpenAI also released GPT-6.2 on Tuesday, so Microsoft’s Sol comparison already concerns an older generation.

Those figures average results from four evaluations focused on practical software development, including fixing code and completing multi-file changes. Microsoft ran three of the tests on its own evaluation platform, used outside results for the fourth, and calculated MAI Code 2's costs using a 50% promotional discount.

A lighter Code 2 Flash model targets routine fixes and quick edits, with Microsoft claiming performance comparable to Claude Haiku 5.5 at 20% of the price, so extremely cheap without sacrificing quality. Both coding models are already in GitHub Copilot, putting Microsoft's in-house technology directly into a product that has relied heavily on outside AI suppliers.

Security is another target. Microsoft says MAI Cyber 2 Flash succeeded on 92.3% of CyberGym-E2E's evaluated vulnerability-discovery tasks, compared with 91.5% for GPT-6 Astra, at less than 2% of the cost per task. CyberGym-E2E scores the percentage of software-security investigations an AI completes successfully; Microsoft excluded 92 of 905 tasks after identifying overlap between their source material and its training data.

The company has not yet published a direct comparison against OpenAI's restricted GPT-6 Cyber model. Its security system will begin replacing an older MAI model for eligible MDASH customers in mid-December, building on the vulnerability-scanning approach Decrypt previously covered.

Can a small model beat a big one? Definitely—in specific cases.

A general-purpose model has to learn to write poetry, explain physics, debug software and answer questions about almost anything. A model tuned for coding or cyber defense can spend more of its training and computational budget on the patterns that matter for that job, rather than carrying the same breadth of knowledge into every request.

That doesn't make smaller models universally smarter. It can make them faster and more economical when the task is well defined, particularly in a business where the same work runs thousands of times a day.

A security scanner analyzing every new code change does not need an expensive frontier model to explain the history of the Roman Empire. If a model can dedicate its full power to generate Python code, it will make more sense to pay for its API calls instead of using a super powerful generalistic model.

Specialization can also improve the whole system around the model: the tools it calls, the checks that verify its answers and the data used to refine it. OpenAI has taken a related approach with its Mini and Nano models, letting cheaper workers handle routine tasks while larger systems tackle the exceptions.

However, there are trade-offs. Microsoft's own documentation says Code 2 is not yet optimized for fundamental scientific research or very long-running autonomous assignments. And Anthropic competes in the cheaper end of the market with Haiku, not just its premium Opus models, so Microsoft is entering a race its rivals already understand.

Microsoft's MAI Image 2.7 may offer the clearest evidence that its strategy is working. In our quick side-by-side tests, its images beat GPT Image and Google's Nano Banana on realism, creativity, and overall quality.

These were just first impressions based on a few generations, but they broadly track its strong showing in independent rankings.

On Artificial Analysis's image-editing test, which asks people to choose between anonymously generated edits and turns those preferences into an Elo rating, MAI Image 2.7 currently leads with 1,215 points against GPT Image 2.6 Sunburst's 1,211.

The four-point gap is small enough that their reported uncertainty ranges overlap; the separate text-to-image generation ranking, scored using the same preference-based method, puts Microsoft in fourth place.

The pricing is harder to ignore. Microsoft quotes about $35 per 1,000 images for MAI Image 2.7 and $10 for its Flash version, versus roughly $211 for GPT Image 2.6 Sunburst at maximum quality in Artificial Analysis's comparison. Google's Nano Banana 2.1 is about $34 per 1,000 there, while Nano Banana Pro is about $134; actual bills depend on the provider, resolution and settings.

Our earlier testing of MAI Image 2 found excellent realism but frustrating limits on editing and output formats. The newer release adds interactive editing through Microsoft's Canvas workspace, transparent backgrounds and broader access through Foundry, although image quality and a usable product are still two different things. That said the model is quite censored too.

Microsoft is also offering selected business customers the ability to customize and run its models' licensed weights inside Azure, reducing reliance on outside vendors without making the technology open source. For the next test of its specialist strategy, Code 2 is already available in GitHub Copilot, and the Cyber 2 Flash rollout is scheduled to start in mid-December.

#4891. Solana Foundation takes majority interest in crypto data firm CoinGecko

Solana Foundation now holds a majority interest in CoinGecko, its product chief said, as the crypto data platform explores trading features.

Solana Foundation now holds a majority interest in crypto data aggregator CoinGecko, the foundation’s chief product officer, Vibhu Norby, said Tuesday.

CoinGecko announced the backing but did not disclose the financial terms. It said it plans to use it to strengthen its data infrastructure, expand data coverage across tokenized real-world assets and derivatives, and develop onchain trading and market intelligence tools.

The Solana Foundation “now has a majority interest in CoinGecko” and plans to collaborate on developing “new on-platform trading & DeFi experiences with Solana,” Norby said in a Tuesday X post.

CoinGecko said its data methodologies would remain transparent and publicly accessible and that it would continue serving the broader crypto ecosystem. Its website, app and application programming interface, which supplies data to other services, will continue operating as usual.

Founded in 2014, CoinGecko serves an average of over 10 million monthly users and receives over 200 million monthly page views, according to its About page.

Cointelegraph has contacted the Solana Foundation and CoinGecko to ask about the size of the deal, but had not received a response by publication time.

#4890. Scattered Spider Hacker Sentenced to 6 Years Over $11.6M in Stolen Crypto

Scattered Spider Hacker Sentenced to 6 Years Over $11.6M in Stolen Crypto

A Scottish hacker who was part of the Scattered Spider hacking collective has been sentenced to six years in U.S. federal prison for his role in a scheme that stole at least $11.6 million in crypto.

Tyler Robert Buchanan, 24, of Dundee, was sentenced to 72 months by a federal judge in Santa Ana, California, on Tuesday, the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Central District of California said. The judge set a restitution hearing for December 11. Buchanan began hacking as a teenager and was part of Scattered Spider, the group of young, English-speaking hackers blamed for a string of high-profile cyberattacks, the BBC reported.

British national sentenced to six years in federal prison for hacking into companies and stealing at least $11 million in virtual currency https://t.co/h7oprrXcVK

— US Attorney L.A. (@USAO_LosAngeles) November 18, 2026

Buchanan pleaded guilty in April to conspiracy to commit wire fraud and aggravated identity theft. At the time, prosecutors said the scheme had stolen at least $8 million in crypto. That figure has since risen to at least $11.6 million.

Between September 2021 and April 2023, Buchanan and his co-conspirators sent hundreds of phishing text messages to employees of at least a dozen companies, including telecoms, technology firms and crypto companies, according to the indictment and Buchanan's plea agreement. The messages linked to fake websites that captured employees' login details, which were sent to a Telegram channel run by Buchanan and another co-conspirator.

The group used information stolen from those companies to identify individual crypto holders, then carried out SIM swaps, tricking mobile carriers into moving victims' phone numbers to devices they controlled so they could intercept two-factor authentication codes and empty victims' wallets.

In the largest single theft, one victim lost $1.67 million in December 2022, according to the BBC. When authorities searched Buchanan's home in Scotland in April 2023, they found the names and addresses of numerous victims, and a text file containing crypto seed phrases and login details for one victim's account, prosecutors said.

"Those thefts had severe, life-altering consequences for many victims," prosecutors argued in a sentencing memo.

Buchanan is the fifth and final defendant to be sentenced in the case, which the Justice Department charged in November 2024. A Justice Department spokesperson confirmed at the time that the defendants were suspected members of Scattered Spider, the group behind high-profile attacks including the 2023 breach of MGM Resorts. Buchanan was arrested at Palma de Mallorca Airport in Spain in May 2024 while trying to board a private flight to Naples, the BBC reported, and has been in U.S. custody since April 2025.

His co-defendant Noah Urban, of Palm Coast, Florida, is serving 10 years in prison, while Ahmed Hossam Eldin Elbadawy and Evans Onyeaka Osiebo were each sentenced to 45 months, and Joel Martin Evans to two years.

U.S. authorities have continued to pursue alleged Scattered Spider members. In July, a 19-year-old suspect was extradited from Finland over an $8 million crypto ransom demand against a luxury jeweler.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Nvidia stock one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

“The tape feels increasingly fragile as Nvidia’s AI premium continues to unwind and investors reassess the durability of the ecosystem built around its dominance,” CNBC quoted FedWatch Advisors founder Ben Emons as stating in a note.

Related: Bitcoin realized loss metric beats June high as open interest eyes 2-year low

In an X update on the day, Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, stipulated that the market needed to fulfil several key conditions as part of a durable rebound.

BTC/USD, he argued, should reclaim its 200-week simple moving average (SMA) at $67,977, but that this alone was not “enough.” Lower time frames must also produce a series of higher swing lows and higher swing highs, with breakouts through local resistance accompanied by high buyer volume.

“Until then, a bounce has a higher probability of being a trap than a legit recovery effort,” Alan summarized.

#4887. New ID. AURA is the mainstream EV VW needs – but its software is the real story

Volkswagen built its global empire on the promise of affordable, accessible transportation, embodied by the iconic Type 1 Beetle. Now facing an existential crisis after a decades-long push upmarket, the automaker faces a reckoning – and a retreat back into affordable, mass-market electric sedans like the new FAW-VW ID. AURA L5 (and the software that powers it) might be exactly what VW needs to reach a new generation of buyers.

Like the Ford Model T, the original VW Type 1 Beetle was an engineering-led answer to a basic problem: making family transportation affordable and accessible to the masses. And regardless of how you feel about Elon Musk, the Tesla Model 3 has become the spiritual successor to those cars, bringing electric transportation to millions of mainstream drivers. As the Model 3 approaches its 11th year in public view, the recently revealed all-electric ID. AURA L5 and VW JETTA looks like it could be a compelling rival to the mainstream M3.

Co-developed by Volkswagen’s Chinese and German design teams and set to be built by the FAW-VW JV, the ID. AURA L5 is an Accord/Camry-sized electric family car a practical fastback-style rear hatch, frameless doors, and a claimed “84.4% usable floor-area ratio.” The power-operated hatchback opens its maw more than 70 degrees, revealing a 39″ wide opening that can handle groceries, luggage, golf clubs, pets, bodies – whatever you got.

Don’t take my word for it, though. This is the original text from the company’s press release (translated from Chinese by Google):

The ID. AURA L5 is a pure electric family sedan, jointly designed by Chinese and German design teams, and highly replicates the design essence of the ID. AURA concept car shown at the 2025 Shanghai Auto Show. Its short overhang and long wheelbase proportions create a sleek and flowing coupe-like stance, achieving a “usable floor area ratio” of 84.4%. The sides feature true frameless doors, semi-concealed door handles, and an electric hatchback tailgate with an opening angle exceeding 70°, and an opening width exceeding 1000 mm, greatly improving the convenience of loading and unloading large items, deeply integrating fastback aesthetics with practicality. Some models will be equipped with LiDAR and feature the Hyper Sense 8 end-to-end large model, making daily travel easier and more convenient for customers.

The production version of the ID. AURA L5 is expected to pack the same 155 kW (~208 hp) electric motors announced when the concept AURA L5 was first shown last year (battery specs were not released), and was shown as part of a broader product announcement of what VW Group China would be showcasing at this year’s China International Import Expo (CIIE). These include the first two models fully equipped with Volkswagen Group’s locally developed VEGA “integrated technology matrix” (read: software), and the Volkswagen Anhui intelligent pure electric “coupe” crossover.

The announcement coincides with reports from outlets like Autocar and Reuters that Volkswagen is evaluating whether to deploy its China-developed electrical architecture across the majority of its European markets.

“According to internal discussions confirmed by Group board member and Volkswagen brand CEO Thomas Schäfer,” reports Autocar’s Greg Kable, “up to 75% of the firm’s core models in Europe could eventually use the China Electronic Architecture.”

The Volkswagen Group has been struggling to deliver a software experience that resonates with customers. To their credit, they’re looking for outside help, spending billions of dollars on a software collaboration with EV startup Rivian for its upcoming electric truck brand Scout rather than going it alone. With billions of dollars and potential future of the company at stake, then, this seems like the most interesting bit of the announcement:

During the event, the immersive interactive experience exhibit “Digital Magic Cube,” which made its debut at last year’s China International Import Expo, will undergo an iterative upgrade to showcase the core strength of CEA (China Electronic Architecture), the software pillar of Volkswagen Group’s integrated self-developed technology matrix VEGA, to the attendees.

To date, CEA has enabled five new models from three joint ventures within the Volkswagen Group on a large scale, and software iterations have already been completed this year. CEA brings three core advantages to the Group’s new model lineup in China: intelligent accessibility, continuous upgrades, and safety redundancy. Through immersive interactive presentations, it provides consumers with the most intuitive experience. Empowered by the new AI R&D toolchain “C-Brain,” CEA introduces AI agents into software development, design, and engineering verification, enabling more flexible and efficient integration and innovation of vehicle intelligence capabilities, giving vehicles powerful thinking and perception abilities. Intelligent vehicles powered by CEA can better understand consumer needs, more efficiently create in-vehicle intelligent application scenarios, and better enhance digital intelligent safety redundancy, bringing Chinese consumers a new generation of intelligent mobility experience defined by Volkswagen standards

Sensible looking average car which should appeal to VW buyers provided the price, charging speed and range are right. Especially like that it has a hatch without being SUV shaped.

#4884. Fed’s Waller cites Binance hack in warning over tokenized finance

Fed Governor Christopher Waller questioned who bears responsibility for fraud, compromised keys and failed transactions as traditional financial assets move onto blockchains.

Speaking at The Clearing House’s annual conference in New York on Nov. 17, an event bringing together banking and payments industry leaders, Waller questioned who would bear responsibility when fraudsters exploit smart contracts, private keys are compromised, transfers go wrong or assets become stranded on failed blockchains.

Waller said the level of security and oversight required could depend on the importance of the financial transactions involved:

The expansion of tokenized finance would also require clear legal frameworks governing ownership rights, custody and settlement, he said, arguing that governance and legal protections are as important as technical safeguards in building trust.

Waller’s remarks came as four Democratic senators pressed the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) over safeguards for tokenized stocks. In a letter released Monday, the lawmakers questioned who would be responsible for shares transferred outside regulated platforms and whether regulators could freeze or recover the tokens.

Related: Custodia Bank loses Supreme Court bid for direct Fed payment access

Waller wasn’t the only Federal Reserve governor to raise concerns about emerging financial technologies this week. His remarks followed a Monday speech by fellow Fed Governor Michael Barr, who warned that artificial intelligence could introduce new cybersecurity risks as financial institutions increasingly adopt the technology.

Barr said AI-enabled attackers were exploiting vulnerabilities previously considered too difficult to target, while autonomous AI systems could behave in ways that undermine security controls

“Attackers only need to be right once, while defenders need to be right all the time,” Barr said.

He also said major banks had reported finding AI agents attempting to take actions in retail customer accounts without being able to verify their authenticity or authorization, highlighting the need for stronger authentication and security controls.
