#4852. Electric dirt bikes keep getting called e-bikes. The bicycle industry wants that to stop

The line between electric bicycles and electric motorcycles has been getting increasingly blurry – though perhaps not because the vehicles themselves are actually that hard to tell apart.

Instead, much of the confusion comes down to terminology. High-powered electric dirt bikes capable of reaching motorcycle-like speeds are increasingly being referred to as “e-bikes,” despite sharing relatively little with the low-speed electric bicycles millions of Americans use for commuting and recreation.

Now the U.S. bicycle industry is asking federal regulators to make that distinction much clearer.

PeopleForBikes, the bicycle industry’s leading trade association, recently testified before the U.S. Consumer Product Safety Commission (CPSC), calling for stronger federal regulations that specifically distinguish electric bicycles from higher-powered e-motos and electric dirt bikes.

“E-bikes and e-motos are fundamentally different products, and federal safety regulations need to reflect that distinction,” explained PeopleForBikes Policy and General Counsel Matt Moore. “When high-powered electric dirt bikes are marketed as e-bikes, it creates confusion for consumers and puts the credibility of legitimate e-bikes at risk.”

There’s a real nomenclature problem hiding underneath all of this. The term “e-bike” is widely used as shorthand for “electric bicycle,” but it isn’t exclusively used that way. Motorcycles have been called “bikes” for generations, and motorcycle media and riders will sometimes casually refer to electric motorcycles as “e-bikes” too, just in the way a biker refers to his Harley as his “bike”. In fact, it all comes from the same root. We don’t call motorcyclists “motorists”, do we? No… we call them “bikers”.

All of that makes phrases like “legal e-bike” and “legitimate e-bike” that were even used by PeopleForBikes in its arguments somewhat problematic when discussing proposed regulation. The term “e-bike” simply doesn’t mean anything specific, at least not legally.

What actually matters from a legal standpoint is whether a vehicle meets the codified definition of an electric bicycle, not whether someone calls it an e-bike – especially when the term e-bike is practically applied so broadly. In other words, perhaps part of separating electric bicycles from e-motos is being more precise with the terminology ourselves: “electric bicycle” has a specific regulatory meaning, while “e-bike” has increasingly become a much fuzzier colloquial term. As much as some would want the term “e-bike” to mean something with pedals, it simply doesn’t – at least not in North America.

I’m certainly guilty of this myself in my writing and videos, falling back on the commonly used phrasing instead of the technical legal terminology, but keeping them separated could help with the growing misunderstanding in the industry.

That distinction has become increasingly important as lightweight electric motorcycles and dirt bikes have exploded in popularity. Many feature bicycle-like frames and components, and some even include pedals, but their performance can extend far beyond what federal law defines as a low-speed electric bicycle.

The concern goes beyond mere semantics. When a 50-60 mph (80-100 km/h) electric dirt bike gets lumped into the same category as a 20-28 mph (32-45 km/h) commuter e-bike, crashes and reckless riding involving the former can quickly become part of the public conversation surrounding the latter. That can ultimately influence how lawmakers, regulators, police departments, and the general public view e-bikes as a whole.

In fact, many of the supposed electric bicycle crash figures we’ve seen touted in the news aren’t from electric bicycles at all, but rather from e-moto accidents that have been lumped in with electric bicycles, making the latter appear more dangerous than they truly are.

If street-legal electric bicycles were separated from e-motos in crash statistics, the category would be revealed to be much safer than conventional reporting would have us believe.

All of this and more has led to PeopleForBikes pushing federal regulators to draw a clearer line.

As part of its CPSC testimony, the organization recommended retaining the existing federal definition of a low-speed electric bicycle while creating a separate definition for e-motos, including machines capable of speeds up to 60 mph (96 km/h). It also called for mandatory electrical safety standards for off-road e-motos, which it says currently fall into a regulatory gap, as well as greater scrutiny of conversion kits capable of turning conventional bicycles into vehicles operating beyond their original structural design limits.

well, if its not an electric bicycle, it should be treated as a moped, registration and insurance at least, possibly a VIN and a title. So it must carry a plate if it rides on the street.

Personally I think even the level-3 electric bicycle standard (28mph with pedal assist) is to permissive, even gas mopeds have pedals, and few exceed 30mph.

But I'll lose that argument most of the time.

Now, suggestion, in the USA: a new category for "light motorcycle".

HP limit at about 15, weight limit at about 250lb(or less), speed limit at about 60, not allowed on freeways and interstates. Gas or electric does not matter. License, registration, title, insurance, they all depend on the state mostly. Helmet required, if rider is younger?

A great entry point for younger riders, much of Europe does it like this.

The testimony came as the CPSC considers new mandatory federal battery safety standards for e-bikes and other electric mobility devices. PeopleForBikes says it supports stronger battery standards, while recommending that regulators rely on existing UL standards without modification.

PeopleForBikes is also asking the CPSC and National Highway Traffic Safety Administration to coordinate enforcement against e-motos that evade federal requirements.

None of this means e-motos are inherently bad. They’re incredibly fun machines, and there’s a rapidly growing market for them. But a 60 mph (96 km/h) electric dirt bike isn’t an electric bicycle simply because it looks vaguely like one.

And increasingly, the bicycle industry wants regulators to make sure everyone understands the difference.

#4850. Jensen Huang’s Houses: Exploring the Former Nvidia CEO’s $38 Million San Francisco Mansion and Other Properties

It’s been a big week for Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang. The billionaire announced he is stepping down as CEO of the AI hardware giant following reports that he had at least two extramarital affairs: one with a suspected Chinese spy, and another with one of his company’s senior executives (other reports allege a third affair with another Nvidia employee). Huang will reportedly testify before Congress later this month to discuss his relationship with the alleged spy. We can only assume that the Taipei City native is weathering the storm at home, but where are Jensen Huang’s houses? Even Huang himself may be wondering about the answer to that question, if a recent New York Times interview is any indication (more on that later).

Unsurprisingly, the tech titan hasn’t strayed too far from Silicon Valley, where Nvidia has been based since it was founded in the mid-’80s, with his real estate purchases. After graduating from Oregon State University, where he met his wife, Lori Huang, Huang began buying properties in California’s Bay Area, and he and Lori still own two of them. So far, the superbillionaire’s only exception to that rule has been an estate on Maui in Hawaii. Time will tell if the scandal spurs any real estate changes for him.

One thing that will presumably change about Huang’s home life in the immediate future: footing the bill for his security team. Nvidia has, until now, covered the cost of its former CEO’s private security. The company spent about $4 million in 2026 on “home security, protection during personal travel, consulting, and drivers” for Huang, per the New York Post. Though that arrangement is likely coming to an end, that shouldn’t be too big a problem for Jensen; the scandal has put Nvidia’s stock—and therefore Jensen’s net worth—in a slight flux, but he is still worth an estimated $144.7 billion at the time of publication.

Read on for a rundown of Jensen Huang’s houses dating back to his arrival in Silicon Valley.

According to The New York Times, Huang married Lori shortly after his 1984 graduation from Oregon State University, where they met as lab partners in an electrical engineering class. The newlywed graduates hightailed it to Silicon Valley to start their careers as microchip designers, and Jensen bought his first house: a 1,500-square-foot three-bedroom home in San Jose, California. It’s unclear exactly when or for how much he bought the property, but Jensen sold it in 1988 for $185,000.

That same year, Huang and Lori moved into a larger San Jose home that they bought for $338,000. With 2,300 square feet of interior living space, it had four bedrooms. Huang lived here when he co-founded Nvidia in 1993; he sold the house in 2002 for $500,000, the Wall Street Journal reports.

From there, the Huangs moved into an even more upgraded residence: a 7,000-square-foot home in Los Altos Hills, a tony area about 15 miles from the Nvidia headquarters. They paid $6.9 million for the newly built six-bedroom dwelling in 2003. According to Realtor.com, the two-story house hosts a resort-worthy swimming pool. The Huangs still own this house.

A year after buying his house in Los Altos Hills, Huang paid $7.5 million for a Hawaiian estate. The oceanfront property is located within Wailea Resort, an exclusive gated community on the south side of Maui, per the Wall Street Journal. The contemporary-style mansion reportedly has seven bedrooms across about 8,000 square feet. The Huangs still maintain this residence too.

In 2017, Huang made his most recent real estate purchase: a newly completed $38 million mansion in the high-end Pacific Heights neighborhood on San Francisco’s Gold Coast. The residence is modern in style, with a curved roofline and a limestone exterior. It boasts 11,400 square feet of interior living space, with seven bedrooms, seven full bathrooms, three half-baths, a wine cellar, an outdoor kitchen, an elevator, a home theater, a gym with a sauna, a library, numerous fireplaces, and a fifth-floor observation deck with views of the Golden Gate Bridge. The house took four years to construct, its developer told the WSJ.

San Francisco’s famous Golden Gate Bridge.

This seems to be Huang’s primary residence—although, according to a September 2026 New York Times interview, he could not tell you where it is. “There are a lot of skills that don’t matter,” Huang told the outlet in response to a question about AI weakening students’ basic math skills. “My first confession, I actually don’t know my address.… One day I had to pump gas—it was a few years ago. They needed my ZIP code, and I panicked. I didn’t know my ZIP code. I don’t know my telephone number. I forget these things. I can live with it.”

#4847. Bitwise seeks Texas Stock Exchange listing for Zcash ETF

Coinbase Custody would safeguard the fund’s ZEC holdings, while Bitwise Investment Advisers would serve as its manager and sponsor.

Bitwise Asset Management is seeking to list a spot Zcash exchange-traded fund (ETF) on the Texas Stock Exchange (TXSE), according to a registration statement filed with the US Securities and Exchange Commission.

The S-1 filing names TXSE as the anticipated listing venue for the Bitwise Zcash ETF, which would trade under the ticker ZEC. The fund would hold Zcash directly and seek to reflect the value of its holdings, minus operating expenses and liabilities.

Coinbase Custody would safeguard the fund’s ZEC tokens, while Bitwise Investment Advisers would serve as its manager and sponsor.

The Bitwise fund has not begun trading, and its registration statement has yet to become effective. Its proposed annual sponsor fee also remains unspecified. The public filing also follows an Oct. 16 draft that identified NYSE Arca as the proposed exchange.

The filing comes as asset managers continue to expand their Zcash-linked investment products in the US.

Grayscale launched its Zcash ETF on NYSE Arca in August, becoming one of the first issuers to offer investors exposure to the cryptocurrency through a US-listed ETF. Meanwhile, Winklevoss Asset Services filed in October to launch a competing spot Zcash ETF on Nasdaq under the ticker WINK.

#4845. Tokenized Nvidia market cap surges 159% amid Jensen Huang’s exit

Nvidia stock tokens reached $401.9 million in combined value as Jensen Huang stepped down and the chipmaker posted record quarterly revenue.

The combined market value of tokenized stocks tied to Nvidia shares rose about 159% from Nov. 8 to Tuesday, while the chipmaker’s Nasdaq-listed shares fell nearly 17% from their last close before co-founder Jensen Huang publicly admitted to an extramarital affair.

The value of Nvidia (NVDA) tokens tracked by Token Terminal rose from $155 million on Nov. 8 to $401.9 million by Tuesday.

Nvidia shed about $750 billion in market value, falling from $4.5 trillion at the Nov. 6 close to $3.75 trillion at Tuesday’s close, according to Yahoo Finance data.

Huang acknowledged the affair in a message to Nvidia employees on Nov. 8, nearly two weeks after The New York Times reported that US intelligence officials suspected Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang of spying for Beijing. China denied the allegations.

Related: ARK sells $85M in Nvidia shares ahead of chipmaker’s Q3 earnings results

Nvidia supplies chips that cloud providers such as Google Cloud and Microsoft Azure use to train and run AI models, making its earnings a gauge of global spending on AI infrastructure.

Tokenized stocks are blockchain tokens designed to track the price of a share. In one common setup, an issuer creates tokens backed by shares held by a custodian.

Binance, for example, lists an Nvidia token that can trade when Nasdaq is closed. It gives investors exposure to Nvidia’s share price without shareholder rights. The market value can rise as more tokens enter circulation, even if Nvidia’s share price falls.

Huang resigned as president and CEO of Nvidia and stepped down from the company’s board Thursday. Nvidia named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.

On Tuesday, Nvidia’s board said it “does not intend to reinstate” Huang, shortly after the chipmaker reported record third-quarter revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from a year earlier and above Wall Street estimates. Data center sales more than doubled to $107.6 billion, accounting for about 93% of total revenue. Nvidia forecast fourth-quarter sales of about $130 billion.

Nvidia’s shares rose 2.82% to $159.80 in Wednesday premarket trading as of 12:50 pm UTC (07:50 am ET), according to Yahoo Finance.

#4844. This 377-Square-Foot Island Getaway in Denmark Is a Picture-Perfect Pint-Sized Retreat

A mini vacation home in Zealand, Denmark, is a particularly appealing retreat, no matter the season. With its rugged coastlines, forests, and small villages, the western side of Zealand offers an ideal setting for a break from everyday life. Nestled among trees is a tiny house that was designed to be as unobtrusive as possible. Its compact shape, natural color palette, and large windows allow it to blend almost seamlessly into the forest. Here, nature isn’t just right outside the door—it’s always present inside as well.

Surrounded by trees, this small wooden house in Denmark blends seamlessly with nature.

Named the EcoNeo House, the home was designed by the Danish architectural firm SAGA Space Architects, both as a family home and as a prototype for tiny house-style vacation homes. Although originally conceived as a summer house, the cottage has been insulated and heated, allowing it to be used even during colder weather. At only 377 square feet, it combines the essential features of a vacation home, sleeping up to six people. The design prioritizes a thoughtful use of the available space and forward-thinking architecture that blends into the landscape.

The spacious terrace is perfect for sunbathing and dining outdoors, especially in the summer.

Inside, the living and dining areas flow seamlessly into one another. Thanks to the large windows that offer views of the surrounding trees, there’s a connection between indoors and outdoors. The kitchen is set slightly apart, but opens up into the dining area, blending smoothly into the floor plan. Designed with functionality in mind, it’s still fully equipped with all the necessary appliances.

The dining area flows seamlessly into the kitchen. Additional storage space was created below the stairs on the left, which lead to the sleeping lofts.

Instead of breaking up the floor plan with additional walls, the architects made use of the home’s height: Stairs next to the kitchen lead to two sleeping lofts that offer a private retreat for up to four people. Storage was also a priority in the design. Items not needed on a daily basis can be stowed under the stairs, a clever solution that again makes the most of the small space.

Large windows let in plenty of natural light and create a connection between indoors and outdoors. Despite its compact size, the kitchen has all the necessary appliances.

There’s even a sauna, which can be converted into a sleeping space for two additional guests if needed. The generous window front offers a view of the surrounding landscape, while a cold plunge pool on the terrace is waiting for a refreshing dip afterward.

Through the sauna’s large window, you can enjoy the surrounding nature even while relaxing. It’s an appealing retreat, especially on chilly days. There’s also a cold plunge pool on the terrace.

The choice of materials complements the understated architecture. The floor’s wide Douglas fir planks introduce a warm color and vibrant grain. The use of wood is consistently carried through to other areas of the house, creating a visual connection between the living space and the sauna. Natural materials are also used in the bathroom, including Kolmården marble, a Swedish stone distinguished by its characteristic green hue.

Wood defines the interior and lends the rooms a natural warmth. Large windows bring the surrounding nature into the home, while the dining area is spacious even within the compact layout.

Together with the stones and the greenery outside, the result is a design that deliberately avoids contrasts and excess. “When designing the summer house, every detail was intended to reflect the harmony between sustainable innovation and timeless craftsmanship,” explains Niklas Munk-Andersen, architect and partner at SAGA Space Architects.

Beanbag chairs provide extra seating and blend harmoniously into the interior with their colors.

The Douglas fir floorboards also give the clean design a natural, tactile quality. The material is more than a surface design choice, instead it shapes the way that the rooms feel.

The bathroom features natural materials and Swedish marble finishes.

The sauna can also be converted into a sleeping area for two additional guests.

The compact design also has a positive impact on the home’s carbon footprint. According to calculations by SAGA Space Architects, the EcoNeo House produces approximately 386 pounds of CO₂ annually, whereas an average vacation home for six people is estimated to emit about 2,282 pounds. This means emissions are approximately 83% lower. In addition to the small living area, bio-based materials are the primary factor contributing to this difference.

The boundary between indoors and outdoors blurs, bringing nature into focus.

The architects also designed the construction process to be as efficient as possible by digitally planning the house, precisely prefabricating its individual components, and then assembling them on-site in a modular fashion. This approach not only simplified assembly but also minimized the impact on the site.

Thanks to its small footprint, this vacation home’s emissions are 83% lower than those of the average vacation home that can accommodate six people. In addition, the modular construction minimizes the impact on the natural environment.

The EcoNeo House demonstrates that sustainable construction isn’t just a matter of the materials used. The size of a building and how the available space is utilized also play a crucial role. Though it may sound small, 377 square feet can be perfectly sufficient for a getaway home—provided the floor plan, storage space, and amenities are all carefully designed.

Although primarily intended as a summer home, this small vacation home can also be enjoyed in colder months.

A skylight lets light into the upper sleeping lofts.

The small vacation home has two separate sleeping lofts accommodating four people.

#4841. How to preserve wealth and access funds during a national emergency

As geopolitical and technological threats multiply, the big question is whether you can hold on to your wealth and access funds when the system fails.

Amid kinetic war, cyberattacks and the prospect of the power grid failing, governments around the world have been encouraging citizens to prepare for major disruption.

In the UK, citizens have been stocking up on tinned food and bottled water in response to the government’s Be Ready campaign.

With fighter jets streaking across the skies of the Middle East, talk of a dirty bomb attack on Washington and the world’s largest software company going offline for a day, the possibility of disaster striking close to home is starting to look more realistic.

But what do you do with your money in times like these? How can you protect your wealth, but still be able to access it when the system fails?

There are two key aspects: choosing assets that maintain value even as the world goes up in flames, and being able to access funds during an emergency.

On the first point Maximilian Kunkel, chief investment officer for ultra-high-net-worth clients at UBS Global Wealth Management, tells Magazine, “We would caution against thinking of any single asset as ‘safe’ in all circumstances.”

Instead, he says wealthy investors should focus on building a portfolio that is “resilient across different scenarios,” with enough liquidity and diversification across asset classes, currencies and geographies.

The corporation’s 2026 global family office report, representing families with an average net worth of $2.7 billion, found 64% cited major geopolitical conflict as a concern over the next 12 months. These investors are not flocking to crypto for wealth preservation however.

Hadley Stern, head of institutions at Silence Laboratories and founder of Fidelity Digital Assets, explains that financial institutions may now place Bitcoin in the same category as gold, but it does not yet enjoy the same reputation as a safe haven.

“Gold has thousands of years of history [...] Bitcoin has less than two decades. But the reasoning people use is the same: a scarce asset that isn’t anyone else’s liability,” he says.

Related: Will gold’s ‘infinite mint bug’ make Bitcoin more valuable?

He advises against using it as a reliable hedge in a crisis; “I’d be careful with the hedge story,” he says. “Bitcoin is still volatile, and in a sell-off it often trades like other risk assets.”

Cash, by contrast, is handy for emergencies “for liquidity and paying bills,” but not for “holding value over long stretches,” he says.

Michael D’Alessio, chief operating officer at digital asset trading platform Talos, tells Magazine, there are early signs that Bitcoin is beginning to behave more like gold. He said that through the volatility around the US midterm elections Bitcoin’s correlation with equities remained high. However, over the same period, its correlation with gold also rose sharply, to around 0.75 on a 30-day basis as of early August:

BTC vs gold, 30-day rolling correlation of daily returns. Source: Talos/Coin Metrics

The collapse of Mt Gox, or more recently the failure of Paraguay’s Ueno Bank show that “the asset is only as safe as the system holding it,” according to Stern.

Rather than automatically moving into Bitcoin or gold when tensions rise, risk committees are asking different questions:

“Where is the asset? Which jurisdiction is it in? Who can freeze it? What happens if a counterparty or a payment rail is down for a week?” he says. “Those used to be back-office questions. Now they come up at the board level.”

Kunkel said that concern over major geopolitical conflict is encouraging wealthy families to consider “not only asset allocation, but also where assets are held, currency exposure, liquidity and jurisdictional diversification.”

The UBS report found that 88% of family offices hold bankable assets in two or more jurisdictions, while 30% are increasing or considering increasing currency diversification.

Related: Half of UK wealth advisers say clients’ crypto is ‘invisible’ to them: CoinShares survey

Fortunately the banks and custody providers are making practical disaster preparations too.

“Regulators expect firms to plan for severe but plausible events,” Stern explains, such as a cloud outage, a cyberattack, a key vendor failing or a market closed for days, and “show they can keep their most important services running through them.”

The UK government is advising citizens to stock up on supplies. Source: Gov.uk

For custody providers, that means testing whether assets can still be moved if a signing location goes offline, and how quickly operations can recover if a data center is lost. Stern says while households might prep for an emergency by stocking a full pantry:

Similarly investors need to work out which assets you can reach quickly and which you can’t, and “keeping enough liquidity to get through a bad stretch without being forced to sell.”

Kunkel says capital preservation “does not mean eliminating volatility, but maintaining enough liquidity and diversification to withstand volatility” without becoming a forced seller.

When the balloon goes up, diversification can protect a portfolio from a market shock, but it can’t guarantee access to funds if the infrastructure supporting it fails. A safe haven isn’t just about owning the right things; it’s about being able to access them when the system stops working.

#4840. Musk says Cybertruck isn’t dying from weak sales — the numbers disagree

Elon Musk now claims Cybertruck orders “far exceed” Tesla’s production rate, and that the truck isn’t being killed because it’s unprofitable. It’s his second explanation in three days for ending the pickup.

The problem is that Tesla’s own numbers say the opposite. The Cybertruck line has been running at roughly a quarter of its installed capacity, and Tesla spent all of 2026 discounting, re-trimming, and repricing the truck to find buyers.

“The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate. We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people. This is not a case of Cybertruck being unprofitable, as has been misreported by legacy media, but rather that the opportunity cost of the complexity of Cybertruck is vastly outweighed by the value of incremental Cybercab production.”

The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate. We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people.This is not a case of Cybertruck being unprofitable, as has been misreported… https://t.co/rGmNKV3ILs

He was quote-tweeting a Tesla fan telling people to “get it while you can.” So yes, this is also a sales pitch.

On Friday, Musk announced Tesla would wind down Cybertruck production at Giga Texas around mid-2027 “to make room for increased Cybercab production.” That was the first story. Now it’s about “complexity” and “opportunity cost.” Let’s go through it.

Tesla controls its production rate. That’s the flaw in this claim.

Tesla built the Cybertruck supply chain for 250,000 units a year, and its latest shareholder letter still lists more than 125,000 units of installed capacity. In Q3 2026, it delivered 8,295 “Other Models.” With Model S and Model X out of production, we estimated 7,000 to 8,000 of those were Cybertrucks. That’s a 30,000-a-year pace, down 48% year-over-year in that category.

If orders had been outrunning production at any point this year, Tesla had about 95,000 units of idle annual capacity sitting right there. It didn’t use it.

And companies with a backlog don’t behave like Tesla did. In February, it offered a dual-motor Cybertruck at $59,990 for “only the next 10 days.” It pulled a single-motor RWD version after just 173 sales. It leaned on Musk’s own companies too: SpaceX bought 1,279 Cybertrucks in Q4 2025, about 19% of US registrations that quarter once you add his other firms.

Cox Automotive estimates US Cybertruck sales fell from 14,416 in Q3 2024 to 3,519 in Q1 2026 and 3,744 in Q2. It’s a demand problem everyone could see.

So if orders spiked this weekend, there’s an obvious reason: Musk told the world on Friday that the truck is going away. A last-call rush on a discontinued vehicle is not proof that it was selling.

Tesla has been sitting on Cybertruck deposits it was never going to fill for years.

Musk touted more than a million reservations, and crowdsourced tallies put the list near 2 million by 2023. Its best US quarter, by Cox Automotive’s estimate, was 14,416 trucks. The reservation book was a vanity number, not an order book.

Two weeks ago, Tesla cancelled the Cybertruck for Australia and New Zealand and refunded deposits people had paid in 2019. Returning deposits is something Tesla was already doing because it couldn’t sell the truck in those markets, not because it was swamped.

This one is a straw man. Our reporting, and most of the coverage Friday, was about sales, not margins. The truck sells at about 12% of the volume Tesla planned for.

Tesla doesn’t disclose Cybertruck margins, so Musk can say whatever he wants here and nobody can check it. But he’s the one who said in 2023 that Tesla had “dug our own grave with Cybertruck” because of how hard it was to build profitably.

And look at what Tesla did the same day it announced the end of production: it raised Cybertruck prices by up to 10% and paused orders for the cheapest trim. The cheapest orderable Cybertruck went from $69,990 in early August to $92,490. You do that to squeeze margin out of the last trucks, not because the line was printing money.

Follow Musk’s logic to the end. If the Cybertruck were profitable and orders really outran production, killing it would mean walking away from guaranteed profit for a robotaxi that had 463 units registered in Texas as of late October and no proven business yet.

The only way that trade makes sense is if the Cybertruck’s profit at 30,000 units a year is small. Which is another way of saying it doesn’t sell enough.

Giga Texas also has more than 10 million square feet of floor space, and the Cybercab runs on its own “unboxed” line. Tesla can’t make use of the Cybertruck line and floor space was never the constraint. To further adapt his logic for shutting down Cybertruck production, Musk shifted his argument from “space” to Cybercab’s line needing some “smart people”, who he implied work on the Cybertruck.

Musk wrote on Saturday that “there are a very limited number of smart, dedicated people”, but Michael So, who led Cybercab new product introduction and was Giga Texas employee #1, left in April for nuclear startup Aalo Atomics. Last month, Tesla lost its Cybercab general assembly lead to Rivian’s R2 program just weeks after the launch. If talent is the bottleneck, it’s the Cybercab team that’s bleeding it.

Musk doesn’t want the Cybertruck to go down in history as a flop, and we get it. It’s his truck. He approved the controversial design, hyped it for four years before it shipped, and boasted that 1 million reservations could translate to 500,000 units a year.

But you can’t run a line at a quarter of capacity, run “10 days only” discounts, pull trims after 173 sales, and sell trucks to your own rocket company, then turn around and tell people demand was never the issue. The data is public. Tesla’s own delivery reports are public.

Our read on what happened is simple. Demand collapsed in 2025 and never came back. Tesla tried every pricing trick it had. When nothing worked, it found a future-facing reason to end it, and now it’s spinning the inevitable end-of-life rush as proof the truck was a hit all along.

Musk loves a big TAM. SpaceX’s IPO filing put its total addressable market at $28.5 trillion, and last month he went further, writing that he sees “a path to SpaceX being worth orders of magnitude more than the current Earth economy.”

So apply the same lens to Tesla. Pickups are one of the biggest and most profitable vehicle markets on the planet. Ford sold 801,525 F-Series trucks in the US last year, GM sold 925,656 Silverados and Sierras combined, and Ram moved 374,059. That’s more than 2.1 million trucks from three automakers, easily north of $100 billion a year at today’s transaction prices.

Its funny that such a "free speech absolutist" won't disclose something as simple as auto only OpMargins...proving my point that Tesla post-Musk buyout simply existed to make products that were eligible for EV and green tax credits.

Robotaxis? Waymo, by far the leader, is doing about 500,000 paid rides a week, and analysts estimated its revenue run rate at roughly $355 million earlier this year. Tesla doesn’t even break out robotaxi revenue and we expect it to be a fraction of Waymo’s.

Maybe that flips one day. But that’s the bet here: Tesla is walking away from a giant market it can sell into today for one that only gets big if autonomy is solved in the next couple of years. Musk has made that exact bet before. He promised a million robotaxis on the road by 2020 and has said unsupervised self-driving was a year away almost every year since 2016. He’s lost every time.

And that rush is real, for what it’s worth. Some people want the last of a weird, genuinely impressive truck. That doesn’t make it a business.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4838. 007 Off Duty: 10 Photos of Former James Bond Actors at Home

For a secret agent, James Bond generates a remarkable amount of public discussion. Barely a day goes by without fresh speculation about who will be the next actor to take on the role of 007. Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor are among the names linked to the role, with recent reports suggesting the search has reached final screen tests.

Whoever takes over will join the small group who have played the part since Sean Connery’s debut in Dr. No in 1962. Away from the screen, the homes of these former James Bond actors reveal more about the men in the dinner jackets. These archival photographs find them settling into family life and indulging interests that occasionally followed them onto set. Below, find a little domestic distraction while we wait for the next Bond to introduce himself.

Sean Connery relaxing in his ground-floor basement flat in NW5.

Photographed at home in England in 1955, Connery is still around seven years away from his first appearance as James Bond in this shapshot. His surroundings are modest, with little suggestion of the polished interiors his screen alter ego was about to frequent. Connery established the role of Bond across six films in during the 1960s and early 1970s.

Landing the role of James Bond required a little wardrobe planning on George Lazenby’s part. According to the official Bond website, the former model bought an unused suit made for Connery, then visited his predecessor’s barber at the Dorchester to request the same haircut. Producer Cubby Broccoli happened to be in the neighboring chair.

George Lazenby pictured at his home in Kensington, London, 1973.

His only appearance as 007 was in the 1969 film On Her Majesty's Secret Service. It gave Bond one of his most personal storylines, with Diana Rigg playing his ill-fated bride, Tracy. By the time of the Kensington photograph shown above in February 1973, Lazenby had already left the role. Seated with papers in hand, he is surrounded by books, records and a guitar propped beside the sofa. The glass-topped coffee table is crowded with objects, including a decorative horse’s head, while a substantial wooden cabinet provides storage behind him. Later that year, Roger Moore made his Bond debut in Live and Let Die.

Roger Moore reading the dictionary at his home in Los Angeles, circa 1955.

Roger Moore had yet to order his first on-screen Bond martini when he was photographed at home reading a dictionary, while working as a television actor.

Roger Moore kissing his wife, Welsh singer Dorothy Squires, outside their home in Bexley Heath, London, in 1957.

He married Welsh singer Dorothy Squires in 1953, and they lived primarily at The Mount—a mansion on Wansunt Road in Old Bexley, Kent.

The couple divorced in 1968, and a year later, Moore married Luisa Mattioli. Sherwood House in Denham, located close to Pinewood Studios, served as the couple's base during Moore's first three Bond films. In his autobiography, he described the property as “near London and perfect for a growing family.”

Its appeal extended to 11 acres of grounds, which included a tennis court, heated swimming pool and greenhouses when the house was advertised for £5 million in 2015.

When he finally took on Bond's on-screen persona, Timothy Dalton was well-prepared for the role.

Producers considered Timothy Dalton for Bond early in his career, but he felt too young for the role. When he eventually signed on for The Living Daylights in 1986, he reread Fleming’s novels to prepare for a more serious interpretation of the character.

Pierce Brosnan at home in Los Angeles in 1983.

Pierce Brosnan was making his name in Remington Steele when he posed at his Los Angeles home in June 1983, but his introduction to the world of Bond had come through his first wife, actress Cassandra Harris. She appeared in For Your Eyes Only, and visiting her on location in Corfu brought him into the producers’ orbit. Brosnan was offered the role in 1986, but was prevented from taking it due to his television commitments at the time. His debut in GoldenEye came almost a decade later, in 1995.

Pierce Brosnan playing pool with his son, Sean, at their Los Angeles home in 1992.

This 1992 photographs show Brosnan playing guitar on the sofa, shooting pool with his eight-year-old son Sean, and entertaining two dogs in the garden. Cream sofas and floral upholstery adorn the living room, while arched windows and white shutters open the space to the surrounding greenery. Taken the year after Harris died of ovarian cancer, the photographs capture Brosnan bringing up their family before Bond transformed his career.

Pierce Brosnan with his dogs outside of their Los Angeles home in 1992.

Painting had become a creative outlet for the star during Harris’s illness. Having trained as a commercial artist before acting, Brosnan returned to it in the late 1980s, recalling on his website: “I started painting again and out came every colour.” By the time he became Bond, painting was so sufficiently embedded in his life that his dressing room at Leavesden doubled as a studio during GoldenEye.

Daniel Craig attends the Bond 25 film launch at Ian Fleming's Home, GoldenEye, in Montego Bay, Jamaica, 2019.

For this final entry, the front door stays closed. Of all the former James Bond actors, Daniel Craig represents a more guarded approach to sharing domestic life. His wife, actress Rachel Weisz, explained the couple’s position in a 2012 conversation with British Vogue: “I think it’s pretty ordinary to think that marriage is private and sacred.”

Speculation over who will inherit Craig's martini glass keeps his name in the headlines even after his departure.

#4836. Tesla launches Robotaxi in San Antonio with a 274 sq mi geofence on paper

Tesla is launching its Robotaxi service in San Antonio, its eighth city, with a launch geofence covering about 274 square miles. That’s the biggest day-one map Tesla has ever posted, and more than four times the size of Waymo’s San Antonio service area.

But if Tesla’s recent maps are any guide, a big chunk of that area won’t be bookable. In Austin, an independent check found the Robotaxi app only accepted pickups and drop-offs in about 59% of the zone Tesla advertises.

Tesla’s official Robotaxi account made the announcement just after midnight local time: “Robotaxi available in San Antonio starting tomorrow, 5am.” It came with a map of the “launch geofence,” a large polygon that runs from Loop 1604 on the north side down to just south of downtown.

Robotaxi Tracker, which measures operator maps and logs fleet sightings, puts the zone at 274 square miles. That covers about 1.0 million residents (51% of the metro) and 721,000 jobs. San Antonio International Airport sits inside the polygon, but it isn’t served.

Waymo started driverless rides in San Antonio in February with more than 60 square miles and opened to all riders in October, airport trips included. Robotaxi Tracker currently lists Waymo’s San Antonio area at 59 square miles.

What Tesla didn’t say is how many cars will cover its 274 square miles. As of Monday morning, Robotaxi Tracker hadn’t logged a single Tesla Robotaxi in San Antonio.

The launch landed the same day Tesla expanded Austin again. The tracker now measures Tesla’s Austin zone at 443 square miles, up 178 square miles since mid-September.

We’ve seen this movie. Last month, Tesla expanded its Houston geofence into the shape of a rocket, and within two days the creator of Robotaxi Tracker found the app refusing downtown pickups inside it.

Since then, Koby Pierce, who tracks Tesla’s service on X, has done the tedious work of checking city after city. He searched thousands of real addresses in the Robotaxi app on an emulator and mapped only the ones where Tesla allows a pickup or drop-off, then compared that with the map Tesla advertises. Here’s the share of Tesla’s advertised land area that actually works:

Austin's dropoff/pickup Geofence is ~41% smaller than advertised.Seems mostly the East side of town is inaccessible. Unsure the cause. Any ideas?Dallas is next and should be done by noon tomrrow. pic.twitter.com/WvU99sJlJh

Austin is the one that matters. It’s Tesla’s flagship market, with 453 unsupervised vehicles in service according to Robotaxi Tracker. Yet about 41% of the advertised map Pierce tested, mostly on the east side of town, wouldn’t take a ride request. Pierce points to railroad crossings as a big factor in several cities, which lines up with the strange downtown routing we saw in Houston.

To be fair, Pierce says Waymo is next on his list, and he expects “they’re no better.” Robotaxi Tracker already had to trim Waymo’s Orlando area last month after finding the airport, inside Waymo’s published map, wasn’t selectable. We’ll look at those numbers when they’re out. But it doesn’t change what Tesla is doing with San Antonio today.

Credit where it’s due. A 274-square-mile launch in a new city is progress, and Tesla now runs unsupervised rides in eight cities. At the start of this year, it had no unsupervised rides anywhere.

But we need to stop taking Tesla’s geofence numbers at face value, and that includes us. We used Tesla’s 254-square-mile figure for Houston, and it turned out to be overstated. In all six cities Pierce has checked, the real service area is between 59% and 78% of what Tesla posts. In Austin and Miami, it’s about 60%.

So the honest way to read the San Antonio announcement is “up to 274 square miles.” In practice, expect something closer to 160 to 210 square miles, with an unknown number of cars, and the airport already off the table. That’s still bigger than Waymo’s 59 square miles in the city.

These maps are mostly fiction. I live inside the Houston map but the app says I am outside. If you drive to where the app allows, no vehicles are ever available. Waymos are a routine site and are available on a short notice. This is smoke and mirrors to shore up the company value ahead of the SpaceX merger.

The problem is that square miles are the metric Tesla fully controls. Drawing a polygon costs nothing, and the numbers were all over X within hours of the post. Cars per square mile, wait times, and rides completed are much harder to inflate, and nationally Waymo still runs over 4,000 driverless vehicles to Tesla’s 666, per Robotaxi Tracker.

I still consider this a small pilot by any standard, and safety remains the bottleneck. Tesla is still forcing riders through hour-long rides that should take 15 minutes because they have to take routes that avoid areas that Tesla can’t safely navigate without supervision.

Until that’s fixed, robotaxi won’t be able to meaningfully scale beyond these limited demonstrations in geo-fenced areas.

Robotaxi or not, most EV charging still happens at home, and pairing it with rooftop solar can cut your cost per mile to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #4833. Sanctions fears loom over Russia’s regulated crypto trading launch

Russia’s crypto trading rollout faces uncertainty as companies weigh sanctions exposure, while Moscow Exchange prepares to launch crypto trading in December.

Russia’s push to bring cryptocurrency trading under government oversight is raising concerns that greater transparency could expose market participants to international sanctions.

Some companies are reluctant to join the Bank of Russia’s public registry of crypto exchangers because they fear becoming sanctions targets, a person familiar the regulatory discussions told Cointelegraph on Wednesday.

Industry representatives repeatedly asked the central bank to keep the registry private, but the regulator refused, arguing that crypto businesses should follow the same requirements as traditional financial service providers, the source said.

The concerns come as the European Union prepares its 22nd sanctions package against Russia, which is expected to include new restrictions on crypto platforms.

Russia’s new rules allowing retail investors to buy Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and Tether’s USDt (USDT) took effect on Monday, and the central bank’s registry listed 30 authorized exchange operators as of Wednesday.

The list includes major lenders Sberbank and VTB, but registration alone does not establish whether customers can already complete transactions.

Related: KuCoin suspends services in Russia and Belarus

Alexander Nam of HSE University’s Center for Decentralized Finance and Digital Financial Assets Research said the market still faces technical and regulatory hurdles, including investor testing, order execution, settlement and custody.

“Being included in the registry does not in itself mean readiness to fully serve investors,” Nam told Cointelegraph.

Alexander Brazhnikov of the Russian Association of Cryptocurrency and Blockchain told Cointelegraph that he could not identify a registered operator offering confirmed retail access as of Monday.

Moscow Exchange (MOEX), Russia’s main securities trading venue, plans to launch crypto trading on Dec. 1, with investors accessing the market through brokers rather than directly through the exchange. Cointelegraph contacted VTB, T-Invest Lab and MOEX for further details on their respective crypto trading launch plans but had not received responses by publication time.

The European Commission reportedly plans to present its next sanctions package ahead of a Nov. 26–27 European Council summit, days before MOEX’s scheduled launch.

Russian President Vladimir Putin said Wednesday that Western sanctions had backfired and strengthened Russia’s economy, speaking at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen.

Russia’s broader crypto activity has declined despite its push to establish a regulated trading market. The value of cryptocurrency transactions fell 14.7% to $284.1 billion in the year ended June 2026, according to Chainalysis. The country still accounted for roughly 44% of Eastern Europe’s crypto activity.

#4831. 19 Best Christmas Ornament Picks, Vetted by AD (2026)

There’s no universally correct way to decorate a Christmas tree, though everyone seems to have an opinion about it. Some people swear by coordinated color palettes and perfectly spaced ornaments. Others treasure the funky glass baubles that have graced their family trees for decades, a hand-carved wooden figure picked up at a German Christmas market, or the lopsided ornament made in elementary school. The sentimental ones usually come with better stories. A Christmas tree, after all, is as much a collection of memories as it is a decorating opportunity.

And yet, there’s always room for one more. Maybe it’s a handblown glass ornament, a miniature version of your favorite food, or a decoration that’s so uniquely you it somehow feels essential. Your tree doesn’t need a theme, and your ornaments certainly don’t need to match. We’ve rounded up our favorites, including sculptural glass baubles, handwoven treasures, cheerful little oddities, and keepsakes you’ll look forward to unpacking every December.

These 3D-printed ornaments have the nostalgic glow of vintage Christmas lights, even when the tree is unplugged. Designed by Austrian architecture firm Sheyn, their sculptural shapes give the classic holiday bauble a whimsical, modern twist. The watermelon and grapefruit hues are a refreshing departure from the usual red and green.

Sleek, understated, and not the least bit fussy, this Villeroy & Boch set deserves a moment beyond the tree. Tuck a few into a wreath or garland, where they won’t have to compete with a tree’s worth of tinsel and twinkling lights.

Trade sparkles for natural texture with these ornaments handcrafted by Colombian artisans in the Andes. They bring a well-traveled touch to your tree, and the best part is that each one is a surprise, with its color combination chosen by the maker. They’re also far less fragile than glass when your dog’s tail inevitably sends one flying.

If you’ve ever rearranged a vase just to catch the afternoon light, you’ll appreciate the way these ornaments from il Buco Vita play with it. Handblown in Italy from recycled glass, each one has subtle irregularities and shifting blue-green hues that take on a new life among the twinkling lights on a Christmas tree. This delicate floral shape is pretty enough to keep out long after the tree comes down.

Here’s a new tradition for the little ones: Unwrap one of these miniature porcelain ornaments each day like an advent calendar as you sing your way through the Twelve Days of Christmas. The set from Lenox includes all the familiar characters, like the partridge in a pear tree and the twelve drummers drumming. Apologies in advance for the song getting stuck in everyone’s head.

Balsam Hill’s expertise with high-quality trees extend to their collection of decorative baubles. Don’t underestimate the power of classic shapes—like a set of metallic orbs in varying sizes.

Your love of rattan doesn’t have to stop at your furniture. These woven bell ornaments bring a little natural texture to the festivities, with warm, honey-colored tones that look right at home among your Christmas tree’s evergreen branches.

You don’t need an Eichler home to have a very merry, midcentury Christmas. With their geometric silhouettes and gold detailing, these glass ornaments bring a dose of vintage glitz to the tree.

Leave it to Alessi to make Grinch green look downright elegant. These blown-glass ornaments, designed by Nika Zupanc for Alessi, dress up the famously mischievous shade with graphic stripes, playful polka dots, and touches of gold. Even the Grinch might reconsider his stance on Christmas.

Playing with sizing is key to adding dimension to your Christmas tree and these darling miniature ornaments offer a rainbow of colors to dial up the saturation on your tree. They come with mini ribbons that you’ll need to hand knot, but we think they add an extra personal touch.

Nostalgia looks best in a brass frame. With its braided border and antique-inspired finish, this ornament from McGee & Co. makes a thoughtful gift—just print a favorite photo and tuck it inside before wrapping.

When in doubt, put a bow on it. These delicate velvet bows are slightly understated; pin them to the tree in lieu of ornaments for a charming coquettish vibe or have the two join forces for maximum drama.

Since your order for the table is always a dozen oysters on the half shell, why not dish out the same for your Christmas tree? And if you’re trying to nail down a fête-themed tree, you’ll probably want some Champagne to go with it as well.

Our love of gnomes is no secret, and a set of ten might just be enough for a holiday fix. Each fabric gnome measures 5.7" x 2.2" and can be hung from your tree or placed around the house as holiday decor.

Pescatarian or tinned fish enthusiast, this glitter-flecked glass Christmas ornament is a lighthearted way to tell your favorite “girl dinner” guest how much you appreciate them. Coming in at 4" tall and 1.5" in diameter, it’s the perfect size for anchoring a branch.

You’ll thank us when you’re looking back at photos of the tree in January and admiring how dimensional it looks even from a phone screen. This set includes a collection of matte, metallic, and flocked ornaments so you don’t have to over think the process and you don’t have to go crazy buying individual ornaments.

This French bread is made of glass, so it will really shine against your tree lights. It’s got a kitschy yet vintage vibe, and it will stand out from other icicle-style ornaments.

Vintage-inspired with wintery botanical etchings, these glassy ornaments have some rustic appeal but still feel elegant. We’d pair them with some silver tinsel to really bring the sparkle.

Handblown from clear, silver glass, these finials and spheres add some metallic elegance to your tree, especially if your inspiration is a disco ball.

#4830. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, tokenized Nvidia’s market cap surged 159% as the chipmaker’s stock sank, reports of cryptocurrency theft involving tampered Ledger hardware wallets have emerged in Dubai and Eastern Europe recorded the steepest decline in crypto activity of any region tracked by Chainalysis.

The combined market value of tokenized stocks tied to Nvidia shares rose about 159% from Nov. 8 to Tuesday, while the chipmaker’s Nasdaq-listed shares fell nearly 17% from their last close before co-founder Jensen Huang publicly admitted to an extramarital affair.

The value of Nvidia (NVDA) tokens tracked by Token Terminal rose from $155 million on Nov. 8 to $401.9 million by Tuesday.

Nvidia shed about $750 billion in market value, falling from $4.5 trillion at the Nov. 6 close to $3.75 trillion at Tuesday’s close, according to Yahoo Finance data.

Huang acknowledged the affair in a message to Nvidia employees on Nov. 8, nearly two weeks after The New York Times reported that US intelligence officials suspected Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang of spying for Beijing. China denied the allegations.

Tokenized stocks are blockchain tokens designed to track the price of a share. In one common setup, an issuer creates tokens backed by shares held by a custodian.

Binance, for example, lists an Nvidia token that can trade when Nasdaq is closed. It gives investors exposure to Nvidia’s share price without shareholder rights. The market value can rise as more tokens enter circulation, even if Nvidia’s share price falls.

Two Ledger owners have reported cryptocurrency thefts involving hardware wallets they say were purchased from an authorized retailer in Dubai, raising concerns that a hardware wallet tampering campaign had also spread to the UAE.

An X user posting as Rewinsky on Monday said they found their Ledger Flex had been tampered with after reporting the loss of about 1,300 Solana (SOL) Mark Karpelès’ Ledger Implant OSINT registry subsequently confirmed the device as containing a hardware implant.

A second owner, posting as New Babylon Order Worldwide, said Tuesday that 507,000 USDt (USDT) was stolen from their Ledger Stax on Saturday. They said the device was bought new at Virgin Megastore Dubai on Oct. 11, two days after Ledger publicly disclosed an investigation into crypto losses involving devices sold by Southeast Asian reseller CryptoBilis.

The reports suggest the Ledger device tampering has extended past retailers in just Asia and Europe. Ledger lists Virgin Megastores among its authorized retailers in the United Arab Emirates.

Eastern Europe recorded the steepest decline in cryptocurrency activity among the regions tracked by Chainalysis in the year ended June 2026, falling 7.9% even as retail-sized transfers increased in value, according to a report published Tuesday.

The region’s crypto economy shrank to $642.1 billion from $697.1 billion a year earlier, compared with a 1.6% decline globally, the report states. Russia, which accounted for roughly 44% of the regional total, saw activity fall 14.7% to $284.1 billion over the period. By contrast, Belarus recorded 27.6% growth, while Bulgaria, Moldova and Ukraine saw smaller increases.

The value of transfers of $10,000 or more to crypto services fell 17.4%, from $591.5 billion to $488.6 billion, according to the report.

The value of smaller transfers to crypto services, meanwhile, rose 8.3%. These retail-sized transfers accounted for 13.1% of the value sent to crypto services in Eastern Europe, the highest share of any region studied and above the global average of 10.1%.

Eastern European crypto activity by country, July 2025 to June 2026. Source: Chainalysis report

#4827. LiveWire just showed off its biggest electric motorcycle lineup yet

LiveWire has spent the last few years expanding beyond the premium electric motorcycles that originally defined the Harley-Davidson spin-off. But seeing the company’s latest machines all together at EICMA in Milan really drives home just how much broader LiveWire’s ambitions have become.

I stopped by the LiveWire booth at EICMA 2026, where the company had everything from its new lightweight S4 Honcho models to the freshly acquired DUST electric dirt bikes – plus STACYC’s electric balance bikes for the youngest riders.

It’s quite a spread, and perhaps more importantly, it paints a picture of a company that increasingly wants to have an electric two-wheeler for just about every stage of a rider’s life.

I also had a chance to spend several hours test-riding the S4 Honcho Street in Milan, so be on the lookout for that article and video to experience it with me.

But for now, let’s see what was at the LiveWire booth.

Perhaps the most important bikes at the booth were the S4 Honcho models, LiveWire’s new lightweight and lower-cost electric motorcycles.

The production S4 Honcho Street made its European debut at EICMA alongside the dirt-oriented S4 Honcho Trail. The Trail version recently began reaching customers in the US, with the Street now en route to dealerships, and European sales are beginning around the EICMA launch.

These bikes represent a significant departure from LiveWire’s earlier strategy. Instead of trying to compete directly with full-size gasoline motorcycles on outright performance, the Honcho platform is aimed at riders looking for something smaller, simpler, and more approachable.

And now the family is getting even bigger.

Also on display was the new S4 Honcho Skate, making its first public appearance.

The Skate is planned for 2027 and further expands the modular Honcho platform with a stripped-down, playful design.

For those who have followed H-D’s electric ambitions for as long as I have, you might even remember the original Harley-Davidson electric moped prototype that seems to have served as inspiration for the Honcho Skate’s design.

Seeing the various Honcho versions together also makes LiveWire’s strategy with the platform much clearer. Rather than creating one small electric motorcycle, the company is building an entire family around the concept.

The other major attraction at LiveWire’s booth was the DUST lineup.

LiveWire acquired the assets of DUST Moto earlier this year, bringing the lightweight electric dirt bike company under its umbrella. EICMA marked the first public showing of the production-intent DUST Hightail under LiveWire ownership.

The Hightail had already generated plenty of interest before the acquisition as a lightweight, high-performance electric dirt bike. Now we’re getting our first look at how that machine fits into LiveWire’s broader plans.

The company is targeting a 2027 introduction for the production Hightail.

LiveWire also showed the new DUST Spur, another model planned for 2027 and intended to expand the newly formed DUST family.

The DUST acquisition is particularly interesting because it gives LiveWire an immediate foothold in the rapidly growing lightweight electric off-road motorcycle space without having to develop an entirely new platform from scratch.

In what could be a hint at faster charging to come, there was an interesting S2 Alpanista Corsa motorcycle that had been outfitted with a NACS plug and DC fast-charging capabilities.

LiveWire claimed a 20-80% charge in under 10 minutes, which would revolutionize the ability to do long-distance motorcycle touring, or just make it more convenient to get a quick charge and head back to your apartment.

There’s on word on whether the concept will make it to production, but the specs are enticing!

The most interesting part of LiveWire’s EICMA display may not have been any individual motorcycle, but rather what happens when you stand back and look at the entire booth.

On one end you’ve got STACYC electric balance bikes. LiveWire says the brand has already introduced more than 250,000 riders to two wheels.

Then you’ve got the lightweight S4 Honcho family. Above that sit the DUST machines for more serious off-road riding, while LiveWire’s existing S2 motorcycles continue to cover the higher-performance street end of the spectrum.

That’s becoming something of an electric two-wheeler ladder.

A kid can theoretically start on a STACYC, graduate into increasingly capable electric two-wheelers, and eventually wind up on a proper electric motorcycle – all within the same corporate family.

And that feels particularly relevant at a show like EICMA, where much of the motorcycle industry is still trying to figure out exactly where electric motorcycles fit.

LiveWire seems increasingly convinced that the answer isn’t simply to build electric replacements for existing gasoline motorcycles.

Instead, the company is spreading out into categories where electric drivetrains can create different kinds of motorcycles altogether.

I’ve been following LiveWire’s evolution for years, and the company standing in front of me at EICMA looks considerably different from the one that started out selling a single expensive flagship electric motorcycle.

The big, high-performance bikes are still important, but machines like the S4 Honcho and DUST Hightail are arguably more interesting to me because they can bring entirely different riders into electric motorcycling.

They’re smaller. They’re simpler. And perhaps most importantly, they’re targeting the kind of fun, accessible riding that doesn’t require superbike levels of performance.

There’s still plenty we need to learn about several of these new models, particularly final specifications and pricing for the 2027 machines.

But seeing the Honcho, Honcho Skate, Hightail, Spur, and STACYC machines together at EICMA makes one thing pretty clear: LiveWire isn’t just trying to sell a few electric motorcycles anymore.

It’s trying to build an entire electric two-wheeler ecosystem.

#4826. CoinShares sells all treasury ETH, halves SOL holdings for buybacks

The October sales raised $19.3 million, nearly half the $40 million CoinShares can spend on buybacks before its next annual meeting.

European digital asset manager CoinShares sold its entire Ether treasury position and half of its Solana treasury holdings in October, raising $19.3 million to fund share buybacks.

CoinShares raised $16.6 million from the Ether sale and $2.7 million from the Solana sale, the company said in its third-quarter results on Wednesday.

CoinShares reported treasury holdings of 6,562 Ether (ETH) at a fair value of $17.6 million and 50,213 Solana (SOL) valued at $5.9 million as of Sept. 30. The company also held 235 Bitcoin (BTC) worth $19.6 million at quarter-end.

CoinShares’ board approved up to $40 million in buybacks on Oct. 22 under a five-year authorization that shareholders granted in September. The authorization covers up to 32.9 million shares, or 25% of shares outstanding. The $40 million program runs until the company’s next annual meeting, expected in the second quarter of 2027.

“That’s nearly half of the $40 million authorized by the Board and gives us the flexibility to act when conditions allow,” said Jean-Marie Mognetti, co-founder, president and CEO of CoinShares, referring to the sale proceeds in a Wednesday press release.

CoinShares also reported $7.4 billion in total assets under management, up from $5.5 billion at the end of June, driven by rising crypto prices and $125 million in net inflows during the quarter. Revenue fell 45% from a year earlier to $27.7 million, including $19.1 million in asset management fees.

CoinShares’ shares fell 5.41% to close at $3.15 on Tuesday, ahead of the earnings report, leaving them down 63% from their $8.61 close on the company’s April 1 Nasdaq debut.

#4825. 6 Best Bamboo Sheets That Mirror Silk, Tested by AD (2026)

If you love the slight sheen and smooth feel of silk bedding like me, my guess is that bamboo sheets are probably up your alley too. The natural material can make your bedroom look elevated, and you won’t actually have to pay for pure silk or satin.

What makes bamboo sheets so popular is that they’re also just as good for hot sleepers: They have moisture-wicking properties, are hypoallergenic by nature, and feel cool to the touch (no disrespect to linen lovers). The lightweight weave also has a lovely drape to it. For a dramatic look, especially in a dark and moody color palette like this Upper East Side family home, these sumptuous sheets can’t be beat.

I’ve put on Sleep Week for the last couple of years, where AD editors share the essentials for styling a bedroom. Needless to say, I’ve tried a lot of sheets from our roster of beloved bedding brands to match my ever-changing space. I arguably have too many sets to count, some new and some old, to give my space personality—and my colleagues can say the same. After all this research, I can confidently share this concise list of our all-time favorites in the bamboo category.

For consistency, all of the prices reflect queen sizes. However, many brands reach up to a king size.

Cozy Earth is a personal favorite of mine for bamboo sheets—the brand’s bread and butter. They’re so breathable—even when I layer with a thick down comforter on top and throw blanket at the foot of the bed when it’s cold outside or when my AC is blasting—and have deep pockets to fit my notoriously tall mattress and topper combo. My taste just so happens to overlap with Oprah’s, if you’ve peeked at her Favorite Things list recently, which feels like a good omen, too.

I’m not the only fan, this set has earned the top overall pick in this story because pretty much every Shop AD writer, editor, and contributor who’s tried the Cozy Earth bamboo sheet set stands behind it for a few reasons: It is temperature-regulating, long-lasting (even after several cycles in the washing machine), and some of the softest bedding out there. Contributor Yelena Moroz Alpert calls her relationship to Cozy Earth a “borderline obsession” because of the natural fibers and neutral colorways that give the bedding an “earthy sophistication.” Shopping editor Audrey Lee calls the sheets “super light and airy, so it’s mostly a summer staple.”

Contributor Megan Wahn adds that the set is a favorite of hers because it feels much more expensive than it actually is: “I honestly thought these sheets were silk when I first slept on them, that’s how smooth and buttery soft they feel.” I love the walnut shade since my bedroom leans more moody, but if you’re into a clean, neutral shade like 2026’s color of the year, Cloud Dancer, I recommend the white or gray color for a calm, minimal bedroom. Their shore set is another style for anyone who likes a more coastal look. But Alpert says it best: “Not sure how Cozy Earth does it but the sheets feel like velvet without being heavy.”

Quince is the luxury-on-a-budget brand I’ve become pretty familiar with over the last two years of trying their duvets, sheet sets, and even their bath towels—and the bamboo sheets don’t fall short. Breathable bedding is a must for my sleep setup, and I’ve been a little surprised by how well I sleep through the night with this set. It feels smooth and cool to the touch, but still cozy enough to bundle up with during the winter months. One of my best friends is a hot sleeper, so whenever he visits, I use these (and the matching bamboo duvet cover) to make his bed. He says these truly keep him at a comfortable temperature throughout the night, so he’s never throwing the covers off mid-REM sleep.

This is the brand I’d recommend if you want to style your bed to a tee. I have the bamboo sheets in a warm terracotta hue to give my bedroom a serene, almost desert Airbnb-like aesthetic in the summertime. There are almost a dozen neutral and vibrant earth tones to match your bedroom’s design, though. These points, on top of the sub-$150 price tag, earned them a top spot in this list. However, if you’re like me and wear oily sheet masks and use thick body butters at night, you might notice some light staining on the sheets.

Because of their glossy sheen, these Luxome sheets probably look the closest to silk out of all of the picks in this list. I have the bedding in a truffle color, but what makes this option so unique are the jewel tones available, like sapphire, that are hard to find with other brands. The generous size of these sheets (they have a 17-inch pocket depth) gives them an even more elegant drape for a glam bedroom.

The neutral tones are compelling, too. For Sleep Week this March, the Shop AD team and I explored how to design a bedroom with a narrative, so we designed rooms inspired by classic novels. One of those storybook sanctuaries took inspiration from Dune, and I think Luxome’s sheets (which happen to have the same name) have a cool, chrome-like finish for fans of futuristic Sci-Fi novels like this one. All in all, these are also soft and breathable, a welcome relief for me when I’m combating hot NYC days.

Yes, we like heritage brands that have been acclaimed for ages at AD, but we’re always on the hunt for new and budget-friendly brands that are worthwhile, too. Sijo launched an Amazon-exclusive line a few years ago that offers a luxe feel and sustainable material sourcing at a lower price point. This basic Doz by Sijo set of bamboo sheets is another staple for my sleep setup. My skin has gotten pretty sensitive over time, and I love how soothed I feel when I sink into these sheets. They have a little more of a polished texture than other picks in this list, so I glide into bed. It’s hard to tell the difference between these and other high-end picks on the market by virtue of their material; it feels like a softer, less slippery satin. The pockets aren’t as deep as Cozy Earth’s, and the colors aren’t as striking as Quince’s burgundy and terracotta options, but for the price, I say it’s still a nice and simple set to shop.

Even though contributor Blake Bakkila prefers more textured bedding like linen, she says Cariloha’s “Aloha” bedding has been a saving grace to keep her cool. “These were the perfect sheets to layer on our bed during a heat wave that rocked Southern California, and they only got softer with each wash,” she shares. After Bakkila and her husband adjusted to the supple texture, she says the “temperature-regulating bamboo” sheets have been a great choice to keep in rotation since they’re “exceptionally smooth and lightweight” with a “cooling effect.” Keep in mind that these are truly thin sheets and almost have a transparent appearance. So if you’re looking for bedding that’s a bit weightier, the Quince or Cozy Earth in this list might be a better way to go.

These sheets are made of 100% bamboo infused with what Bearaby calls “smart botanicals,” including extracts like peppermint and marine proteins. They don’t feel sticky like some silky sheets can when it’s hot outside—they’re airy and refreshing to sleep on and the minty essence only enhances the feeling of freshness. These botanicals do more than smell good—they eliminate up to 99.9% of bacteria directly within the fabric. As your heat rises, the botanicals activate to cool you while keeping the material clean. And—to answer the million-dollar question—the sheets do live up to their “second skin” name—the fabric is so soft and fluid, it feels like it's melting on my body.

Here at Shop AD, we don’t test sheets in a lab—we test them in our very own homes. Why? Because that’s where you’ll be using them too. Each of the sets in our Best Of roundups has been washed, dried, and slept on for countless nights by one of our editors to ensure that they’ve put them through the ringer. The sets are selected to reflect a variety of price points, materials, and styles, and we pride ourselves in giving each set firsthand, real-world use.

Whether you’re a bamboo bedding faithful or it’s your first time exploring the category, there are a few factors I recommend keeping in mind below.

When it comes to the world of linens, bamboo fibers are made from the bamboo plant, which requires far less water than cotton and regrows quickly, so bamboo sheets are often considered one of the more eco-friendly options available. There are few popular bamboo materials: bamboo viscose, rayon, and lyocell. All are antimicrobial, soft to the touch, and absorbent. Depending on if and what they are blended with, they can take on more linen- or cotton-like properties. Here’s a breakdown of each:

The fabric’s weave will change the texture, softness, and general propensity for wrinkling. Below, here are more details on the two most popular weaves: twill, sateen, and percale.

To keep laundry day simple, the bedding in our list simply requires a machine wash and tumble dry. We generally recommend washing on cold and drying on low, as these gentler settings can help preserve the bedding’s quality. However, be sure to check the labels on the sheets for any specific care instructions.

While all bamboo sheets do a superb job of temperature regulating, your best bet (for overall comfort plus silkiness!) is to find a 100% bamboo viscose or lyocell set—like the Cozy Earth pick—with a thread count between 250 and 350, made with a sateen or percale weave. This typically indicates a durable weave that won’t wear easily over time.

As enthusiasts for all things interiors, we recommend looking for bamboo sheet sets that come in colors, prints, and patterns that can match your bedroom’s design. So, if you lean more minimalist, try neutrals like ivory. If you love maximalist style, go big with bold designs and hues—from embroidered details to splashy colors like terracotta or butter yellow.

You can usually find a set of this bedding type between the $50 to $400 range. To get the most out of your purchase, we recommend looking for eco-friendly certifications to help make sure your sheet set has the best quality. For example, the Oeko-Tex Standard 100 applies to bedding that is free of harmful chemicals.

In essence, yes—but how much so really depends on the quality of your set. Bamboo bedding is known for being cool to the touch overall, but the weave itself will ultimately determine the breathability of the sheets. Keep an eye out for bamboo sheets with a 300 thread count weave or above, as our editors mention that these typically have the most airflow for a good night’s rest.

Is Bamboo Viscose Better Than Egyptian Cotton?

Bamboo sheets, because of the strength of their fibers, actually have better longevity than cotton sheets, and offer superior moisture-wicking and temperature regulation to cotton sheets. Bamboo, especially bamboo viscose, also brags a smoother, more soothing feel against the skin compared to cotton, which can require more washes to get the the super soft or silky feel you desire.

Yes! The nature of bamboo is hypoallergenic, making bamboo bed sheets perfect for those with sensitive skin, eczema, or anyone prone to allergies. Bamboo’s super-fine fibers inherently resist things like dust mites, mold, and mildew. Even at the molecular level, it’s a moisture-wicking material, helping you stay sweat-free while also creating an environment inhospitable for dust mites (that need moisture). The fibers are naturally smooth, too, making for a soft sleeping experience.

How Do Bamboo Sheets Compare to Linen in Terms of Softness and Durability?

In our experience, both materials are durable when the sheets are made of 100% natural fibers instead of blends, but bamboo sheets are much softer. Unless it’s stonewashed, linen is usually known to feel a bit scratchy at first and then soften with wear and care. Bamboo sheets are softer down to their microscopic makeup, as their very fibers are smoother than linen.
