#4822. Bitcoin Covenants Part 6: FE Covenants — Enforcing covenants through cryptographically restricted signing keys

FE covenants encode spending constraints into the signing key, so only transactions that satisfy a predefined condition can be signed.

This is Part 6 in the technical article series about Bitcoin covenants by Cointelegraph Research. To read the previous article, click here.

Changing Bitcoin’s consensus rules is not merely a technical process. It requires that the network’s node operators, miners, developers and economic actors coordinate around a change. This coordination is difficult to achieve, which is why Bitcoin’s base layer has gradually ossified.

Researchers have spent the last several years investigating whether covenant behavior could be enforced without touching consensus rules. In May 2024, Jeremy Rubin, the original author of the OP_CTV proposal (BIP-119), offered an answer in a paper titled FE’d Up Covenants. His design proposed using functional encryption (FE) to move the covenant out of Bitcoin Script and into the signing key.

In ordinary public-key cryptography, a private key can sign any message. In hypothetical FE-based covenants, signing would be restricted to a predefined set of valid transactions. Bitcoin nodes would verify the signature with OP_CHECKSIG exactly as they do today, obviating the need for a soft fork.

Functional encryption (FE) generalizes public-key encryption. In ordinary cryptographic schemes, a private key can be used to fully decrypt a ciphertext and arrive at the plaintext. In FE, a master secret is instead used to issue subsidiary functional keys, each of which reveals only one chosen function of the plaintext. The holder of the functional key for a function f and the ciphertext of a secret x can compute f(x) but never gets to reveal x as a plaintext message.

In the academic literature, FE was first formalized by Dan Boneh, Amit Sahai and Brent Waters in 2011. Constructions for arbitrary functions followed from 2013, with polynomial runtime but overheads far beyond the resources of current computing technology. Furthermore, their security rested on new, untested building blocks called multilinear maps. Only in 2021 did researchers develop constructions from well-founded cryptographic assumptions, which could in principle open the door to practical implementations of FE.

The f in an FE scheme can be any feasibly computable function. However, in Bitcoin covenant constructions, f is a signing function. Its output is a Bitcoin signature for a transaction tx. Because this signature depends on both a private key and the transaction that is being signed, it takes at least two inputs, which is why Rubin’s design uses multi-input FE. For a covenant that only permits a single spending transaction, akin to OP_CTV, two inputs suffice. General, expressive constructions take more arguments, as in Rubin’s f(k, tx, program, proof).

Crucially, f does not sign directly with the private key k that is supplied in an encrypted form. Instead, the function derives a new key pair by tweaking k with a hash of the covenant’s spending policy. Signatures produced by f are valid for the tweaked public key, not for the base public key P itself.

To enable FE-based covenants from these primitives, a setup authority is required. It generates a Bitcoin key pair (the private key k and the public key P) as well as an FE key pair (the master secret msk and the FE public key mpk). It publishes P, mpk, k encrypted to mpk, and the functional key for f, which it derives from msk. The setup authority then needs to be trusted to destroy k and msk. Otherwise, it could sign spends that violate the covenant. With this setup completed, an indefinite number of new covenants may be created.

Every covenant has its own dedicated key pair, obtained by tweaking (k, P) with the spending policy. The private key of this tweaked key pair exists only inside f and is never revealed. The public key can be derived from P by anyone who knows the hash of the desired spending policy. This way, covenant receiving addresses can be generated. In practice, the receiving address would commit to a 2-of-2 script that requires a signature from a key the user controls alongside the FE signature. This is to keep unauthorized parties from triggering a transaction permitted by the covenant.

To create an FE signature for a covenant spend, the signer takes a tx that conforms to the spending policy and encrypts it to mpk. Using the encrypted tx, together with the encrypted k and the functional key published by the authority, they can then evaluate f. The function f signs tx with k tweaked by the hash of the spending policy. A transaction that breaks the spending policy of the address that holds the coins could at best be signed for some other, differently tweaked public key that does not control the funds.The function f can also be designed to block such a signature outright.

The general version of FE-based covenants with f(k, tx, program, proof) is strictly more expressive than OP_CTV. The general function proposed by Rubin takes a program that checks a condition on the transaction at spend time, and a zero-knowledge proof that the program accepts the transaction.

This zero-knowledge proof facilitates the implementation of the FE scheme, but does not add to it conceptually. A single fixed f works either way. Without the proof, f would have to be a general interpreter that runs any given program on tx itself. Such an f is large and costly to evaluate under FE. With the proof, f only has to verify a succinct proof. This could make it possible to enforce arbitrarily complex covenants without the need for more complexity in f. The proof also lets a spending policy depend on data outside the transaction, such as the result of an off-chain computation.

Goldwasser et al. (2014) introduced multi-input FE and built it on indistinguishability obfuscation. Multi-input FE and obfuscation are known to imply one another. Ananth and Jain (2015) showed that even single-input FE for general circuits yields obfuscation once it is made compact. The cryptographic primitive this design needs is therefore at least as hard to build as so-called indistinguishability obfuscation itself. Implementations of this principle remain at toy scale.

Carmer, Malozemoff and Raykova (2017) needed 3.7 hours to obfuscate a 12-bit pseudorandom function, a toy circuit of about 62,000 gates with a 64-bit key, far smaller than ECDSA signing on 256-bit numbers. The obfuscated program took 120 GB of disk space, and an evaluation ran for 67 minutes with 128 GB of RAM. The cost of implementing such a scheme would fall on whoever produces the signature, not on Bitcoin nodes. The authors called this “still far from practical.”

Furthermore, FE schemes that are practical cover only narrow function classes, such as the inner products of Abdalla et al. (2015), and the type of ECDSA signing required for Bitcoin covenants is not among them. Rubin’s paper concedes this point and lists under-developed cryptography and the setup trust assumption among its downsides. CTV-style opcodes remain far more efficient in bytes and verification, but require a base-layer change.

In our next article, we turn to Un-FE’d Covenants, which Rubin proposed to approximate covenant behavior without relying on impractical FE schemes. It replaces FE with oracle signers bonded through BitVM. [alloc]init’s Bitcoin PIPEs began in 2024 as an FE design and moved in 2026 to witness encryption. This proposal will also be covered in a follow-up article.

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

#4819. 7 Best Moroccan Rugs, Vetted by AD Editors (2026)

The Moroccan rug is one of the easiest ways to add texture and intrigue to any room. Originally made to protect floors against the harsh cold of the Atlas Mountains, we see these lush, woolly carpets as a staple in the modern design world because of the range of color and style options available. The rug type has become synonymous with traditional undyed cream colors, vibrant jewel tones, and geometric patterns. It’s no wonder they pop up across tons of our home tours—like this artful Brooklyn studio or this floral Victorian house—and have become a favorite among artists like Claudio Bravo and celebs like Patrick Dempsey.

Boucherouite, the hand-weaving technique employed to make these rugs, was created by semi-nomadic Amazigh tribes, and the style can be found in abundance in the busy souks of Marrakech. Now, with many direct-to-consumer and vintage Beni Ourain–inspired styles on the market, the perfect Moroccan rug is just one click away. With all the options at your disposal, it may be intimidating to know where to start. Fear not, we’ve rounded up a few top picks below along with advice on how we’d style them.

Give your guests the coziest welcome with this Azilal rug from Revival Rugs. Its long, narrow design makes it ideal for tight entryways. And while the oblong diamond pattern speaks to geometric patterns you’ll see across traditional Moroccan rugs, the pink tassel is a niche, colorful detail that won’t overpower surrounding decor. This is an authentic hand-knotted Moroccan rug, with wool locally sourced from the Atlas Mountains. Because of its small size, you may also consider hanging it up to add a textural element to your wall decor.

Break up the monotony of home office decor by placing an eclectic rug under your desk setup. While the soft grey and subtle design still create a distraction-free workspace, the cheeky oxblood hues add a touch of personality to make you smile between emails and Zoom meetings.

You don’t have to get a custom carpet straight from Marrakesh to have a high-end rug. The Saira rug from Quince is a handwoven option at an affordable price. The geometric motif is heavily inspired by Moroccan design and the medium-pile, hand-spun wool is designed to feel plush under your feet.

Calling all maximalists and color drenchers. The rich, vibrant colors from this rug come from a family of third-generation dyers, and the irregular shapes of the boxes infuse a quirky texture to whatever room they are placed in. Either have this rug as the standout piece in a room that is otherwise neutral, or pair it with other green pieces, like this couch from Ellison Studios, to engulf yourself in a verdant indoor forest of your own making.

Even though this muted rug may look understated at first glance, it brings visual interest thanks to the grid design along the border. The motif takes inspiration from Mediterranean tile patterns, in case you were wondering. We can see this as a soft, elegant layer underneath your dining table. Water-resistant and machine washable, this rug can survive stains and spills without making you bat an eye.

Why should the adults have all the fun? Bring whimsical design to your child’s bedroom or playroom with this Moroccan area rug from Benisouk. We have a feeling that the high pile will feel comfy for the kids to run around on and durable enough to withstand a little wear and tear from playtime. The dreamy creamsicle hues of this rug can match youthful decor, but the abstract minimalist design is elevated enough to be a piece that grows with them through the years.

If you find yourself longing for sleek, beige interiors—or are in the midst of remodeling your home to mirror an Aman resort—look no further. This handmade sheep’s wool rug is a minimalist dream. The neutral cream brings a layer of calm, while the organic shapes offer a wabi-sabi element that may be necessary in a pristine white room.

#4815. BYD is going all out on its ultra-luxury EV with coach doors and 1,000+ km of range [Images]

BYD unveiled the Yangwang Altior, its new ultra-luxury EV with coach doors, over 1,000 km of CTLC range, a 1,000V fast-charging platform, and plenty of premium extras.

The “super-luxury executive flagship sedan,” as BYD calls it, made its official debut in Abu Dhabi, UAE, over the weekend.

For those who don’t know, Yangwang is BYD’s ultra-luxury sub-brand, sitting at the very top of its brand hierarchy.

The Atrio is Yangwang’s largest and most luxurious sedan, set to compete with high-end brands like Rolls-Royce, Bentley, and Mercedes-Benz Maybach.

Measuring 5,600 mm long, 2,030 mm wide, and 1,590 mm tall, with a 3,500 mm wheelbase, BYD’s flagship sedan sits squarely between the Rolls-Royce Ghost and Phantom (standard wheelbase) in length.

BYD said the “Golden Body Proportions” are designed to maximize interior space for both front and rear passengers.

Its meticulous front-end design is inspired by “Ding,” a traditional ancient Chinese cauldron, featuring “diamond-cut” headlights, a 137-gemstone dot-matrix grille, and, to top it off, a 24K pure gold car emblem sits up front and center.

The coach doors create a grand entrance, with the front doors opening at an 80-degree angle and the rear doors opening to a 90-degree angle.

Inside, the flagship sedan takes luxury up a notch with a massive, full-length panoramic center infotainment screen that extends from the driver to the passenger side.

The “Wrap-around cockpit” is designed to create an immersive, premium experience, with four executive seats and a central island for rear passengers that includes vehicle controls, storage, and even a champagne glass holder.

BYD said it offers the most second-row space in its class, but didn’t reveal specifics. The zero-gravity “cloud-like” rear seats feature a four-ball mechanical massage head, you know, for those longer trips.

Meanwhile, the front passenger seat can fold at the push of a button to open up more space for rear passengers.

The interior features premium materials, including full-grain genuine leather seats, diamond-cut detailing, real wood trim, and natural lambswool foot pads.

And that’s not all. It also has a 39-speaker Dynaudio Platinum Evidence high-end sound system that creates an immersive 360-degree “hall-level” sound experience.

The Yangwang Altio is packed with BYD’s latest breakthrough tech, including a high-speed tire-blowout stability system, emergency float mode, and in-place U-turn or tank-turn control using its quad-motor e4 platform.

It's main rival is the Maextro S800, which wasn't in the list.

It also uses BYD’s second-generation Blade Batteries and Fast Charging Technology, which provides a CLTC driving range of over 1,000 km (621 miles).

The flagship sedan features “extremely fast flash charging,” BYD said, thanks to its 1000V ultra flash charging tech. It takes just five minutes to charge the vehicle’s battery from 10% to 70%. To go from 10% to 97% it takes just nine minutes.

BYD opened orders for the limited-edition “Mountain and River Rhyme” Yangwang Altrio model, which will be extremely exclusive with just 399 units.

Buyers can place a reservation with a 300,000 yuan ($45,000) deposit. BYD didn’t reveal final prices, but the global version is expected to start at around 2 million yuan ($300,000).

#4811. A Brooklyn Townhouse Celebrates a Forgotten Design Pioneer

In the early 1920s, the Pittsburgh-based manufacturer Armstrong Cork Company (now Armstrong World Industries) made the prescient move of tapping a young arts educator named Hazel Dell Brown to act as its in-house decorator. Hers would be an under-recognized yet wildly influential career. Over the course of more than three decades, she designed countless rooms for advertisements, booklets, catalogs, and more that showcased the brand’s products, chief among them linoleum flooring. With their bold colors, fearless mix of patterns, and daring pastiche, her interiors shaped an entire generation of homes in the Rust Belt and beyond.

For two contemporary artists setting out to renovate a Brooklyn town house for their young family, Brown’s little-known work recently served not only as inspiration but also as a litmus test. When interviewing architects to take on the project, the couple first presented examples of vintage Armstrong schemes. “A lot of people would take one look and then show me something different,” reflects the wife, who learned about Brown while working on a project in Pittsburgh. Not so in the case of Tal Schori and Rustam Mehta of GRT Architects. The AD100 duo instantly responded to Brown’s surface-heavy approach and inward-facing embrace of small spaces, or as Schori puts it, “rooms within rooms.” As for their interior designer, Penelope August, a friend of the wife’s since preschool—she implicitly passed with flying colors.

A circa 1980 Gio Ponti chandelier crowns the living room, which is furnished with a vintage Vladimir Kagan sectional sofa and a Jens Risom chair upholstered in a custom fabric based on a Macgregor Harp painting; artwork by Rose Marcus and wallpaper by Antoinette Poisson.

The kitchen features art-glass cabinet fronts by Paul Wissmach Glass Company and a custom terrazzo floor inspired by ancient roman mosaics of unswept debris.

Hembus wallpaper in a custom blue distinguishes the primary bedroom; painting (above bed) by Michael Wang.

The project evolved into a dynamic collaboration as the couple and their team gutted the town house, set in Carroll Gardens and previously renovated in the 1990s. Together they took care to preserve historic details like the original doors and staircase, with its original mahogany newel posts and handrails. Wherever appropriate, they embraced imperfections, filling in the gaps between old floorboards with contrasting tinted epoxy and leaving levels ever so askew. “This house shows signs of age and that’s not a bad thing,” says Schori. Adds Mehta, “As artists, they understood what is and isn’t precious so that the home does not feel overly restored.”

The backyard was updated by Brook Landscape, with a shed and swing set by GRT Architects.

In the son’s room, shaker-style cabinetry reveals a reading nook.

A refurbished tub from The Demolition Depot anchors the primary bath, which is lined in tiles by Balineum (on walls) and Artistic Tile (on floor).

Brown was nothing if not a gleeful problem solver, presenting flexible solutions to the needs of everyday life, often through inventive secondary spaces. (“Your rumpus room can be anything you like” proclaimed one Armstong campaign.) True to that strategy, GRT devised a floor plan full of versatile surprises. Adjoining the dining room is a cave-like lounge for watching television or just relaxing, with a pass-through to the kitchen for the easy arrival of snacks. Opposite the cookspace—more on that showstopper later—is a cozy breakfast area, with a load-bearing skylight that offers glimpses of the roof terrace (and sometimes curious children) above. And on the second level, a secret passage leads from the primary suite into the library, one column of shelving concealing a door. “All these appendages to the main spaces allow multiple things to happen at once,” says Schori.

In the daughter’s room, Shaker-style trim is painted in Farrow & Ball’s Blue Ground.

The kids’ bath mixes terrazzo walls with custom floor tiles by Haand.

Oak millwork incorporates vintage Piero Fornasetti panels in the dining room, where Guillerme et Chambron chairs surround an antique farm table; painting by Jo Nigoghossian, and custom fireplace fabricated by Daniel Armitano.

Surfaces and furnishings tend toward the eclectic and one-of-a-kind, with a mix of wallpapers and fabrics, some deadstock, setting the stage for vintage treasures. In the living room, a Vladimir Kagan sectional sofa found at auction doubles as a jungle gym of sorts. The primary bath is anchored by a salvaged earthenware tub, since glazed a light blue to complement the retro checkerboard tiles and scalloped vanity. And the dining room’s custom millwork incorporates a series of midcentury Fornasetti panels. Doorknobs, lighting, motifs, art by their circle of creative friends—it’s all a mishmash. “We liked things independently of how they worked together,” says the wife. Adds August: “It was so fun to stitch all these elements into an old house—I love a space where you come upon something unexpected.”

A detail of the kitchen’s terrazzo floor shows stone and metal shapes (some abstract, some legible) inspired by detritus.

A custom sofa frames the lounge off the dining room, which has a pass-through to the kitchen; carpet by Stark.

In the living room, a Gretta Johnson painting hangs above the fireplace, which is flanked by artworks by Cletus Johnson (left) and Ted Mineo (near right) and Miles Huston (Far right).

The husband’s office/music room is cocooned in cork; painting (at left, bottom) by MacGregor Harp.

The stairwell’s painted checkerboard floor hints at classic Americana.

Floor to floor, the mood shifts. The son’s and daughter’s rooms each riff on classic Shaker woodwork, with rails and built-in cabinetry that respond to the exposed ceiling joists. Enlivened by a splatter-painted floor and celestial configuration of ceiling lights, the plywood-lined attic operates as a kids’ club, craft room, and family theater in one. (How’s that for a rumpus room, Hazel?) Thanks to some clever excavation and engineering on the part of GRT, the combined garden level and cellar is now a double-height studio for the wife, complete with a gantry crane for hoisting heavy sculptures. Outside, meanwhile, Brook Landscape transformed the backyard into a romantic swath of greenery.

Vintage globe lights from a one-time optometry office add a surreal touch to the powder room.

Loose arrangements of greenery frame the steps to the backyard.

GRT Architects combined the garden level and cellar into a double-height art studio with a register of exposed brick.

A vintage Vilhelm Lauritzen ceiling light punctuates the primary bedroom; painting by Margaux Ogden.

As for that kitchen, here the team took inspiration not only from Brown but from two additional touchstones. Cabinet fronts nod to art glass at the Upper East Side’s late great Glaser’s Bake Shop, the salvaged interiors of which August and the wife chanced upon at The Demolition Depot. The floor, meanwhile, riffs on ancient Roman mosaics of unswept detritus, an example of which can be found at the Vatican Museums. “We wanted a kitchen that wouldn’t look bad if it got messy,” says the wife. Working closely with GRT and August, she and her husband evolved that historic precedent into a contemporary series of shapes, some abstract and some legible (a knife, a carrot, a wine glass). Rendered in scraps of stone or metal, their forms now appear to float in poured terrazzo.

“The way artists think about objects and architecture is as a constant work in progress,” says Mehta. “These are visual people with an incredible mental library of references.” Would Brown approve? Without a doubt. As the designer once wrote: “Whatever you do, use your own ideas. Don’t be afraid of them.”

#4805. 37 Best Housewarming Gifts to Get You Welcomed Back (2026)

Yes, the best housewarming gifts should ideally be useful, but that doesn’t mean you should completely abandon elements of charm and whimsy when you’re selecting the perfect item. Whether you’re bestowing something special upon a new homeowner or a pal settling into a new apartment, a vibe-enhancing and aesthetically pleasing present goes a long way to add warmth and personality to any new space.

With that in mind, we’ve rounded up the best housewarming gifts—many of which have blessed our own spaces—at various price points. You’ll find practical presents, such as throw blankets and kitchen tools; home decor items, like chic glassware; and a few indulgences, like caviar. Conveniently, several options also work as hostess gifts.

Here are some of our absolute favorite housewarming gift ideas for kicking off a whole new era of feeling at home, in an elevated way.

This lightweight set of borosilicate glasses has a playful raised-spot pattern. They’re blessedly dishwasher-safe, which matters a lot for glasses in daily use.

This high-quality reprint of a 1902 guest book is shopping writer Julia Harrison’s go-to housewarming gift. The book instructs guests to draw a pig rather than the more traditional practice of writing a note. This is “for most guests, a great relief,” says Harrison.

It’s not often that we come across a toolbox that is actually attractive. This model from Japanese brand Toyo sure is, and it’s just the right size for essentials, like screwdrivers, a level, and a measuring tape. It’s sure to get used by the end of the first week in every new home.

Kitchen towels get used harder than almost anything else in a new home. These are heavy cotton terry made in Portugal, with a jacquard pattern on one side and loops on the other. They’re thick enough to stand in for a trivet on many surfaces, which most kitchen towels can’t claim.

Gifting someone beautiful custom stationery is a thoughtful and useful way to make their new chapter that much more official. It’s one of the easier housewarming gifts to personalize, and you’re sure to smile when you receive a handwritten thank-you note.

This isn’t your grandpa’s soap on a rope. Ash’s revamp involves a spicy yet floral scent and a pop of color in its elegant red string.

For those who prefer to sip their spirits neat, these hand-blown mezcal glasses are a vibrant way to toast to the new digs.

Help the amateur chef in your friend group get a head start on their new home’s kitchen essentials. #4802. Musk admits Tesla doesn’t have enough talent to build more than one car at a time after exodus

Elon Musk now says Tesla can’t build the Cybertruck and the Cybercab at the same time because there are only so many “smart, dedicated people” to go around. Half the executives he showed off in 2023 as proof of Tesla’s “bench strength” have since left the company.

It’s the most honest explanation yet for how Tesla went from five consumer vehicle programs at the start of 2026 to a single platform, Model 3 and Model Y, by the middle of 2027.

On Friday, Musk said Tesla would wind down Cybertruck production at Giga Texas around the middle of next year “to make room for increased Cybercab production.” As we pointed out at the time, Giga Texas isn’t short on space. The Cybertruck line runs at a fraction of its installed capacity because the truck sells a fraction of what Tesla planned.

A day later, replying to a user on X, Musk gave a different reason:

“The biggest thing most people fail to understand is that there are a very limited number of smart, dedicated people! They can only do so many things.”

He added that if Tesla has to “maintain a complex production line, supply chain and allocate engineering for Cybertruck, we can’t use those people” for scaling Cybercab.

By this morning, the explanation had shifted again, to the “opportunity cost of the complexity of Cybertruck.” But the Saturday version is the one that matters. Musk is telling us the bottleneck at Tesla isn’t factory space or money. It’s people.

The Cybertruck is the second casualty this year. In January, Musk announced the end of the Model S and Model X on Tesla’s Q4 earnings call and summed up the reason in one word: “Autonomy.” The Fremont line is being converted to build Optimus robots, and by April, only inventory was left.

The flagships had been on life support for years. Their last real update was the 2021 interior refresh. The June 2025 “refresh” brought a new paint color, a front bumper camera, a bit more range, and a $5,000 price increase. That’s not how you treat a vehicle program you care about.

Tesla painted a very different picture just two years ago. At its 2024 shareholder meeting, it showed this slide of its lineup: the Model 3, Model Y, Model S, Model X, Cybertruck, and Semi, plus three vehicles under covers that Musk said were “going to be pretty special.”

Of the five consumer vehicles on that slide, three are now dead or on their way out.

So here’s what will be left in the second half of 2027: Model 3 and Model Y, two vehicles built on the same platform, plus the Model Y L and cheaper “Standard” variants of both. The Semi is a commercial truck. The Roadster’s production is still a giant question mark. And the Cybercab is a robotaxi with no steering wheel, not a car you can buy – even though Tesla is going to let fleet buyers get them to add on its own Robotaxi network. We previously explained how this is a terrible idea.

For comparison, Hyundai Motor Group sells more than half a dozen EV models in the US across its Hyundai, Kia, and Genesis brands, on top of its gas and hybrid lineup. GM and Toyota run dozens of nameplates at once. Tesla can’t keep more than two programs alive at the same time.

At Tesla’s Investor Day at Giga Texas on March 1, 2023, Musk mostly stayed out of the spotlight. Instead, Tesla paraded more than a dozen executives on stage to present its plans, a rare move for a company long criticized for being a one-man show.

“We’ve obviously got significant bench strength here,” Musk said at the time.

Of the 14 executives named as presenters that day, seven are gone:

Martin Viecha, who ran investor relations through that event, left in April 2024 as well.

Seven are still at Tesla, according to their LinkedIn profiles: Tom Zhu, Lars Moravy, Franz von Holzhausen, Ashok Elluswamy, Karn Budhiraj, Laurie Shelby, and Brandon Ehrhart. And the ones who left weren’t peripheral. They ran batteries, powertrain, software, chips, energy, charging, and the money.

The bigger problem for Tesla’s ability to run vehicle programs is the program-level talent, the people who take a car from prototype to volume production.

In November 2025, Tesla lost the head of its Cybertruck program, Siddhant Awasthi, and the Model Y program manager, Emmanuel Lamacchia, within a day of each other. Lamacchia had led Tesla’s best-selling vehicle for four years.

Then the Cybercab, the program Musk is now pulling people toward, started losing its own leaders:

Add 17-year finance VP Sendil Palani and Thomas Dmytryk, the director who built Tesla’s over-the-air update system and Robotaxi infrastructure, both in March, and you get the picture.

So when Musk says he needs Cybertruck’s people for Cybercab, part of the reason is that Cybercab keeps losing its own.

Musk will frame all of this as focus. Autonomy is going to be so big, the argument goes, that Tesla has to go all in.

But we’ve heard that before. Tesla made the yoke steering wheel standard on the Model S and Model X in 2021 because the cars would soon drive themselves, then brought back a round wheel as an option. It removed the turn signal stalk from the Model 3 in 2023, then brought the stalk back in 2025 and started selling a $350 retrofit to owners. Each time, the autonomy that justified the change wasn’t there yet.

And Tesla isn’t the leader in unsupervised driving. It has the best driver-assist system on the market, but Waymo still logs roughly seven times more driverless miles per week, and NHTSA estimates Tesla’s entire Robotaxi fleet at about 1,000 vehicles. Zoox is scaling too, and Baidu’s Apollo Go runs a far bigger driverless fleet in China.

This is something that I’ve been highlighting for a decade. Even after it became a big company with a large headcount, Tesla has never been good at running several vehicle programs in a healthy way. The Model S and Model X were starved for updates long before the talent exodus started. Tesla has always thrown everything at its newest program and let the older ones coast.

What changed is that the slack is gone. In 2023, Tesla could still neglect the flagships and keep them on the market, because it had deep benches in engineering, manufacturing, and software to absorb mistakes. Today, half the executives Musk called his bench are gone, and the people running the Cybercab ramp keep walking out. Musk is now openly telling us there aren’t enough people left to build a pickup truck and a robotaxi at the same time.

Musk calls it focus. It looks a lot more like a constraint Tesla can’t hire its way out of.

Tesla never had the capacity to hire, organize, manage and retain the amount of human talent required to develop multiple car model lines in concert. Tesla barely paid its staff enough for one model line development.

You only need to look at other companies with decades of experience in developing many, separate (but technically strongly overlapping) model lines to see how much does that require in terms of human resources and in terms of R&D cost. Like BMW has always been investing ~5 billion EUR every year in R&D. That is how you have ~14 core models with at least another dozen specialist models and more regional models plus Mini and RR on top. At times up to 60 different significant variants with different drivetrains also impacting design, logistics and production. All being always in development for the next generation and also for a mid-life refresh or two. Across the whole group, including maintenance work, there can easily be triple-digit number of vehicle model projects going at the same time.

Will the Cybercab be revolutionary and make it all worth it? We have our doubts. Tesla leads in driver assistance with FSD, but it has failed to evolve the product into unsupervised self-driving at scale, which it promised to buyers for a decade. It’s clearly not the leader in unsupervised self-driving, and the competition is crowded. Tesla’s lasting impact on the robotaxi market is genuinely hard to predict.

What’s not hard to see is the trend. Tesla has shown a complete inability to sustain multiple vehicle programs at once, and it’s getting harder for the automaker, not easier. If Tesla can’t hold on to the people who build its cars, how long before the Model 3 and Model Y get the same treatment as the Model S?

Both vehicle programs will undoubtedly go down in history as some of the most critical EVs of all time, but that doesn’t guarantee lasting success in a rapidly changing auto landscape.

If you’re driving an EV, the people building it matter, but so does where your electricity comes from. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4800. Ford’s EV batteries retain more than 90% health after 100,000 miles

Your electric vehicle battery may last longer than you think. Ford says its EV batteries retain greater than 90% health on average after 100,000 miles. Here are a few tips to keep yours healthy.

The Mustang Mach-E went on sale in late 2020 as Ford’s first fully electric crossover SUV designed from the ground up.

Since launching the 2021 Mustang Mach-E, Ford has been collecting data from over 36,000 vehicles that have traveled two billion miles across the US and Canada.

According to Ford, the Mach-E’s battery has retained 92% of its health on average over the past five years.

Across its full EV lineup, including the F-150 Lightning and E-Transit, Ford said its battery packs maintain more than 90% health on average after 100,000 miles. The key number to look at is the spread, with 95% of vehicles falling between 87% and 97% health.

Of 2021 model-year EV owners, Ford said, more than 2,000 have crossed 100,000 miles with their original battery averaging better than 91% health, while over 30 have crossed the 200,000-mile mark and average 88% health.

Ford is using real-world data to help owners take good care of their electric vehicles, no matter how much they know about batteries and other EV tech.

It’s also applying lessons from developing the Universal Electric Vehicle (UEV) platform, which will underpin its next-generation EVs, starting with the Fantom pickup next year.

Ford offered a few simple, everyday tips to help owners support long-term battery health. For one, if possible, charge at home on Level 2 for daily commuting or driving and save DC fast charging for longer trips.

It also helps to avoid charging in extreme temperatures if you can. Preconditioning the battery can help bring it into the optimal temperature range, while parking in the shade on hot summer days can also help avoid long-term battery degradation.

Last, but not least, you can use the Ford App to find charge-level recommendations designed specifically for your vehicle and battery system.

The 2021 Mustang Mach-E uses an NCM battery, but some newer Ford batteries use a lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery chemistry, which is recommended to charge to 100% from time to time.

Ford has introduced new features in the Ford App to help owners maintain battery health specific to their vehicle and will continue to use the data to improve the experience as it introduces its next-gen EVs.

This is an article about Ford EV's, so my comment is kind of relevant.

Recently I picked up a 2017 Ford Focus Electric for my daughter to use when she gets her permit soon. It had 91,000 miles when I got it and now has 92,400 miles. I like driving it enough that I've been driving it and letting my Equinox stay home.

Now, I have no idea how this car has been used or charged since new (does have the DCFC since it's a 2017). And since it's range was 115 miles factory rated, I have no idea if it's been charged to 100% most or all of the time. What I can tell you is that when I charge it to 100% now, it's giving me about 120 miles on average and I've seen as high as 134 miles. If I turn the A/C on, those numbers drop to 110 on average. Since I live in a mild climate and use the A/C sparingly, my daily commute with mixed driving is giving me a real life 120 to 125 miles.

Not bad for a nearly 10 years old compliance EV!!

Ford will open pre-orders for the Fathom, a midsize electric pickup and first UEV-based model, starting at $28,350 in early 2027.

Meanwhile, the 2027 Ford Mustang Mach-E is now on sale, starting at $39,995. The 2027 Mach-E drops the standard-range battery offered in the 2026 model and now comes standard with the larger 88 kWh battery with an EPA-estimated range of up to 320 miles (RWD).

#4795. Danielle Frankel Reimagines the 21st Century Bridal Store

After nearly 10 years in business, fashion designer Danielle Frankel Hirsch has opened a 12,000-square-foot location in Tribeca with interiors set to change the bridal game. To create the new space, which opened in early November, Frankel Hirsch worked with AD PRO Directory designer Augusta Hoffman and Studio Partners Architecture.

The nearly two-year design process is complete, and the Danielle Frankel bridal salon is now ready for appointments. “We’re finally at the point where my heart and my head have been,” says Frankel Hirsch, who worked with Hoffman on her previous Los Angeles boutique and New York City atelier. “Because this is our third space together, we can almost speak in shorthand now.”

Frankel Hirsch on the staircase wearing a shirt she designed from Fortuny fabric. Hoffman used the same Fortuny print throughout the space for wrapping side tables, keepsake boxes on the shelves, as well as for the woven seats on barstools.

The unassuming entrance to Danielle Frankel on Hudson Street

In the reception room just inside the street entrance, the desk is custom, the chairs are antique, and the custom pendant is by Jamb.

Hoffman worked with the artist Miriam Ellner to create eglomise paneling surrounding the staircase. “The eglomise is one of my favorite things because when we were working with the artist we worked so intimately with her,” Frankel Hirsch says. “We worked on the placement the way that we do our laces. We kept going back and forth with the drawings because I wanted it to feel like our lace placement.”

On a recent morning, a bride and her wedding party were busy in the VIP suite making a final selection. The beaming group entered the space through an unassuming brown storefront entrance on Harrison Street, its two large glass windows encased in a sheer linen. From there they ascended a staircase up to a large gallery with over 30-foot floor-to-ceiling windows overlooking the streets below on two sides, and a hand-painted mural pattern above on the ceiling.

The interiors of the original 19th-century Queen Anne–style red brick building were transformed by Hoffman (full disclosure: I am writing Hoffman’s design monograph publishing with Rizzoli in Fall 2027) into a modern, elegant bridal boutique that honors the history of the original New York Mercantile Exchange, first established in the 1880s to keep the standards of the grocer business. “We wanted the space to stay pretty bright and focus on the architecture, but we needed a lot of seating,” Hoffman says. “There was a lot of pressure because we wanted it to feel like Danielle and we wanted to leave our mark on the space, but it’s also so beautiful as is and we didn’t want to have too many interventions that take away from that.”

The rug, vintage Dutch leather armchairs, and custom clothing racks create one of the four seated zones in the gallery area. The wall features antique mottled glass windows. “We wanted this wall to feel historically accurate,” Hoffman says.

The custom oak screen features shelves for displaying miniature gowns by Danielle Frankel. The shelves are lined in silver leaf. The antique stool is reupholstered in a Fortuny fabric.

The view of the gallery area from a second floor bridal suite. The vintage teak seating set is reupholstered in a fabric by Manuel Canovas and the glass cocktail tables and Fortuny wrapped side tables are custom. The custom window panels are by Augusta Hoffman Studio for Wovn Home and the custom curtain hardware are bronze leaves designed by Hoffman. Hoffman pulled the leaf design from the details on the columns.

What previously functioned as a large, open space with a small mezzanine, had little existing infrastructure–no electrical, HVAC, or plumbing—and is now an area where brides can plot as they make the most important decision for their wedding day. In the gallery, Hoffman created distinct zones where wedding parties wait before their appointment in a private fitting room.

Much of the furniture was sourced by Hoffman and Frankel Hirsch together on trips to Paris. Antique glass vitrines separate the space, which they included from the previous New York gallery and filled with Frankel Hirsch’s own family wedding photos as well as printed details brides can use for their fitting. One vitrine has a board layered with references of crinoline and interlining. Another has custom Danielle Frankel cufflinks. An oak folding screen lines one half of the room, with miniature gowns set on shelves lined in silver leaf paper. Custom parchment and cerused oak racks Hoffman designed to withstand the weight of a wedding dress line the floor displaying roughly 65 dresses from Danielle Frankel collections.

The reflection of the wall is seen in the mirrored structure designed to house the elevator bank and stairwell to the street entrance. A framed antique floral plaster relief is flanked with 1930s Italian wall sconces in patinated glass and metal.

The staircase and elevator bank within the mirrored structure. “We had to add the elevator for function and for code, and we were like ‘How do we take this existing architecture and put an elevator in the middle of the room?’” Hoffman says. “It feels super unexpected, versus trying to hide it, and you get to see more of the surrounding architecture and light in the reflection.”

The custom rug is topped with an antique stool upholstered in a Fortuny fabric, a 1950s chair, and a 19th-century English pine console table.

The view from the sitting area adjacent to a bar. The vintage teak seating set was among the first items Hoffman and Frankel Hirsch sourced for the space. The custom bar stool is woven with a Fortuny fabric.

The view from within the staircase to the gallery area. The custom center table is by Rafael Carneiro Triboli and the French chairs are from the 1930s.

Hoffman and Studio Partners Architects took what was formerly the mezzanine, originally separated from the below with only a single-bar metal railing, and made it into a full wall with antiqued mottled glass windows. In the center they constructed a cascading grand staircase, cleverly concealing the stockroom underneath, which leads upstairs to three private bridal suites. The hallways, painted in Wenge by Benjamin Moore, lead to the bridal suites, each designed differently and filled with antique and vintage furniture, custom carpets, lighting, and racks. The walls are painted in Presidente Roman Clay by Portola Paints, an earthy contrast to the white and ivory dresses, which are naturally the center of attention for visitors in each room.

When guests are finished in their suites, there’s a bar in the gallery space where wedding parties can sit and make the final selection. The wedding party that morning had selected the dress and were toasting champagne to celebrate. “It’s just as much about the hospitality and the warmth as it is about the gowns,” Frankel Hirsch says.

In the VIP bridal suite, which can be accessed through a discreet entrance on Harrison Street, the custom sofa is upholstered in Holland & Sherry’s Corbel in Henna. The artwork features fragments of 19th century Japanese brocade.

The midcentury armchairs are upholstered in a Watts 1874 fabric and flank a vintage oak table.

The rack, mirror, and ceiling pendant are all custom in the VIP bridal suite.

In the dressing area of the VIP bridal suite, the ceiling pendant is custom and the closet wall covering is by Watts 1874.

The 1930s lounge chair is reupholstered in a Fortuny fabric and the 1970s lamp is from Brazil.

#4788. ‘Dune: Part Three’: An Exclusive First Look at the Sets

Nothing about the cinematic language of Dune is small—director Denis Villeneuve’s film trilogy does span multiple planets, after all. But throughout his work on the movies, including the upcoming Dune: Part Three, production designer Patrice Vermette has made sure to stay focused on the little details.

“My approach has always been with symbolism and semiotics,” Vermette tells AD. “It is my philosophy of design, because for Denis, empty design cannot exist. Things need to have a purpose. Need to have a signification.”

In conversation with Vermette about the sci-fi epic, examples of that core tenet come up often. Carefully designed details imbue hidden meaning in each set, creating a captivating, immersive on-screen world that earned the designer an Oscar for his work in the first Dune. The trilogy’s latest installment, Dune: Part Three, which lands in theaters on December 18, expands on that ethos. And for the final film in the series, the sheer scale of the sets was larger. “It was our last chance working in that world,” Vermette says. “It’s like in a fireworks [display], it’s the end, it’s the bouquet final, it’s like, ‘alright, let’s do it!’”

Robert Pattinson as Scytale, with director Denis Villeneuve.

Over 450,000 square feet of interior stages and 475,000 square feet of backlots were used for scenes shot in Budapest alone, with each set typically taking 14 to 16 weeks to build. (Filming also took place in Abu Dhabi and Iceland.) Even the sandworms that slither through the titular dunes got grander this time around: Instead of the relatively small platforms that were used to film worm-riding sequences as in the previous films, the production team built a 300-foot-long by 65-foot-wide sandworm in the middle of the desert.

Like in Dune: Part Two, Vermette was challenged to create entirely new sets for this installment, which adapts Frank Herbert’s 1969 novel Dune Messiah. One of the new settings viewers can expect is Wallach IX, the home of the powerful and secretive Bene Gesserit sisterhood. Throughout their planet, Vermette incorporated stone etchings that resemble human cells seen through a microscope, referencing the Bene Gesserit’s breeding program that has gone on for millennia. Mother nature also informed the architecture. “When you arrive on the planet, the buildings are basically eggs of a spider, like crops of spider eggs as you find them in nature,” Vermette explains. “Ecology has always been a huge source of inspiration for me, and also for the storytelling it gives—you know, they’ve got their web across the universe, for the genealogy they create.”

Another new setting is the Veteran’s District, where the Fremen, the native inhabitants of the desert planet Arrakis, retire after fighting in the catastrophic holy war spurred by Paul Atreides, aka Muad’Dib (Timothée Chalamet). In the previous films, the Fremen lived in communal residences lined with hand-carved stone that recorded their history and culture. Returning from the battlefield, they find mass-produced housing units instead of the “green paradise” they were promised would await them. Made of ceramic instead of rock, with stamped-on rather than hand-carved markings, the Veteran’s District represents an artificial facsimile of their former homes. “And when you put all these units together, and you look at the rooftops, it’s there to represent sand dunes,” Vermette explains. “All these triangles align [to look like dunes], but it’s fake.”

Another circular doorway represents an all-seeing eye near the lush Imperial Gardens, which illustrate Paul’s extreme wealth and control of resources—including water—on the dry planet of Arrakis.

There is a green paradise on Arrakis, just not for the Fremen. Lush gardens grace the planet’s Imperial Palace, a massive inverted pyramid with a large circle cut out of it—a reimagined version of the all-seeing eye. “I got inspired by the title of the book, Messiah, and from the fact that Denis, just like Frank Herbert, wanted to make sure that Paul Atreides was not going to be seen as a hero, and it’s a tale of being cautious about messianic figures,” says Vermette. Similar representations of the all-seeing eye are found throughout the palace, sinisterly implying that Muad’Dib is always watching.

In actuality, Muad’Dib’s personal quarters are completely isolated from the rest of the palace. His wife, Irulan (Florence Pugh), must find her way through a twisting mass of hallways to reach him. “Basically, she’s walking into the psyche of Paul,” Vermette explains. “She goes into a void, and then it’s all these mazes of one’s mind.” When we get to the end of the maze, we see that Paul’s inner sanctum resembles a humble desert camp. Not unlike the Fremen, his dwelling has been reduced to an echo of what once was. He is “living as a recluse because he doesn’t trust anybody,” Vermette explains. “He wants to go back to a place where he felt the most comfortable, which was the desert. So he recreated that space.”

“Denis really wanted that set to be the golden cage of Irulan,” Vermette says of these private chambers. “She’s married to the emperor, but she’s not free at all, she’s a prisoner.” The window is shaped similar to an infinity symbol, but is covered in metal screens. “It’s like eternity, but eternity is blocked. It’s like a cage.”

The entrance to the maze within Muad’Dib’s inner sanctum. Vermette designed 40 maze wall panels that needed to be moved into various configurations to shoot the scene, which was filmed in less than a day. Eight people were each assigned a portion of the 12-foot-high panels and learned a choreography of sorts, quickly moving into each configuration until they could do each one in five minutes or less. “We rehearsed and rehearsed, just like dancers or musicians; they knew exactly where to fit their pieces” within the roughly 14,400-square-foot space, says Vermette.

On the planet Wallach IX, the office of Bene Gesserit Reverend Mother Mohaim features stone carvings inspired by human cells under a microscope.

The Imperial Palace throne room took 20 weeks to build. Behind Muad’Dib’s emerald throne, the windows are shielded with metal cast screens inspired by Montreal’s Saint-Ambrose Church, which was built in 1925 by architect Ernest Cormier.

The all-seeing eye is a repeating symbol Vermette inserted throughout the Imperial Palace, including a representation seen here as the circular entrance of Paul’s Keep.

The architecture of this Imperial Palace corridor has slanted walls to reflect a pyramid-shaped exterior and flying buttress support structures inspired by Gothic-style cathedrals.

#4785. The electric Vespa just got better where it actually matters

Vespa’s electric scooters have never exactly screamed for attention. That’s sort of the point. They look like Vespas, ride like Vespas, and just happen to swap the gasoline engine for a much quieter electric drivetrain.

But for 2027, Vespa has given its electric lineup a fairly significant technological overhaul while leaving that iconic steel bodywork largely untouched.

Unveiled at EICMA 2026 in Milan, the new electric Vespa lineup now consists of the Primavera Elettrica, Sprint S Elettrica, and the faster Primavera Tech Elettrica. And while you’d be forgiven for struggling to spot the differences from across the show floor, there’s a lot more happening underneath.

The biggest upgrade is an entirely new electric motor developed and manufactured in Italy by Vespa parent company Piaggio Group. The motor uses a new modular design intended to improve both performance and efficiency, while also doing away with rare-earth materials.

On the 45 km/h (28 mph) Primavera and Sprint S models, Vespa says the new motor provides stronger acceleration for stop-and-go city riding. Range reaches up to 85 km (53 miles) in Eco mode.

But perhaps the biggest practical improvement is the new removable battery. Being able to pull the battery out of the scooter and carry it inside for charging makes a massive difference for urban riders who don’t have access to a garage or dedicated charging spot – exactly the kind of riders Vespa is targeting.

The Primavera Tech is where things get a little faster. This version is homologated as a motorcycle rather than a moped in Europe and reaches 67 km/h (42 mph). That’s still not terribly fast, but the electric Vespa line has never been known for its top speed.

Its range has increased by 12.5% thanks to the new motor and Vehicle Management System, reaching 90 km (56 miles) on the WMTC cycle or up to 100 km (62 miles) in Eco mode.

There’s also a new charger that Vespa says reduces energy consumption from the wall by 17%, with a 0-80% charge taking around 2.5 hours. A new Maintenance mode is designed to protect the battery during longer periods when the scooter isn’t being ridden.

Vespa is clearly only really interested in selling their gas models, which come in 50cc, 125cc, & 310cc classes. And the only reason anyone would get a 50cc is if they didn't want to bother to get an actual motorcycle license in some jurisdictions. The electrics are nearly as expensive as the 310s but even the "faster" model only has the power equivalent of about 70cc's. At least the battery in the faster model is now a decent 4.2 kwh, though that one I believe is not removable. Hey I'm actually a Vespa lover I have a 200cc. They are beautiful quality machines, and I don't mind the price premium. But the electrics are quite disappointing, especially for the money. If they had a true 125cc equivalent I would be first in line at the showroom. Very disappointing in that a stouter motor really wouldn't cost them much more at all.

The Primavera Tech also gets the technological bells and whistles you’d expect from the flagship version, including a 5-inch TFT display, keyless operation, navigation through Vespa’s smartphone connectivity system, and remote seat unlocking.

Perhaps the most Vespa thing about the whole update is how little of it you can actually see.

The company hasn’t tried to turn its electric scooters into rolling science-fiction props. They’re still unmistakably Vespas, complete with the same classic styling that has defined the brand for generations. They just happen to have a much more interesting electric drivetrain hiding underneath now.

Of course, they also carry some very Vespa pricing. While we don’t have word on the updated pricing for the new models, the outgoing Primavera Elettrica 70 RED was priced at $8,299 in the US. So don’t expect these models to come cheap.

FTC: We use income earning auto affiliate links. More.

Micah Toll is a personal electric vehicle enthusiast, battery nerd, and author of the Amazon #1 bestselling books DIY Lithium Batteries, DIY Solar Power, The Ultimate DIY Ebike Guide and The Electric Bike Manifesto.

The e-bikes that make up Micah’s current daily drivers are the $999 Lectric XP 2.0, the $1,095 Ride1Up Roadster V2, the $1,199 Rad Power Bikes RadMission, and the $3,299 Priority Current. But it’s a pretty evolving list these days.

You can send Micah tips at Micah@electrek.co, or find him on Twitter, Instagram, or TikTok.

#4782. 41 Best Gifts for Women on All Special Occasions (2026)

The best gifts for the women in your life are probably right under your nose. Consider the unique facets of their personality, their hobbies, and what lights them up. Is it watching countless episodes of Barefoot Contessa, dreaming about an upcoming trip to the Poconos, sampling every kind of Sephora mini, or curating a high-design interior vignette? No matter the answer, a great present should match their energy to ensure they end up with something that reflects their essence and also shows you’ve been paying attention.

We’ve compiled a curated list of gift ideas tailored to all sorts of interests to minimize the guesswork for birthdays, Mother’s Day, the holiday-gifting scramble, and every momentous occasion in between. Our picks run the gamut—from home decor gems and high-tech gadgets to skin-care gift sets and culinary staples—but are still selective enough to feel special during the big unboxing reveal.

Silk pillowcases not only do wonders for your skin and hair, but they also make your bedscape feel more luxurious. Gifting her a set is a guaranteed upgrade to her nighttime routine.

A special pair of champagne flutes to mark special occasions and milestones will become a treasured fixture in her barware collection. This cheerful duo features bubbly glass adornments that will pair perfectly with a bottle of her favorite bubbly.

Receiving any Le Creuset cookware is a win for anyone who feels most at home in the kitchen. This Dutch oven is a treat that will become a staple for home chefs, who can use to make endless roasts, stocks, casseroles, and more. So as long as you’re invited for dinner, it’s a win for you, too.

Author Lydia Davis is best known for very short stories, some of them a single sentence long, but her essays are where the reader can see how her mind works. This first volume gathers five decades of writing on reading, translating, Proust, early tourist photographs, and the city of Arles. Complete the gift by dropping her off at a café, where she can read it while people-watching and underlining passages. Don’t forget to buy her favorite coffee.

No gift guide would be complete without a cozy blanket, and we’re making a bid on this mohair one. The colorblocked number is comfortable enough to drape over her shoulders whether she’s snoozing or curled up with a book.

A tablecloth is a smart gift idea for the singular reason that it’s not something you’d really ever buy for yourself. It’s delightfully multiuse: covering the many drink rings on your walnut table, performing as a park blanket in a pinch, and—most importantly—giving your favorite gal a sense of cohesive calmness as she sets her table.

Whether or not she has the dreamy silverware to match, Sabre’s serving set with trendy burgundy handles will feel like a little luxury for plating cheese boards and opening bottles. Elegant and slightly retro, they’re versatile enough to accompany both bold and understated tabletop decorations.

Paper napkins are fine for any regular evening, but a hostess deserves a beautiful set of cloth linens. Borgo delle Tovaglie, the Italian linen house Valentina Muggia took over from her mother, makes these in soft-washed pure linen. The red colorway is embroidered with an ivory floral motif that makes even takeout look planned.

“Few things are so favored in my home as this tabletop lighter,” says shopping writer Julia Harrison. “I got it on a whim, and then found myself using it routinely every night.” Perfect for incense, candles, burning cursed love notes, or enjoying a cigarette on the fire escape. Don’t forget the butane.

For the minimalist coffee lover who can’t resist a little chrome, Louise Roe has just the cup-and-saucer set to slide under the tree.

Her Guido cup and saucer bring the relaxed sense of a Southern Italian breakfast table to an ordinary weekday morning. The jade glaze is a staff favorite, and the set mixes with the line’s other colors if she wants to build a collection. Nota bene: It’s dishwasher-safe.

One thing about many a woman: She loves a pitcher. Not necessarily for pouring lemonade, but for the concept that she could, should the occasion present itself. These thick glass styles from Il Buco Vita double as great vases, too, making them a great hostess gift. They also hold plenty of water for her guests at one of her routine dinner parties.

If getting in a chapter or two of her current book du jour before lights out is a must, she’ll find a new best friend in this little task lamp. The head is magnetized, so you can direct light in pretty much any direction you choose. The sage shade has a notably relaxing quality.

No one has ever been charmed by a cardboard Kleenex box, which is what makes this such a satisfying find. Todd Nickey and Amy Kehoe designed the handmade lacquer tissue box with James Lowther of the Lacquer Company for a collection drawn from antiques they’ve collected over the years. The cover, made in Vietnam, slips over most standard tissue boxes. In khaki and chocolate, it will look at home on a nightstand or vanity long after cold season ends.

The year’s best photo deserves better than her camera roll. It’s small enough to be a stocking stuffer and sentimental enough to hold its own among the bigger Christmas gifts.

A pair of candleholders from design giant Herman Miller (of Eames fame) is among the most affordable ways to say: “I think you have great taste.” If you feel inclined, you could tuck in a couple of her favorite scented candles to complete the gift.

This text that was pivotal in contemporary color research will surely become a favorite in her own book collection. Both visually appealing and full of knowledge, it includes 348 color combinations design nerds (like us) can use in their next project.

#4774. 46 Best Gift Ideas for Men (2026): Presents That Won’t Disappoint

Guys can be disastrously tough to shop for, but the best gift ideas for men don’t have to involve a brainteaser. What do you buy for the dad with a fondness for practical things? Some elevated basics or tech essentials, not just another six-pack of tube socks, should do the trick. For the brother with a taste for the finer things? We’ve got some ideas of the luxury persuasion that don’t involve placing bids on Grailed or eBay. For the husband who claims to be OK with no gift at all (he’s lying), please resist the impulse to hit him with a gift card. There are several handsome layering options along with some cooking upgrades that will please even the man who struggles with paint-by-numbers meal kit recipes (ahem, a Japanese grater might change his life).

We’ve got a lot of covetable and cool gifts at every price point for Father’s Day, birthdays, and every ‘just because’ milestone in between. An actually stylish baseball cap is hard to come by, but this sophisticated-looking herringbone design from Todd Snyder won’t dress down his outfit.

If they’re never seen without their favorite timepiece (or second-favorite, or third-favorite) fastened around their wrist, they’ll love this handsome leather watch roll. An instant travel-set upgrade, it’s made of durable epi leather and a soft suede interior (no watch-face scratches here!). It also has enough room to tote around up to four favorite watches.

Made of ring-spun cotton with a seamless finish, these tees play well on their own or peeping out of his favorite button-down. He’ll love the versatility, which is a gift in itself.

This classic wax jacket can easily be dressed up or down, whether he’s headed to the office or cosplaying as an English gent on the weekends. Classic for a reason, Barbour has nailed the balance between “lightweight and breathable” and “durable enough to qualify as a family heirloom.”

On the topic of elevated loungewear, a pair of cashmere joggers are just the thing to finish off his surprisingly elegant weekend look. Just make sure you don’t forget the slippers.

If your favorite runner keeps misjudging the weather, make sure they’re prepared with an efficient, packable jacket. Lulu Lemon’s Fast and Free jacket features a cinching hood, zippered pockets, and folds into a hands-free belt bag to keep him moving unencumbered.

These Jimmy Fairly sunglasses are a bit choose-your-own-adventure. The modern frame shape makes even the most mundane outfits seem more interesting, and they come with both brown and grey-tinted lenses that can be switched out depending the sun levels you’re hoping to block (grey provides maximum protection while brown leans more minimal).

This Homme Plissé short sleeve from Issey Miyake puts a sculptural, Japanese twist on his favorite polo silhouette. While it plays well on its own, the full set will forever outshine any competing airport outfits.

Level up his sleepwear with Comme Si’s luxurious interpretation of flannel PJs. Just make sure he tosses those old gray sweatpants in return.

Socks are absolutely a basic Christmas gift, but no one likes being cold and these cashmere socks from Elder Statesmen are a step up from your ordinary pair.

From the same designer as his new favorite cap (see above), some grown-up wool and suede gloves will keep him frostbite-free through the winter chill.

#4766. Toyota sells 3 electric SUVs, but which one is best for you?

Toyota now offers three fully electric SUVs: the bZ, C-HR, and bZ Woodland. Here’s how they stack up.

After introducing the new and improved bZ (formerly the bZ4X) in late 2025, the electric SUV quickly became one of the most popular EVs in the US.

In the first nine months of 2026, it was the fourth-best-selling electric vehicle, behind the Hyundai IONIQ 5, Tesla Model 3, and Tesla Model Y.

With two additional electric SUVs now on sale, the CH-R and bZ Woodland, Toyota has suddenly become one of America’s top-selling EV brands.

Its EV sales have nearly tripled this year. According to Cox Automotive, Toyota ranked as the third most popular EV brand behind Tesla and Rivian in the third quarter.

Toyota offers three distinct electric vehicles: the 2027 bZ, C-HR, and bZ Woodland, each designed for different lifestyles, so which one is the best fit for you?

After launching the updated model last year, the 2027 bZ starts at $34,980. With the $1,595 delivery fee, the starting price is $36,576.

That’s for the standard 2027 Toyota bZ XLE FWD with an EPA-estimated driving range of up to 236 miles. For longer range, you can upgrade to the FWD Plus model, which offers up to 314 miles and starts at $37,980 (excluding the delivery fee).

The Toyota bZ is available in XLE and Limited grades, with front- or all-wheel-drive (AWD) powertrains.

Two battery options are offered: The 57.7 kWh battery on the standard XLE FWD provides an EPA-estimated range of up to 236 miles, while the larger 74.7 kWh battery provides up to 288 miles of range on the XLE AWD and 314 miles on the XLE FWD Plus model.

FWD models produce either 165 hp (XLE FWD) or 221 hp (XLE FWD Plus and Limited FWD), while AWD models deliver a combined 338 hp.

The 2027 bZ also has a built-in NACS port, so you can use Tesla Superchargers without needing a separate adapter. Toyota said it takes around 30 minutes to charge the battery from 10% to 80%.

Inside, the 2027 bZ features Toyota’s updated cabin design. The setup features a 7″ LCD customizable Multi Information Display (MID) and a new 14″ Toyota Audio Multimedia display screen with wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto.

The bZ is essentially designed as an everyday driver with a long driving range, modern tech, and available AWD.

For those looking for a bigger, more rugged option, the 2027 bZ Woodland may be a better fit. The Woodland is 6″ longer than the standard bZ and comes with standard AWD.

The longer body opens up more interior space, with up to 74.3 cubic feet of storage compared to the bZ with up to 67.1 cubic feet.

Unlike the bZ, the Woodland model has a standard AWD powertrain with Toyota’s X-Mode control system.

The setup delivers a combined 375 horsepower, 37 hp more than the standard bZ, making it Toyota’s most powerful EV currently.

It also adopts a more commanding, upright stance with 8.3″ of ground clearance and 3,500 lbs of towing capacity.

Although it uses the same 74.7 kWh battery as the standard bZ, the Woodland’s EPA-estimated range drops to 281 miles because of the added weight and power. Upgrading to the optional all-terrain tires sacrifices even more range, with up to 260 miles.

Like the bZ, the Woodland also has a built- in NACS port. Charging the battery from 10% to 80% takes around 30 minutes.

Inside, the bZ Woodland features the same 7″ LCD customizable Multi Information Display (MID) and 14″ Toyota Audio Multimedia display screen with wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto.

The 2027 Toyota bZ Woodland is available in Base and Premium trims, starting at $45,380. All variants include standard roof rails, Dual Qi-capable wireless device chargers, heated front seats and steering wheel, and Digital Key capabilities.

Last, but not least, the C-HR is a smaller, sporty, coupe-like electric SUV. The 2027 Toyota C-HR starts at $37,080, or $2,100 more than the standard bZ.

It’s about 6.7″ shorter than the bZ, with a smaller wheelbase, but also comes with standard AWD. The dual-motor AWD setup delivers a combined 338 hp, not quite as much as the bigger, more powerful Woodland.

The C-HR is available exclusively with the 74.7 kWh battery, which provides an EPA-estimated range of up to 287 miles for the SE grade with 18″ wheels.

Upgrading to 20″ wheels adds a street-ready look but offers slightly less EPA range, with up to 273 miles.

The coupe-like design adds a sleek look and opens up interior space, with up to 59.5 cubic feet of rear cargo space when the rear seats are folded. That’s slightly more than the bZ, which offers up to 56.9 cubic feet.

Inside, the C-HR features the same setup as its other EVs, including a 7″ slim driver’s display and 14″ central infotainment screen.

Like Toyota’s other electric SUVs, the C-HR has a native NACS port. Charging the battery from 10% to 80% takes about 30 minutes.

These are all basically the same small vehicles. I did not fit in any of them (6’ 2”) it is so frustrating to have less than 300 miles of range on AWD versions that are supposed to be better models.

It is 2026 over a decade since EVs arrived and over 5 years after the Tesla Model Y with practically zero competition almost at same price (Rivian R2 may be next year when affordable versions ship.) BMW, Mercedes and Volvo need $10k more. Hyundai has kept EV5 out of USA.

It is almost like none of the Legacy automakers want any SUV that can compete on price.

The 2027 Toyota C-HR EV is available in two trims: SE and XSE. Standard features include two wireless Qi phone chargers, low-profile roof rails, Fabric/SofTex-trimmed seating, heated front seats and steering wheel, Toyota Safety Sense 3.0, and a power liftgate.

Which Toyota electric SUV would you choose? The bZ, C-HR, or bZ Woodland? Or would you choose another EV? Let us know your pick in the comments. Next year, Toyota will add the Highland EV, its first three-row electric SUV.

Thinking about making the switch to a Toyota EV? With the 2027 models at dealerships, now could be a good time to find a deal on a current model. You can use the links below to see what’s available at Toyota dealerships near you.
