#4765. AD’s Editor Picks: Gifts for Friends and Family Who Love Design

The holiday season is fast approaching, and we’ve rounded up our picks for the best gifts for friends and family with proclivities for great design—including the picky cousin who couldn’t decide on a sofa for months, your wellness-obsessed mother-in-law, the best friend who loves to host, and everyone in between. No matter what winter has in store, we’re committed to making sure you come out smelling as fresh as pine this gifting season. So to relieve some stress and cut out the guesswork, we’ve rounded up 29 AD editor-approved options that’ll show them how much they mean to you (or that might make a nice treat for yourself).

“Ever since I visited Milan this year, I have craved a cool way to serve up aperitivos stateside,” says senior shopping editor Nashia Baker. The Porta Natalia Criado Astrid Set—handcrafted in Italy—brings a creative edge to the senior editor’s cocktail hours,“all thanks to the geometric food bowls and sliding quartz stones that feel sophisticated yet playful.”

Shopping writer Julia Harrison “hemmed and hawed over these knives for two years” before finally pulling the trigger. She says there’s no other knife like it on the market. “Its craftsmanship speaks for itself, but the signature stripe handle does so much for an otherwise spiritless kitchen,” she says.

“Ninety percent of the meals I ate at home over the past year were eaten out of an Everyday Bowl,” admits commerce editor Audrey Lee. She likes that the design is a nice medium between a traditional bowl and a plate, and how the color options look stacked on her dinnerware shelf. “It’s also a gift that appeals to anyone who’s more picky about the plate than what goes on it.”

“Aesop is my answer to a foolproof gift for pretty much anyone,” says Lee, who’s given the brand’s sets to family members and friends both in and out of the holiday season. “And for anyone reading *wink, wink*, my personal favorite is any variant of the rind concentrate scent.”

This notepad is perfect for writing down any and all to-dos, to-buys, and to-remembers—exactly what Harrison intends for it. “This bedside notepad keeps me off my phone and away from blue light while I’m winding down,” she says. “It also makes my nightstand very literary-looking in the cold light of morning.”

Anyone wanting to buy a foolproof gift for commerce producer Lauren Arzbaecher should head to Anthropologie. “Call me basic, but I never tire of this glassware that regularly makes the rounds on TikTok,” she says. “The colored icons add a sense of personality to a daily drink—I’ve collected the croissant and lemon patterns so far.”

All your gift recipient will have to do is strike a match and light the wick to make them feel like they’re cruising down Highway 1. “Earthy and light, this Big Sur candle instantly transports me to the west coast with notes of fresh eucalyptus and magnolia,” says Baker.

Baker calls these sculptural martini glasses “the cherry on top” in her bar cabinet. The cheery pink-and-blue color palette transforms toasts into something more tasteful, and the little cherry on the stem is actually helpful for keeping your hand steady when raising the glass—“a true win if you’re clumsy like me,” says Baker.

“Certainly a splurge gift, though I think worth it for a piece of Lenny Kravitz’s design genius,” says Arzbaecher. This lamp from his recent CB2 collection takes all the airiness of a Noguchi paper lantern and adds a jolt of chrome and marble for something seriously sexy.

This tea library unfolds like a book, filled with green, black and oolong tea varieties, a warming gift for cozy nights in. “Every night my roommates and I exchange glances that mean: time for our nightly cup,” says Harrison. “We’ve yet to try one that we aren’t enamored with.”

Explore 1,000 of the world’s most thoughtfully designed objects with this eye-catching coffee table book from Phaidon. A great gift for any design enthusiast, this visually compelling read is filled with beautiful photography and interesting anecdotes about some of our favorite pieces from the Eamese, Alvar and Aino Aalto, Isamu Noguchi, and more.

“This Japanese-designed sewing machine strays away from utilitarian-looking models, so I don’t feel like I have to hide it away,” says Lee. The shopping editor also notices that the petite machine is more compact than previous ones she’s sewn with, but it’s still well-equipped to handle hemming her too-long skirts and other low-maintenance projects.

These espresso cups will be a bright spot in their caffeine routine. Each set is handmade by artist Florence Girod at her workshop in the south of France, so no two are the same.

We found the perfect dish to match her Van Cleef alhambra from Anastasio Home. It’s made of solid marble and is the perfect way to display her everyday jewelry.

This robe from Lake makes Harrison “feel like a hardworking mother of three living in a Pacific Northwest logging town, drinking black coffee all day long, and feeding my children baked beans”—a version of herself she’s aspiring to one day.

Pajamas discreet enough to be worn during sunlight hours will be the gift that keeps on giving. Harrison called this Olivia Von Halle set “impossibly high-quality.” While it’s most definitely a splurge, she insists they feel “like a million silk worms made it just for you.”

Mossy hues are an evergreen (pun intended) color scheme in Arzbaecher’s home. “This quilt adds tranquility to my bedscape. Plus, it’s just weighty enough to serve throughout multiple seasons.” It’s an ideal present for the friend that’s perpetually cold—or maybe even a nice winter bedding upgrade for yourself.

“These sheets feel as close to my aspirational vibe as an object can get—vintage, bohemian, heirloom quality,” says Harrison. “I want everything from this brand.” Made and embroidered in Jaipur using heritage techniques, it’ll feel like you’re being gifted an heirloom in the making.

Rest easy knowing that anyone who receives this breezy sheet set with cooling eucalyptus fibers will wake up on the right side of the bed. “The cool-to-the-touch nature of the material is ideal for hot sleepers, so I found the experience very nice,” tester Zoë Sessums told us.

“The Clementine Sleep mask’s pillowy soft material leaves me feeling refreshed by morning,” says Baker, so it’s a thoughtful gift for anyone who enjoys more luxurious sleep. She notes that it filters out light in her sunny bedroom “so sleeping in is the only thing on my mind.”

Ezcaray’s Matisse scarf feels like a fluffy, mini version of the wool throw blanket Baker curls up with on snow days. “I’ve survived too many NYC winters to underestimate the power of a good scarf,” she says. “This one is warm, beautifully designed, and the first thing I think about gifting friends who struggle when there’s a chill in the air.”

“I bumped into a friend who was modeling this sweater at the Eckhaus Latta x J.Crew party last fashion week and was instantly smitten,” says Lee. It’s made of 100% wool to stay cozy in the coldest winter temps, and the colorful palette makes it suit pretty much anything else in your wardrobe. “Be prepared to get stopped a million times from friends and strangers wanting to know where it’s from,” she warns.

These mules add print to a plain T-shirt and jeans outfit without overwhelming it. “Unlike other mules I’ve tried, these don’t seem to have the issue of flying off my foot with every step,” adds Arzbaecher. While AD’s producer is partial to the calf-hair version, there are also lots of suede colorways for a sleek alternative.

Lee reaches for this necklace-scarf hybrid from Kara Yoo at least once a week, since it can add flair to a basic sweater or looks sleek peeping out from a collared shirt. “The pendant can hang from different lengths, or you can loop the silk cord through just one side, so it feels like a different accessory every time,” she says.

The Petit Moyen Messenger from Clare V. has a permanent place in Lee’s packing list because of its versatile shape that can easily fit in overstuffed luggage. “Unlike some of my other bags, I don’t need to stuff my Moyen with my socks and T-shirts to keep its shape in my carry on,” she says. “It comes in many solid-colored options, but I’m partial to the two-tone checkerboard woven designs that have dimension.”

“Don’t sleep on Quince’s cashmere wearables,” says Lee. She told us this quarter zip looks like it could cost double its price tag—and who doesn’t like to get a little cashmere for Christmas? “The jewel tone-enthusiast in me is drawn to burgundy and storm blue, but truffle brown and ivory are great gift ideas for minimalists, and classic additions to any wardrobe,” she says.

Lee’s holiday wishlist also consists of silver or white gold charms that she can use to accessorize Mejuri’s chain necklace—a perfect gift in and of itself for jewelry lovers. “I was given this necklace earlier in the year and wear it multiple times a week. Since it’s easy to attach charms and pendants to the timeless silhouette, it feels like 100 necklaces in one.”

Lee can “hardly think of an occasion where a pair of these classic Pleats Please trousers by Issey Miyake aren’t appropriate.” She says they’re the perfect buy for the effortlessly cool, quiet luxury crowd since she’s worn them to formal events, to the office, and “on busy errand days with a T-shirt and sneakers—they’re that comfortable.”

With a unique open-ear silhouette, the nwm Ones challenge traditional headphone design using Sound Zone Technology that allows for complete auditory awareness—meaning your gift recipient can hear their own voice and surroundings while still listening to music. “These were hands-down my go-to headphones during the summer because they’re so breathable,” says Lee. She added that “it’s also really nice being able to hear your surroundings, especially if you live in a place like NYC.”

#4759. 15 Airbnbs With Indoor Pools & Jacuzzis (2026): Top Summer Escapes

Airbnbs with indoor pools (especially the heated kind) are the kinds of “needle in a haystack” rentals that can save a vacation from feeling completely miserable in the coldest months of the year, or heighten the excitement of the enclave you’ve secured for a week of summer bliss. And though many of your standard Airbnb rentals will offer guests some generous amenities—a plush bed, a functional kitchen, and a high-pressure shower immediately come to mind—only a very select few allow you to book a pool that’s all yours (not shared, or fought over, by a bunch of resort guests). Better yet: There’s no “pool closes at 9 p.m.” signs.

But here’s the catch: The gap between a great indoor-pool rental and a truly tragic one is vast. We’re talking sleek, glass-wrapped sanctuaries on one end—and, on the other, a sad, chlorinated afterthought hiding in a basement with fluorescent lighting and 1994 motel energy. The good ones, though, have the power to allow you to lock in your vacation and tune everything else out. From a cedar-lined pool room with views of the surrounding forest to modern desert escapes where you can watch the sunset turn the surrounding mountains pink at sunset from your floaty, these pools make for an incredible retreat year-round, weather be damned.

Perks: Turkish steam bath, sauna, massage room, private chef, butler service, private backyard, security system

If your ideal vacation involves frescoed ceilings, crystal chandeliers, and a butler to fetch you juice that’s been freshly squeezed from the garden's citrus trees, Villa Dorata in Positano delivers. This five-bedroom Baroque-style villa is gloriously over the top, with jewel-toned interiors, gilded art, elaborate tilework, and terraces overlooking the Amalfi Coast. The private spa is an attraction in its own right, complete with an indoor swimming pool, Turkish steam bath, and massage room. Outside, lush gardens, fountains, and citrus groves provide plenty of places to enjoy the Mediterranean sunshine. There are even two traditional Italian pizza ovens for alfresco dinners. Daily breakfast, housekeeping, and butler service are included, so guests can spend less time managing the logistics of a group vacation and more time enjoying the villa's considerable extravagance. From $5,078 per night.

Perks: Jetted bathtub, private backyard, fire pit, outdoor furniture, pet-friendly, security system, free parking

Swimming season never ends at this three-bedroom home outside Binghamton, New York. The heated indoor pool occupies its own glass-lined space, supplying views of the surrounding trees and simulating the feeling of being outdoors. The rest of the house features vaulted ceilings, bamboo floors, and a contemporary mix of furnishings. Floor-to-ceiling windows let in plenty of natural light into the main living areas, and an expansive deck extends the entertaining space outdoors. There's also a balcony for morning coffee, a propane fire pit for chilly evenings, and a jetted soaking tub in the primary suite. With room for eight guests and a pet-friendly policy, it's a practical choice for a group getaway, particularly when the weather makes an outdoor pool considerably less appealing. From $425 per night.

Perks: Soaking tub, sauna, two fireplaces, private deck

The Catskills have no shortage of cabins with fireplaces and woodland views, but few come with their own indoor swimming pool. This four-bedroom getaway near Callicoon has all three, plus an infrared sauna for the ultimate wellness-centric stay. Cathedral ceilings, exposed timber, and two wood-burning fireplaces give the interiors a classic mountain house look, while oversized windows keep the surrounding three acres of forest in view. Outside, a path leads down to Callicoon Creek, and a generously-sized deck and fire pit make the most of the secluded setting. The house sleeps 10, so there's room to bring a crowd without having to share a hotel pool with strangers. From $410 per night.

Perks: Hot tub, spa deck, sun patio, grilling area, gourmet kitchen

There are indoor pools, and then there are homes built entirely around them. This sprawling five-bedroom retreat centers on a private heated mineral pool and hot tub housed in a dedicated spa space, turning a weekend getaway into something closer to a wellness retreat. Elsewhere, the home feels equal parts time capsule and thoughtful renovation. Original details—including a vintage intercom system, wood-clad ceilings, and dramatic stonework—sit alongside updated bathrooms and an eclectic mix of vintage and contemporary furnishings. Thoughtful extras—from a grand piano and art supplies to a gourmet kitchen and eight smart TVs—make it particularly appealing for multigenerational gatherings. From $945 per night

Perks: Indoor fireplace, private patio, dedicated parking, game room, wine cellar

From the outside, this townhouse looks like a regular Denver-area home. Inside, however, it’s organized around one genuinely unexpected element: a full-size indoor pool set beneath a ceiling of skylights and surrounded by greenery. A fireplace just off the pool deck gives swimmers a place to warm up between dips (or flips off the diving board). The rest of the house is built for groups who plan to stay put. Exposed brick and wood-paneled walls give the interiors a grounded, slightly retro feel, while oversized bedrooms and bathrooms make it comfortable for longer stays rather than quick weekends. A wine cellar and wet bar sit near the pool room, clearly intended for nights that stretch on, and a separate game room—complete with a pool table and shuffleboard—adds another place to gather. From $726 per night.

Perks: BBQ, fire pit, heated indoor pool, washer and dryer

The Invisible House, with its mirror-clad façade, is a well-known landmark in its area of the Joshua Tree Desert. Designed by architect Tomas Osinski, the home quite literally reflects its surroundings while an open plan setup connects one end of the space to the other—encompassing a lengthy 100-foot indoor pool, four bedrooms, and common areas dotted with modern furnishings from the likes of Piero Lissoni and Pierre Paulin. Staying here ensures a prime place for trekking out on hikes, while indoors you can take in wraparound views of the landscape while you swim laps in the deep end. From $2,333 per night.

Perks: Two game rooms, multiple smart TVs, fire pit, disc golf, covered balcony

Perks: Fireplace, breakfast nook, porch, farm animals

For a pastoral adventure with some thoroughly modern comforts, book this inn located on a farm 45 minutes out from Portland, Maine. Here, you’ll be occupying the cozy second floor suite, which offers a living room with a fireplace on cold winter nights and polished sleeping quarters. Since the inn sits on a working farm, you can also commune with the cute sheep and goats on the property, which spans 100 acres for walking and exploring the local fields. From $183 per night.

Perks: Game room, fireplaces, furnished deck, fire pit, movie projector

If your idea of a perfect mountain getaway involves never hearing the words "I'm bored," this Poconos rental has you covered. The four-bedroom home pairs a private heated indoor pool and spa with a long list of rainy-day diversions, including a dedicated game room stocked with foosball, ping-pong, shuffleboard, and a poker table, plus a media room with surround sound and an Xbox. Upstairs, the vibe is bright and contemporary, with an open-plan living area, two gas fireplaces, and a renovated kitchen anchored by an oversized dining table that seats 12. When the weather cooperates, the fun spills outdoors, where you'll find two furnished decks, a fire pit, and access to the community's lake, sports courts, and seasonal pools. From $900 per night

Perks: Mountain views, electric car charger, laundry

Colorado is a state known for its epic ski resorts and wintery locales, but in the city of Colorado Springs, all kinds of adventures await when you book this Southwestern-style home that feels all but plucked out of the desert. The jewel of the home is its long stretch of indoor pool that heats up to 90 degrees Fahrenheit so you can break a sweat even when it’s in the single digits outside. Spread across multiple levels that include plenty of terraces for enjoying the great outdoors, you can also get up and close and personal with nature on any of the surrounding hiking trails (including the ones that snake through Pulpit Rock Park and Garden of the Gods). From $755 per night.

The biggest perk of this rustic five-bedroom chalet in Plymouth, Vermont, is its proximity to Okemo Mountain and the slopes of Killington Peak. But after a long day on the slopes, your aching limbs might need a cooldown, which is where the home’s sizable indoor pool comes in. Surrounded by stone walkways and a comfy seating area, plus accompanied by a wood-paneled sauna, the pool area offers several ways to unwind. Elsewhere, the cozy cabin invites you in with a stone fireplace and modern amenities like smart TVs and central heating. Large windows throughout the home also let you take in the stunning views of surrounding mountains as you lounge or share meals. From $1,685 per night.

Perks: Home gym, game room, mountain and desert views

This amazing home in Tucson’s Catalina Foothills features an indoor pool with a second-story walking track, a home gym, and an extensive game room that encourages some healthy competitiveness. Guests have access to the entire home, which consists of four bedrooms and seven bathrooms—so go ahead and invite everyone on the group chat. When (or if) you decide to venture out, you’ll find yourself in close proximity to several great hiking trails, shops, restaurants, and golf courses that let you practice your swing. From $885 per night.

Perks: Mountain views, fishing ponds, sauna

The misleading name of the town aside, there’s nothing small about this property. In fact, with 13 bedrooms, two fishing ponds, and 33 acres of land to explore, you may not even need to venture off the property. With all that space, it’s hardly surprising that this classic sideboard farmhouse even thought to include an indoor pool. There’s also a sauna and hot tub on one side and a game room complete with a slate pool table, a stylish bar, and a card table on the other. From $2,695 per night.

Perks: Mountain views, hiking trails, pond

This custom-built lodge is situated on 19 private acres of land in the mountains of Pennsylvania, with 5,400 feet of lush wilderness to admire—including a private pond—from the heated indoor pool, which sits on the lower level of a two-story loft space clad in warm cedar wood planks. There’s even a firepit. For anyone willing to brave the cold, the private pool area houses the largest of the home’s five decks and boasts tables with room for 12 along with a collection of Adirondack chairs. Though the snowcapped mountain landscapes are quite visible from just about any of the estate’s rooms—including the six bedrooms and great room with high ceilings and a fireplace—the views are best from this particular terrace. Otherwise, immerse yourself in the surrounding nature by taking to the hiking trails. From $1,491 per night.

Skiers and snowboarders headed to the rugged mountains of Big Sky can enjoy a special après ski experience by way of this 25,000-square-foot estate with a 60 foot heated indoor pool, hot tub, and sauna. After working up an appetite in the deep end, you can pop over to the chef’s kitchen and adjacent dining room (which features two dining tables that seat up to 12 people each) for a recovery meal. Or, for the more traditional chalet experience, sit back and relax in front of the floor-to-ceiling stone fireplace in the living room, which is the definition of rustic coziness thanks to its beamed ceilings and warm décor. From $7,062 per night.

#4752. Kacey Musgraves Is Hanging Her Cowboy Hat in Mexico These Days

Kacey Musgraves may be a Nashville staple with deep Texas roots, but the country star is calling a new locale home lately. The “Rainbow” singer, who has resided in the Music City for many years, revealed in a recent interview that she now lives in Mexico “most of the time.”

Musgraves told People that the change of scene “ended up just completely changing my life and inspiring me so much. I’m so in love with so many aspects of it, and it’s really taught me a lot.” She explained that the transition took place over a year and a half ago when “this beautiful cosmic thing” drew her down to Mexico. Living there with her Australian cattle dog Pepper, the singer-songwriter found comfort in a slower pace of life, which has allowed her to be barefoot often and to spend plenty of time outdoors. The relocation even moved Musgraves to release a sister album to her most recent, Middle of Nowhere Vol. 2: Tex Mex Tapes, which will feature four songs in Spanish.

Musgaves previously opened up about her wanderlust and the possibility of leaving Nashville for the bright lights of New York City. “Girl, I’m always browsing Zillow late at night, acting like a fool—it’s my favorite,” she told People in 2024. “I wouldn’t rule [moving] out, but I do have a community in Nashville that I know that I couldn’t find anywhere else.”

Back in 2022, the country queen and her 3,500-square-foot Nashville home graced AD’s May 2022 cover. She purchased the property in 2020 during a time of big changes in her life, which included her split from singer-songwriter Ruston Kelly. Still, she made it clear that the blank canvas was no “divorce house.”

“I wanted a place that felt like me,” she said, “where I could express myself without having to think about another person and what they might want. This felt like a new beginning.” Musgraves went on to sell that space and ultimately bought a “cottage” in the Tennessee forest.

#4750. The Mercedes-Benz EQS drove 539 miles on a single charge. Still worried about range?

The new Mercedes-Benz EQS 450+ covered 539 miles without stopping to charge, making range anxiety look like a thing of the past.

Mercedes-Benz overhauled the EQS electric sedan, including a bigger battery and an 800V electrical architecture.

The 2027 Mercedes-Benz EQS 450+ uses a 122 kWh battery that provides an EPA-estimated range of up to 467 miles.

While that’s already impressive, the electric sedan will likely go even farther in everyday driving conditions. During Edmunds recent EV Range Test, the 2027 Mercedes EQS 450+ drove a whopping 539 miles without stopping to charge.

The EQS beat its own EPA estimate by 72 miles and is now the longest-range electric sedan Edmunds has ever tested, beating the Lucid Air, the first EV to break the 500-mile mark. The only EV that has it beat is the Cadillac Escalade IQ, which managed 558 miles from its massive 205 kWh battery.

During the test, the 2027 Mercedes EQS 450+ was also more efficient than its official numbers suggest.

The new EQS used 29.6 kWh per 100 miles, beating its EPA estimate of 28 kWh per 100 miles. In comparison, the 2025 Mercedes EQS 450+ covered 464 miles and used 34 kWh per 100 miles during the same test, so the improvements are considerable.

Edmunds observed a peak charging rate of 350 kW, in line with Mercedes’ estimates, while it sustained an average of 222 kW throughout the session.

Both beat the older EQS, which reached a peak charge rate of 208 kW and sustained an average of 175 kW.

The new Mercedes EQS 450+ added 825 miles of range in an hour, and 100 miles in just over 7 minutes, nearly twice the speed of the 2025MY.

The Edmunds EV Range Test is designed to provide a more accurate estimate of an EV’s range in everyday driving conditions. It includes 60% city and 40% highway driving with an average speed of 40 mph. The vehicle stays within 5 mph of the posted speed limit, and the climate control is set to auto at 72 degrees.

How much is too much? 539 miles on a single charge is impressive, but do you need it? In a recent “nationally representative” survey, BMW found that Americans want more EV range than they actually need.

The study showed that 73% consider 25, 50, or 75 miles enough to cover their daily driving. However, 42% said they would require at least 300 miles of range before they would consider an EV.

If you have access to a home charger, nothing beats waking up with a full charge every day. Meanwhile, public charging continues to improve, giving EV drivers more reliable options on longer trips.

Faster charging also helps. Another 53% said they would be more likely to consider an EV if it could add about 150 miles of range in around 10 minutes.

In the same Edmunds EV Range Test last week, the new 2027 BMW iX3 50 xDrive drove 436 miles on a single charge, exceeding its EPA estimate of 392 miles by 44 miles. It also used 28.3 kWh per 100 miles during the test, beating its official EPA estimate of 32 kWh per 100 miles.

I'm really glad to see more articles like this and people talking more about efficiency. It's not just about bigger batteries and range. It needs to be about smaller batteries plus great efficiency giving that same range. We need to do this with more efficient designs, more sedans and lighter vehicles.

The new 2027 Volvo EX60 drove 352 miles while the 2027 Mercedes-Benz GLC Electric covered 392 miles during the same test.

One thing is certain: electric vehicles are becoming more efficient, enabling longer driving ranges and faster charging times. As battery tech advances, EVs will drive farther and charge faster, while safety and performance will continue to improve.

How much driving range would you consider enough for your daily drive? What about for the week? Let us know in the comments below.

#4746. PlugShare finally adds dark mode, and there’s more for EV drivers

PlugShare is giving its EV charging app a big overhaul. Version 8.0 arrives worldwide for iPhone on November 10, with dark mode, a trip planner that shows how far apart charging stops are, and a rebuilt Apple Watch app that can point you to a charger compatible with your EV.

Dark mode was one of the most-requested features from PlugShare users. It works across the app and follows your iPhone’s settings, so if your phone switches to dark mode at sunset, your charging map will, too.

The trip planner gets a small but handy change. Each charging stop now shows how far it is from both the start of your trip and the previous stop. So when you’re deciding whether to stretch to the next fast charger or stop sooner, the number you need is right there.

PlugShare now loads your saved locations, trips, and profile when you open the app or sign in, so those tabs are ready to tap. You can also open and zoom in on station photos right from their thumbnails. That’s handy when you’re trying to figure out what corner a charging station is in in a big parking lot. The app also adapts its layout to the iPhone Duo’s different screens and folded positions.

The rebuilt Watch app lists nearby charging stations with distances and directions. It uses the filters you’ve set on your iPhone, so if you’ve filtered for plugs that fit your EV, that carries over to your wrist.

Tap a station and you’ll see its PlugScore, the network that runs it, a small map, and which plugs it has, including NACS, CCS, CHAdeMO, or J1772. The list refreshes when you raise your wrist and open the app. You can also add a shortcut to a round or corner slot on your watch face.

The iPhone update is rolling out through the App Store. PlugShare says Android users will get dark mode and a redesign based on Google’s Material 3 in early 2027.

#4745. Shirley MacLaine at Home: 10 Photos of the Golden Age Great’s Life Behind the Scenes

“A house is really a life…one you’ve had or one you want to have. A new dream.” So wrote Shirley MacLaine on the first page of her memoir Sage-ing While Age-ing. And she should know. The dancer-actor-author has lived everywhere from the family home at 930 North Liberty Street in Richmond, Virginia, which she shared with her parents and younger brother (none other than Warren Beatty) to Manhattan apartments, Malibu beach cottages, and New Mexican desert ranches. And that’s just in this lifetime; MacLaine believes she has come around again through several incarnations, even writing books about her experience as a nongendered being in the Lemurian era, and another go-round on the lost continent of Atlantis.

In the 10 images below, we focus on MacLaine’s current incarnation, stepping into the Oscar-winner’s homes to see the star of Steel Magnolias and Downton Abbey at work, at play, and dancing on air.

MacLaine relaxes with a magazine in 1954.

At 20, MacLaine was a chorus girl on Broadway and the understudy for Carol Haney in The Pajama Game. When Haney twisted her ankle, MacLaine (who had changed her last name from Beaty to an alternate spelling of her mother’s maiden name, MacLean), replaced her for two weeks. Alfred Hitchcock spotted her and cast her in The Trouble with Harry, and Hal Walis signed her to a five-year studio contract, which led her to move to California. This 1954 shot shows her reading a magazine at home in Malibu, where she rented a tiny beachfront apartment. “Each home was a palace and a new jolt. They loomed on cultivated hilltops or nestled in L-shapes and U-shapes on shaded slopes, jewel-like habitations set in acres of jade carpet,” MacLaine recalled in her memoir Don’t Fall Off the Mountain. “We stared at the fairyland until our eyes hurt and our sense of reality reminded us that we were flat broke.”

When MacLaine moved from New York to California, she writes in Don’t Fall Off the Mountain, “sunshine bled from every corner and the dazzling white buildings gave me a permanent squint.” The happy gaze is on full display here in on the beach in front of the Malibu one-bedroom she and husband Steve Parker rented, which was not white, but a weathered wood cottage on stilts.

MacLaine and Parker, making the most of their outdoor shower in 1955.

“When I met Steve Parker, the attraction was immediate,” MacLaine writes in her photo-driven memoir The Wall of Life. She first encountered the businessman in New York, where, before he proposed, he informed her he was “undercover with military intelligence and work in Japan regularly, as well as other places throughout the world.” Here, Parker sneaks up on MacLaine in the one-bedroom, one-bathroom Malibu pad’s bonus outdoor shower.

The newlyweds on their home’s deck in Malibu in March, 1955.

This 1955 shot captures a rare moment of togetherness for MacLaine and Parker at their Malibu beach house. He often lived in Japan while she made films. “I’m not sure I would say that Steve and I had a happy marriage, but it was a satisfied one,” MacLaine wrote in The Wall of Life. “He had a mistress in Japan and I had whoever I was with.” But in their SoCal haven, they experienced their first taste of married life. “I was up every morning at four-thirty trying to be a housewife—ironing cleaning, and experimenting with the cookbook,” MacLaine wrote in Don’t Fall Off the Mountain. “At six Steve drove me to the studio, where we shot the final sequences of The Trouble with Harry.”

MacLaine dancing on the beach in front of her home in Malibu.

MacLaine, shown here leaping on the beach in front of her home, trained as a dancer since age three. “As I recall my life, I have come to understand that the physicality of dance was the most influential force,” she wrote in The Wall of Life. Here, MacLaine shows of her skills at the rental property she described in Don’t Fall Off the Mountain as “a tiny, one-bedroom apartment in a building set high on pilings that shook with each wave that crashed beneath it.”

This 1955 publicity shot shows MacLaine studying a script under Parker’s adoring eye in their Malibu home. While the residence’s knotty pine walls were peak mid-’50s rec room decor, MacLaine recalls Hollywood looking down on her living arrangements as her star was on the rise. She imagines the whisperings about her in Don’t Fall Off the Mountain: “She’s STILL living in a one-room shack at the beach. She’s STILL married to that man, what’s his name?”

MacLaine plays with her dog at home in 1956.

In this March 1956 photo, MacLaine plays with her boxer at home in Malibu. Six months later, on September 1, her daughter Sachi Parker would be born, inspiring MacLaine to move her new family to larger digs “in the Malibu colony with a spare room for Sachi’s new nurse-caretaker,” she wrote in The Wall of Life. “Our first year together felt like we were beach camping. I never wondered if the lifestyle was good for her, and she was never sick.” In 1963, architect Cliff Mays built an eight-unit Malibu apartment building where MacLaine would claim the top floor, which boasted six bedrooms, four baths, and a deck overlooking the ocean. She retained the property for 30 years until relocating to New Mexico, telling The Los Angeles Times, “I feel the high desert calling me.”

Shirley MacLaine in full 1970s style: maxidress, long hair. and aggressive shag carpet.

In 1972, MacLaine was spending most of her time in an apartment at 400 East 52nd Street after filming her short-lived TV series Shirley’s World. The building is part of the Southgate complex, which opened in 1931, but MacLaine did not adhere to the edifice’s Art Deco style—note the thick shag carpeting and the East Asian–inspired art on the walls. Dancer Tommy Tune bought the penthouse of the building after visiting MacLaine in her apartment.

Describing her life in New York in the 1970s, MacLaine wrote in You Can Get There from Here that “on any given day, I would mosey into Serendipity for a frozen mocha, plunder the skirt shop at Bloomingdale’s, walk to the Village and back until I reached Beekman Place at sunset and breathed in the smell of the East River.… I wanted to sit in my apartment for hours, watching all the news shows, and eating cinnamon buns from the automat.”

In this 1980 photo of MacLaine at the Southgate, her hair is different but the floral sofa remains as it was in 1972. In the years between 1972 and 1980, “I did just three films. I don’t think I got any good scripts in that period, but I didn’t care and was honestly more interested in seeing the world and understanding what was going on in it.” She made a documentary in China, campaigned for George McGovern and Jimmy Carter, and did live shows in New York and London. She was drawn back to Hollywood to make The Turning Point, and won an Oscar for her work in 1983’s Terms of Endearment.

Today, MacLaine’s New Mexico ranch is on the market, and, at age 92, she has been spotted out and about in her old stomping grounds: Malibu.

Most egg cups don’t account for the wreckage of a cracked shell or the spillage from runny yolk. This ceramic one certainly does, with a rim around it for propping a spoon or collecting the remnants of your breakfast.

A chamberstick is a throw-back choice for the most old-school person in your life, or any adamant lover of historical fiction, and also comes in handy during a blackout.

#4736. Humble Hauler autonomous trailer takes the truck out of trucking [update]

Less than a year after its founding, truckless trucking brand Humble Robotics has secured more than $75 million after a $51 million Series A funding round that will fuel the company’s transition from prototype to preproduction.

The latest funding round for the driverless, cab-less, and truck-less battery-electric freight platform developer was led by Obvious Ventures with participation from other existing investors Eclipse and Energy Impact Partners. Humble said it will use the funding for customer pilots, testing and validation, and early manufacturing.

“We’ve spent years proving that a truck can drive itself,” explains Humble Robotics founder and CEO, Eyal Cohen. “The harder question is how to make autonomy something a freight operator can depend on every day.”

We originally covered the Humble Hauler back in May. You can check out the original article, below, then let us know what you think of the company’s prospects in the comments.

The startup that dares to ask, “Why does the Macbook driving my freight around need a whole cab?” just showed off a fully driverless, cab-less, and truck-less battery-electric freight platform designed to handle containerized cargo more efficiently than ever.

Instead of looking at the tractor and asking how to make it work without a driver, Humble looked at the trailer, itself, redesigning the container chassis as a vehicle from the ground up. The end result isn’t just an autonomous solution, it’s an overall lighter platform without a cab that saves shippers the weight of a sleeper cab, a day cab, or the truck at all.

The Humble approach is said to improve payload efficiency of shipping containers, and intended for dock-to-dock operation.

“I have dedicated my career to building electric and autonomous vehicle technology,” explains Eyal Cohen, Humble Robotics’ founder and CEO. “For the first time, freight can be fully automated all the way to the loading dock. We are making freight sustainable, safe and efficient in a way no one thought was possible. And we’re doing it with an exceptional team of industry veterans and AV experts.”

Emerging from stealth after a $24 million funding round, Humble claims to have completed its first Humble Hauler prototype in less than six months – and they’ll soon be ready to begin autonomous testing in pilot programs with a number of logistics partners.

The vehicle uses a combination of cameras, lidar, and radar for 360-degree awareness (unhindered by seeing around a cab), and uses vision-language-action models to help the system respond to new scenarios.

Thanks to superior battery tech, a maturing global charging infrastructure, and a permanently damaged MidEast oil supply, there’s never been a better time to be an EV startup. Humble is hitting all the electric trailer buzzwords, too, while also touting the fact that the electric powertrain can reduce a company’s to volatile fuel prices even as it helps produce other cost benefits like lower maintenance costs.

There is a reason why the transportation industry developed separate cabs and trailers. Trailers can be loaded or unloaded for days and are much less expensive than trucks that serve both purposes in one piece. That allows the more expensive piece of the transport puzzle to be used more and allows the trailer to be loaded and unloaded while the cab is elsewhere working or in the shop. Query whether the savings they cite will surpass the costs of not having the flexibility of a cab and trailer design.

And if a robot like this can deliver on its promises without the baked-in inefficiencies of humanoids, they might be one step closer to provide that the most efficient solution to a problem is rarely a general one, and even rarer is it a solution that looks like a human.

#4735. What to Wear to Thanksgiving: 6 Outfit Ideas for Every Type of Gathering

From dressier holiday looks to comfy and stretchy.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

With Thanksgiving just around the corner, I’ve already started thinking about two very important things: what’s going on the dinner table and what I’ll wear. I’m especially looking forward to my mom’s baked mashed potatoes (a family favorite) and the homemade mac and cheese I’ll be bringing along. But, especially as a style writer, half the fun of the holidays is having a good excuse to put together a good outfit.

My family’s Thanksgiving gatherings tend to be pretty casual, so I’m not one to show up in anything overly dressy or uncomfortable. I’m usually somewhere in the middle, opting for an outfit that looks like I made an effort but still lets me enjoy a second helping of stuffing without worrying about a too-tight waistband. Think a great sweater with loose-fitting jeans, a knit dress with boots, or even a smartly-styled leggings outfit. Because as much as I love dressing up for the holidays, I also want to enjoy post-dinner card games or sit comfortably on the couch afterward.

If you’re approaching Thanksgiving dressing the same way, I’ve rounded up six outfit ideas to help get you inspired. I’ve included a few options perfect for a Christmas card photoshoot, along with more laid-back looks that are just as fitting for a casual family gathering. No matter what your holiday plans look like, these outfits strike that happy medium between looking good and feeling comfortable. Now, if you’ll excuse me, I have some mac and cheese to make.

In my opinion, there’s no outfit more perfect for the holidays than a sweater dress and boots. It’s cute enough for family photos but loose and soft enough to keep you comfy all day (especially if you kick the boots off at the door). Grab a long-sleeve knit dress that hits somewhere between mid-thigh and the knee and pair it with some fleece-lined tights and your fave knee-high boot. A midi or maxi dress with an ankle bootie works too.

Start by pulling out a denim jacket and denim skirt. From there, add a plain white T-shirt. I love brown for the fall, so I wore my version of this outfit with brown boots and a matching brown belt and bag. I love this combo because it looks super stylish, but I don’t feel overdressed or uncomfortable when I wear it. Don’t have a denim skirt or jacket? No problem. You can recreate this look with a leather jacket, jeans, or any neutral mini skirt.

Start with a pair of satin or silk pants and pair them with a sweater and ballet flats in the same shade. This outfit has been one of my go-tos lately because it’s so comfortable, but the monochrome palette and shiny finish of the pants make it look like I tried way harder than I actually did. You can even make it feel a little dressier by swapping the flats for a pair of pointed-toe ankle boots.

It doesn’t get comfier than leggings and slippers. For ultra-casual gatherings when you just want to focus on family and food without looking frumpy, just style yours a little smarter. Go for a pair of leggings with minimal seaming (especially a pair without a front seam), which makes them look sleeker. Then grab a dressy-looking oversized sweater—something with a boxy cut, a turtleneck, or a nice knit. I find that my tweed stitch J. Crew Rollneck Sweater is perfect for this look. Finish it off with a neutral slipper and a pair of scrunchy socks.

Crz Yoga SculptAir No Front Seam Leggings

Opt for a pair of baggier jeans for this more casual outfit, which will be way comfier than a restrictive skinny or straight-leg jean. A looser pant also pairs better with a cozy boot, like this platform ankle boot. Finally, throw on a long-sleeve top or sweater with a cute vest for an outfit that feels very fall.

Banana Republic The Icon Classic Wide-Leg Jean

Okay, you caught me—I actually wore this outfit for Christmas last year instead of Thanksgiving, but it definitely works for either holiday! For this combo, start with your favorite skirt and a turtleneck top. Then top it off with a relaxed-fitting blazer (I love this houndstooth blazer, but any pattern or solid color will do). On the bottom, throw on a pair of tights and a knee-high boot.

Shannen graduated from the State University of New York at Cortland with a bachelor's degree in English. #4733. Theaster Gates is Changing the Future of American Fine China

It all started when Theaster Gates wanted to throw a dinner party. The Chicago-based artist, whose practice blends ceramics, painting, performance, and urban renewal, imagined inviting 200 guests on each of five nights. Central to the project, he envisioned creating all the tableware himself.

“My friends were like, ‘Oh, you should work with Pickard,’ ” Gates recalls, referring to the last remaining producer of porcelain tableware in the US, founded in 1893 and headquartered since 1930 in Antioch, Illinois. When he told Pickard he would need some 10,000 items, the firm admitted such an order would be daunting but invited him to visit.

“I came to the warehouse and realized that the company had had better days,” Gates says. “I’m wanting to say this with a lot of dignity, but as we moved between tarps and equipment, it became clear that the company was just doing what it could to stay alive.”

China dishware fills two rolling racks at the Pickard factory in Illinois, which was recently purchased by Theaster Gates.

Gates at the Pickard facility with molds for dishware

Pickard’s august history of commissions includes White House services for first ladies Laura Bush and Michelle Obama, as well as the official State Department dinnerware used in US embassies around the world. Once an essential element of bridal registries, fine china has waned in popularity, however, along with the tradition of formal entertaining at home. After CEO Andy Pickard Morgan, a member of the fourth generation of the family to run the business, acknowledged that he was ready to call it a day, one thing led to another, and Gates says, “I am now the owner of Pickard China.”

His acquisition might seem out of character. Gates has forged a formidable reputation for himself and his Rebuild Foundation by buying rundown buildings in Chicago’s historically Black South Side, then converting them into thriving arts and education hubs. An abandoned residence is now the Black Cinema House; a century-old savings and loan is now the Stony Island Arts Bank, which mounts exhibitions and safeguards historical collections. Pickard, meanwhile, is located in a quiet, predominantly white village on the Wisconsin border, some 67 miles from his studio on Chicago’s South Side.

Gates, funnel in hand, surrounded by artifacts of the Pickard process

But Gates does not see a contradiction. For starters, he says, his interest in craft transcends racial or ethnic origins; one formative experience was studying with master potters in Japan. Gates notes that while many of his collections relate to Black culture, he also took on the late scholar Robert Bird’s library of films by the Russian director Andrei Tarkovsky and the classical music archive of the LaSalle Quartet, whose core members were Jewish. “I have those because they are rich, and someone passed, and those things needed a new life,” he explains. “The problem of the archive is not one of Black or white; it’s one of stewardship and imagination.” He has even shown an affinity for luxury goods, having made ceramic vessels for Prada Home in Milan this past April.

Pickard also presents the kind of big, multifaceted problem Gates, who holds a master’s degree in urban planning, says “makes me feel alive as an artist,” in this case encompassing craftsmanship and legacy, as well as the value of labor and community. His plans are still developing but include upgrading the facilities, rethinking the product line, and beefing up the staff, which now stands at 14 full-time employees, down from dozens at the company’s height. Preserving their know-how for posterity is a priority. “I want this really capable team to train a new generation of descalers and liners and glazers,” he says. Also definite: The company will continue operating in the commercial sphere, and he will use its massive kilns to fire his own large sculptures, something he’s wanted to do for a decade. “I’m thinking about manufacturing ceramics as an extension of my practice,” he notes.

Gates has worked with a variety of materials over the years, including tar paper—a nod to his roofer father—and salvaged fire hoses, which reference Civil Rights Era marchers who were blasted with water by police. Ceramics, though, were the focus of his formal studies and remain at the heart of his practice. “When I was training, I touched porcelain, but I was always a rough stoneware guy,” he says. “The more grog and sand and feldspar, the better.”

Today Gates considers porcelain “an amazing craft” and expects the workshop to become “a playground for artistic promise,” where he, together with staff and visiting artists alike, can “dream about a raw material that could be anything.”

Setting the table with bone china may no longer be the signifier of position and civility it once was, but Gates believes there’s still room for appreciation, as both an instrument of ritual and a family heirloom. “Legacy objects play a huge role in how we remember our grandparents, our parents, and our past,” he says.

And Gates’s own party is very much still on the table, so to speak. “Porcelain things are beautiful objects, but I’m drawn as much to the use of the thing as I am to the thing,” he says. “The idea that I could make large works of art with porcelain is exciting, but I’m also excited to be able to host a thousand-person dinner and make all the wares for it.”

#4732. RJ Mitte's Death Highlights Mental Health Challenges for People With Cerebral Palsy

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

“Breaking Bad” actor and disability advocate RJ Mitte has died at age 34.

Mitte reportedly died by suicide, his representative told People on October 12. However, local authorities have said the official cause and manner of his death remain under investigation.

Mitte, who was best-known for playing Walter White Jr. on the Emmy Award-winning television series “Breaking Bad,” lived with cerebral palsy and advocated for disability inclusion.

There is currently no evidence to suggest that cerebral palsy caused the actor’s death. Still, Mitte’s untimely passing highlights the mental health challenges that people with cerebral palsy may face.

Research has demonstrated elevated risks of anxiety and depression among those with the condition, alongside barriers to diagnosis, social participation, and employment.

“Living with cerebral palsy can affect mental health through several interacting pathways,” said Shannel Elhelou, neuropsychologist and founder of Brain Centric Neuropsychology in Westlake Village, CA.

“Chronic pain, fatigue, disrupted sleep, and the effort required to navigate daily activities can take an emotional toll. Experiences of bullying, discrimination, social exclusion, and difficulty accessing care can add to that burden. Also, the early brain injury underlying cerebral palsy may contribute to difficulties with emotional regulation, although each person’s experience is different,” Elhelou told Healthline.

If you or someone you know is in crisis and considering suicide or self-harm, please seek support:

If you’re calling on behalf of someone else, stay with them until help arrives. You may remove weapons or substances that can cause harm if you can do so safely.

If you’re not in the same household, stay on the phone with them until help arrives.

Mitte played a key role in challenging stereotypes by portraying a teenager with cerebral palsy on “Breaking Bad.”

Outside of acting, Mitte advocated for people with disabilities through organizations including United Cerebral Palsy (UCP) and Shriners Children’s.

He also led the Mitte Foundation, a non-profit corporation founded in 1994 by his grandparents, Roy F. and Joann Cole Mitte, that improves the quality of life for people in Central Texas.

Mitte’s advocacy emphasized representation, inclusion, and opportunities for people with disabilities.

The legacy he leaves behind underscores the need to recognize mental health as an essential part of care for individuals with cerebral palsy and other disabilities.

Cerebral palsy (CP) is a group of conditions affecting movement, posture, and coordination.

Most people with cerebral palsy have typical intelligence, although some also have intellectual disabilities or other comorbid physical and mental health conditions.

A 2018 study published in JAMA Neurology found that adults with cerebral palsy had a 28% higher risk of developing depression and a 40% higher risk of anxiety than adults without the condition.

A 2024 study in JAMA Network Open found higher recorded rates of anxiety and attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) among children and young adults with cerebral palsy.

The researchers also identified comparatively low rates of depression and suicidal ideation, highlighting concerns that these conditions may be underdiagnosed.

“Research shows that adults with cerebral palsy have an increased risk of depression and anxiety,” Elhelou said.

“However, these conditions should never be viewed as an inevitable part of having a disability. One concern is ‘diagnostic overshadowing,’ when symptoms of depression, anxiety, or other mental health conditions are attributed to cerebral palsy itself, leading those conditions to go unrecognized and untreated. Someone’s fatigue, withdrawal, or changes in behavior deserve careful assessment rather than the assumption that ‘this is just their cerebral palsy,'” she continued.

“While these findings help us understand mental health risks among people with cerebral palsy, they cannot tell us what any one person was experiencing. Many interacting factors may contribute to someone dying by suicide, and it would be inappropriate to attribute an individual’s death to their disability.”

A 2019 study published in Developmental Medicine & Child Neurology found that children with cerebral palsy had higher odds of anxiety disorders and behavioral challenges than children without the condition.

Difficulties with friendships and bullying were associated with some of these differences.

Factors that may affect well-being include:

The researchers also found that supportive friendships may help reduce some of these disparities, reinforcing the importance of inclusive schools and communities.

A 2021 study published in the American Journal of Preventive Medicine found that U.S. adults with disabilities faced increased risks of suicidal thoughts, planning, and attempts compared with adults without disabilities.

The 988 Lifeline received more than 8 million contacts through calls, texts, chats, and American Sign Language videophone in 2025, according to the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration.

Federal funding for the Lifeline’s dedicated LGBTQ+ youth service ended in July 2025, removing the option to press 3 for specialized support.

After pressure from Congress, the service was recently restored at the end of September, according to the Kaiser Family Foundation (KFF).

Employment and financial independence can influence well-being for anyone, particularly those with cerebral palsy.

However, a 2024 study found that just 30.4% of young adults with cerebral palsy who participated in vocational rehabilitation programs were employed when they exited those programs.

Workplace accommodations, accessible hiring, vocational rehabilitation, and inclusive social opportunities can help address these barriers.

“Depression and anxiety are treatable, and they should never be dismissed as an inevitable part of living with a disability,” Elhelou said.

“If you notice persistent sadness, anxiety, hopelessness, or withdrawal, reach out to someone you trust and a mental health professional. You deserve a clinician who takes your concerns seriously and adapts treatment to your communication, mobility, or other access needs. Care should also address pain, fatigue, and sleep difficulties, which can affect emotional well-being.”

Organizations such as United Cerebral Palsy offer information and support for people with cerebral palsy and their families.

Healthcare professionals can also improve outcomes by screening for mental health concerns, using appropriate assessment tools, and connecting patients with accessible treatment.

Improved access to care, inclusive communities, and meaningful opportunities can help people with cerebral palsy and other disabilities thrive.

“Asking for help does not diminish your resilience or your contributions. If you are experiencing thoughts of suicide or feel overwhelmed, you do not have to wait until things feel unbearable to reach out,” Elhelou said.

“Supportive relationships, peer groups, and disability communities can offer connection and understanding. Staying engaged in activities that bring enjoyment and meaning can also help.”

#4728. Pope Leo XIV Diagnosed With Lung Cancer After Surgery to Remove Part of Lung

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Pope Leo XIV has been diagnosed with lung cancer following a successful surgery, the Vatican said.

The Pope, 71, underwent surgery at Agostino Gemelli University Policlinic in Rome on October 13 to remove a nodule from his right lung. This was determined to be pulmonary adenocarcinoma, the most common type of lung cancer.

The surgeons removed the lower lobe of the Pope’s lung to “ensure a radical procedure; the lower lobe of the right lung was removed using a video-assisted technique,” said Dr. Luigi Carbone, the Director of Vatican Healthcare Services, and Professor Stephano Margaritora, the lead surgeon, in a press release.

“An intraoperative histological examination confirmed the diagnosis of lung adenocarcinoma, as suspected from the preoperative examinations,” they said.

Pope Leo was in the operating room for 3 hours, while the operation itself lasted around 90 minutes.

The doctors noted that it was a successful operation with no complications.

According to Matteo Bruni, the Director of the Holy See Press Office, the Pope asked for dinner after the surgery and spent the evening with his secretaries and other Vatican staff.

In a press release on Wednesday, the Vatican said that the Pope’s postoperative recovery is progressing well.

According to Vatican News, Pope Leo had a calm first night following his surgery.

In the coming days, exams will be conducted on the removed tissue. There has currently been no change in the initially announced week of hospitalization.

On October 14, the Pope underwent blood tests and chest X-rays that were scheduled following his surgery. The results of these tests confirm that his postoperative recovery is progressing as expected, and the values are within a normal range for the type of operation performed.

Pope Leo has also undergone some respiratory physiotherapy and has begun walking around his room, the Vatican said.

It has not yet been announced whether he will require any further treatment.

Adenocarcinoma is the most common subtype of lung cancer globally. It accounts for around 40% of all lung cancer diagnoses.

Adenocarcinoma is a type of non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC), which accounts for about 80–85% of all lung cancers.

Lung adenocarcinoma begins in the epithelial cells that produce mucus. It is the most common type of NSCLC.

Adenocarcinoma can be classified further by the genetic changes driving the tumor. For example, EGFR-positive lung cancer is found more often in people who have never smoked and can often be treated with targeted therapies, which is why biomarker testing of tumor tissue helps guide treatment decisions.

Treatment for NSCLC is generally determined by the stage. However, it typically involves one or a combination of:

Surgery is generally the first step in treatment for this type of lung cancer. The right lung has three lobes, or sections, and the left has two.

A doctor will often remove the entire lobe that has the tumor in order to remove the tumor and improve the outlook.

The body eventually adapts in order to compensate for the loss of function.

According to the American Cancer Society (ACS), for NSCLC that hasn’t spread outside of the lung, the 5-year relative survival rate is 67%.

#4727. 5 New Retail Design Rules, According to Experts

When nearly anything can be bought with a few taps on a screen, brick-and-mortar stores have to work harder—and smarter—to earn a visit. What e-commerce can't replicate, even with the cleverest algorithms, is the thrill of stepping into a beautifully conceived space—and discovering something you didn't know you wanted. From side-by-side jewel-box fashion boutiques in Paris to a palatial bridal studio set in a 19th-century building in downtown New York, AD PRO highlights the big ideas shaping today’s most engaging retail environments.

The first goal for any new retail space is obvious: Make people want to be there.

Fashion designer Danielle Frankel Hirsch just opened a 12,000-square-foot studio in Tribeca, collaborating with Studio Partners Architecture and AD PRO Directory designer Augusta Hoffman to create a gasp-worthy dress boutique worthy of any bride's fantasies. Located in a 19th century edifice originally built to house the New York Mercantile Exchange, the shop, says Frankel Hirsch, is designed as a transportive experience. After guests enter the space through its unassuming Tribeca storefront entrance, they ascend a grand carpeted staircase to a large gallery boasting over 30-foot floor-to-ceiling windows. Hoffman worked with artist Miriam Ellner to create églomisé paneling surrounding the staircase, and outfitted the spaces to the height of elegance, with Fortuny fabrics, furniture sourced from Paris, and handpainted ceiling murals.

The 30-foot ceilings at New York City's Danielle Frankel guarantee a wedding dress shopping experience that's hard to equal

In Paris, Rubirosa's two petite boutiques, designed by Louis Charles Aka, deliver a similar sense of occasion on a much smaller scale. The original 500-square-foot shop, which opened in June 2025, was joined this past August by a second location just next door. Rather than simply duplicate the first boutique's nostalgic formality, Aka has given its new neighbor a distinct personality. The lacquered shelving remains, displaying cashmere sweaters, crisp poplin shirts, and soft leather slippers in a rainbow of colors. But where the original featured tall bookcases, the new shop has floor-to-ceiling arches whose cubbies become bands of color when filled with garments of cashmere, cotton, and silk.

In the original boutique, the defining gesture was a dense, velvety red wall-to-wall carpet from La Maison Bineau. “I spent everything on the red carpet,” Aka declares. “People criticized, ‘You will have to change it in three months.’ I said, ‘We don’t care. We want people to feel special.’” In the second shop, the color drama comes courtesy of vivid Yves Klein blue—a knowing wink to the fashion set. Voluminous white balloon drapery heightens the theatricality, suggesting a salon for elegant grand dames even as the boutique attracts a younger crowd of influencers and trendsetters.

Lacquered walls hung with portraits of Pope John Paul II and Mobutu Sese Seko at Rubirosa’s

The exterior of Rubirosa’s on Rue de Grenelle in Paris

AD100 designer Brigette Romanek took a more subtle approach to creating desire at Catherine Bloom for Nordstrom, an appointment-only shop in Los Angeles where Bloom, the department store’s Director of Luxury Styling, works with private clients. “I want to make people feel something,” says Romanek. “A store should be a destination you look forward to.” Her use of paneled walls in camel, khaki, and rose suede, plus her signature mix of elements both edgy (the angular leather sofa) and delicate (the plasterwork ceiling) creates what she calls “a protected feeling” that makes the space feel worth seeking out.

Suede-paneled walls and ornate plasterwork at Catherine Bloom for Nordstrom

A punchy red-orange “wallop” moment at Big Night’s West Village outpost

Where Romanek’s space whispers intimacy, Katherine Lewin’s Big Night shouts joy. The New York City shop Big Night, which sells brightly hued kitchen and entertaining goods, trades old-world refinement for modern exuberance. “People tell me they get a shot of serotonin walking into the store,” she says. Her brand’s signature color of punchy red-orange provides what she calls a “wallop”—an immediate sensory experience that registers before customers even process what they’re looking at. Handmade tiles from Smink Studio, screen-printed with bold geometric patterns, are mixed in unexpected color combinations throughout the West Village store.

FoundRae’s Aspen boutique, complete with velvety chairs, books, and intriguing display cases lined with handwritten letters, old photos, and found objects

Once you’ve lured customers in, the next challenge is keeping them there. “If someone is waiting for their partner to try on 50 things, they need to be comfortable,” says Romanek, who installed upholstered chairs from her collection for Crate & Barrel inside individual dressing rooms. Meanwhile, curtains at the store stay closed at all times, enhancing the protected atmosphere that encourages clients to relax and take their time.

Beth Hutchens, founder of fine jewelry line FoundRae, has turned lingering into an art form. The average customer stay at her fine jewelry boutiques—which include locations in New York, Miami, Los Angeles, Dallas, Palm Beach, and Aspen—is an hour and a half. It makes sense when you consider the cozy reading nooks well stocked with books and a rotating menu of complimentary snacks and wine.

The maze-like layout of Big Night’s West Village

In the age of algorithmic shopping recommendations, physical retail can offer something genuinely different: the pleasure of stumbling upon the unexpected. When Lewin found Big Night’s West Village location, for instance, it was a warren of small spaces and hidden corners that defied traditional notions of retail design. Rather than optimizing for efficient shopping (and increasing her renovation costs), she embraced the pre-existing layout and discovered that it primed shoppers’ sense of exploration and surprise. “The store feels like a house party with different things happening in different corners,” Lewin explains.

At Fishs Eddy's Brooklyn location, deliberately cluttered displays add to the sense of discovery

At Fishs Eddy, the beloved New York City housewares shop founded in 1986, the thrill of the hunt has always been part of the appeal. Now, four decades after opening its original Manhattan location, the retailer has expanded with two new outposts, at Chelsea Market and in Brooklyn's Dumbo neighborhood. The latter, an ambitious 3,800-square-foot space, deliberately preserves the cheerfully cluttered, homespun aesthetic that has become the brand's calling card. Rather than give the larger store a slick makeover, founder Julie Gaines embraced the same informal, decidedly human approach to retail design: Vintage-style dishes, deadstock dinnerware, and offbeat glassware are piled high on reclaimed-wood displays and mismatched vintage furniture, evoking the warehouses where the original merchandise was discovered. Gaines describes her merchandising philosophy as “very direct: the way we found it in the warehouse.” But that seemingly improvised look takes effort. “We spend a lot of time looking like we don’t spend time,” she admits. An old-fashioned soda fountain serving egg creams and inexpensive drip coffee adds to the new store's convivial atmosphere.

FoundRae takes discovery to remarkable lengths. Hutchens installed fully realized miniature vignettes behind unmarked cupboard doors—doll-size rooms that include recreations of her own home and FoundRae boutiques, complete with furniture and art. Display cases feature what Hutchens calls a “menagerie of the ephemeral”—handwritten letters, old photos, and evocative found items loosely pinned behind the jewelry. Then there are the “Discovery Drawers”—wood-fronted, glass-topped compartments that hold one-of-a-kind pieces unique to each location (never photographed or featured online).

At Sézane’s new 2,800-square-foot boutique on New York’s Upper East Side, designer Pierre Lacroix has created a similar element of surprise through a dramatic shift in mood. Dubbed L’Appartement on Madison Avenue, the space evokes a Parisian home crossed with an art gallery, with books, artwork by William Klein, and vintage furniture. Most of the boutique feels light and airy, with creamy walls, cerused oak parquet floors, and parchment light fixtures. But tucked off to one side is a small shoe salon, drenched in dark chocolate tones and moodily lit. The intimate, after hours lounge-like space offers an unexpected counterpoint to the rest of the shop.

Sézane’s newest shop, aka L’Appartement on Madison Avenue, designed by Pierre Lacroix

The shoe salon at Sézane’s L’Appartement offers an unexpected change of atmosphere

Even in Rubirosa’s tiny space, Aka has created moments of discovery. He’s filled the walls with oil portraits of Luciano Pavarotti, Pope John Paul II, and various international dictators—a quirky mix of imagery that gives the space a distinctive personality. Visual layers invite clients to continuously notice new details, rewarding repeat visits.

Wes Anderson calling! A particularly Instagrammable corner of Rubirosa’s

Aka admits that Rubirosa’s Instagram-friendly storefront serves as what he calls “a lure”—beautiful enough that people stop to photograph it even if they don’t come inside. “Some people come just to take pictures in front,” he says. The combination of the crimson carpet, dark lacquered millwork, and those quirky framed portraits creates an irresistibly photogenic and TikTok-friendly environment. Romanek’s thoughtful color palette and material mix at the Nordstrom boutique creates a similarly camera-ready backdrop. The social media effect, says Romanek, is simply a natural byproduct of creating a distinctive space. “When a space feels different, conversations about it happen,” she explains. “And that’s what we like. We like people talking about the experience.”

At the Danielle Frankel bridal studio, the sheer spectacle reaches another level entirely. From the soaring ceilings to the grand staircase with its shimmering églomisé panels, the interiors offer no shortage of photo opportunities. But for a boutique devoted to dressing brides for what is, ideally, a once-in-a-lifetime occasion, perhaps a little over-the-top grandeur is exactly the point.

Hoffman worked with the artist Miriam Ellner to create églomisé paneling surrounding the staircase. “The eglomise is one of my favorite things,” says Danielle Frankel designer Frankel Hirsch. The result is staircase that not creates a heightened emotional atmosphere for in-store shoppers but also provides a dramatic backdrop for photography.

Hutchens believes retail design isn’t inextricably tied to scalability. “Ask yourself, if every store was your only store, what would you do and how would you invest in it and change it?” she says. “You evolve faster when you think that way.”

While all seven FoundRae stores share some design DNA—Hutchens embeds her signature mythological symbols in floors, ceilings, and furniture throughout—each reflects its location. The Aspen boutique is swathed in green, alluding to the town’s namesake aspen trees, with a large marquetry sunburst as its centerpiece. The Palm Beach shop features gilded verre églomisé, while in Miami, a vintage Steinway grand piano with hand-applied gold symbols anchors the room. As a result, the stores feel both part of a cohesive brand while rooted in their specific communities.

Big Night’s location in Brooklyn’s Greenpoint

Even Lewin’s three Big Night locations—Brooklyn, West Village, and the Upper East Side, which she calls “sisters, not twins”—each have distinct personalities while maintaining the brand’s joyful, color-saturated identity. Lewin likens the Upper East Side shop to a Classic Six apartment and notes that the new millwork was designed to reflect an upscale, elevated home. Her cash desk placement in the West Village location, situated in the center of the largest room rather than tucked away, came from understanding that specific space and how her team could best interact with customers there.

The thread connecting these diverse approaches is a fundamental understanding that physical retail can no longer compete with e-commerce on convenience or selection. But the best retail spaces offer atmosphere, discovery, community, and the experience of being somewhere that feels genuinely special. As Aka puts it, “Like a good movie, when it ends...if you don’t have the feeling you want to see it again, it was not that good.”

The back room of Rubirosa’s is painted in a pale baby blue, “like a boy’s little bedroom,” says Aka. “So when you take out your credit card, it doesn’t bother you. It’s like you’re on a pillow.”

A leather, steel, and palmwood sofa by Pacific Green at Catherine Bloom for Nordstrom, designed by AD100 talent Brigette Romanek
